Recovering semipermeable barriers from reflected Brownian motion
Alexander Van Werde, Jaron Sanders

Abstract
. We study the recovery of one-dimensional semipermeable barriers for a stochastic process in a planar domain. The considered process acts like Brownian motion when away from the barriers and is reflected upon contact until a sufficient but random amount of interaction has occurred, determined by the permeability, after which it passes through. Given a sequence of samples, we wonder when one can determine the location and shape of the barriers. This paper identifies several different recovery regimes, determined by the available observation period and the time between samples, with qualitatively different behavior. The observation period T T dictates if the full barriers or only certain pieces can be recovered, and the sampling rate significantly influences the convergence rate as T → ∞ T\to\infty . This rate turns out polynomial for fixed-frequency data, but exponentially fast in a high-frequency regime. Further, the environment’s impact on the difficulty of the problem is quantified using interpretable parameters in the recovery guarantees, and is found to also be regime-dependent. For instance, the curvature of the barriers affects the convergence rate for fixed-frequency data, but becomes irrelevant when T → ∞ T\to\infty with high-frequency data. The results are accompanied by explicit algorithms, and we conclude by illustrating the application to real-life data.



1.
Introduction

Obstructions in the environment can shape the movements of stochastic processes, and uncovering this can lead to important scientific insights.
To name a concrete example, barriers to animal mobility, such as roads or natural features, are crucial knowledge for ecological research
[
8
,
51
]
.
For another, the interaction of molecules with obstacles, revealed by single-particle tracking, has led to numerous advancements in subcellular biology
[
24
,
45
,
2
]
such as the significant discovery that cell membranes are not homogeneous, but rather compartmentalized by barriers which hinder lateral diffusions
[
27
,
50
]
.

As a model for scenarios such as these, we study
reflected Brownian motion with semipermeable barriers
in a setting where the process takes values in a planar domain and where the barriers are smooth, closed curves.
This process acts like Brownian motion when away from the barriers and reflects on contact until a random time allows it to pass through, after which it reflects on the other side until the next random time.
The barriers hence hinder its movements by temporarily constraining it to a single side; see
Figure

1
and
Definition

1
.

Figure 1
.

A simulated sample path of a reflected Brownian motion with semipermeable barriers, and the same data observed at a finite sampling rate. Our goal is to recover the underlying barriers given a finite number of samples.

This process was introduced to the mathematical literature relatively recently in 2016 by Lejay
[
30
]
and has since been used to describe various interface phenomena
[
32
,
22
,
21
,
56
,
53
,
10
,
52
,
11
,
40
]
.
It is also known as snapping out Brownian motion and should not be confused with skew Brownian motion
[
23
,
29
]
.
The difference is that skew Brownian motion has permeable as opposed to semipermeable barriers, meaning that the barriers may be instantly traversed for the latter process, but with a possible directional bias.

The current paper studies the statistical problem which aims to recover the location of the barriers based on a sequence of samples
\{X_{it}:i=0,1,\ldots,\lfloor T/t\rfloor\}
.
Our goal is to construct estimators and to fundamentally understand what affects the problem’s difficulty.
This problem lies at the intersection of two key disciplines of modern statistics: set estimation which studies the statistical recovery of regions and their boundaries based on related observations
[
12
,
19
]
, and statistical inference for stochastic processes
[
9
,
28
]
.

The closest related work is that of Cholaquidis, Fraiman, Lugosi, and Pateiro–López
[
17
]
who estimated a domain
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and its boundary
\partial D
based on a trajectory of a (classical) reflected Brownian motion; see also
[
18
,
16
]
for recent extensions with drift or restricted sampling.
However,
[
17
]
mostly focuses on recovering the domain
D
itself, viewing the boundary as a corollary, and the methods are not suitable for estimating semipermeable barriers.
To our knowledge, ours is the first mathematical work focused on recovering barrier locations given samples from a stochastic process.

Also related to barriers is the estimation of the bias parameter for skew Brownian motion
[
7
,
34
,
33
,
31
,
6
,
5
]
and of discontinuous diffusion or drift coefficients
[
35
,
36
,
54
,
26
]
.
We however desire the locations, not the permeabilities which would be more analogous.
Estimating permeabilities presumably requires the locations, so our results give a starting point for future work in that direction.

1.1.
Summary of main results

We identify fundamental regimes with different notions of recovery, construct explicit estimators for the barrier locations in
Algorithms

1
,
2
and
3
, and quantify what factors affect the problem’s difficulty in terms of interpretable parameters.

One class of such factors arises from the environment.
For example, the barriers’ geometry matters as recovery grows challenging if these may squiggle arbitrarily wildly.
Indeed, dependence on geometric assumptions is a topic of significant interest in set estimation, and it is generally necessary to rule out pathological examples; see
[
19
, §3.2]
.
We do this by assuming smoothness and quantify the geometry using the curvature.
To completely capture all environmental features so that guarantees depending only on the parameters can be established naturally also requires various other quantities including the barriers’ permeabilies to name one; see
Section

2.2
.

Another important class of factors arises from the nature of the available data, determined by the intersample time
t
and the length of the observation period
T
.
Indeed, these quantities are typically constrained in applications.
The observation period could be limited by budgetary considerations or if the problem informs a decision in the near future, and the intersample time by technical limitations of the measurement procedure.

The parameters
T
and
t
have a pronounced effect in our results, giving fundamental regimes where qualitatively different guarantees become possible.
The regimes may be summarized as follows; see also
Figure

2
and
Table

1
.

Partial recovery

Complete recovery

Figure 2
.
Visualization for our recovery notions.
Partial recovery aims to recover the parts of the barrier which were hit by the continuous-time process based on discrete-time samples.
Complete recovery is more demanding and asks to recover the barriers completely.

Complete recovery from fixed-frequency data:

To start, we consider a regime where the observation period
T
is large and where the intersample time
t
is fixed and not too large.

Theorem

2.3
then shows that the barriers can be recovered completely with uniform approximation error.

When the approximation error is measured with respect to the Hausdorff distance, our convergence rate as
T\to\infty
has order
T^{-2/3}
up to a logarithmic factor.
The exponent
2/3
is here expected to be optimal as this is the minimax rate for the related problem of boundary estimation given i.i.d. samples from a planar domain
[
1
]
.

The dominant factors affecting the constants occurring in the rate are the curvature of the barriers and the mixing properties of the process.
The permeability of the barriers does not appear in the rate.
Rather, it determines, together with other factors, what constraint on the intersample time
t
is required for our performance guarantee.

Partial recovery from high-frequency data:

If
T
is fixed, not necessarily large, then the process may not have explored the full domain so complete recovery is impossible.
However, there are practical situations where the observation time is limited.
Even if complete recovery is too much to ask, one may wonder if some partial information can be recovered.

Indeed,
Theorem

2.5
shows that those pieces of the barriers which were hit by the continuous process
(X_{s})_{s\leq T}
can be recovered based on the observed discrete samples, and the convergence rate for typical points has order
\sqrt{t}
up to a logarithmic factor as
t\to 0
.
This is expected to be optimal as
\sqrt{t}
is the order of the spacing between samples.

The word “typical” is not redundant: the analysis suggests that there can be points whose recovery is exceptionally difficult because the process spent little time near them.

Corollary

2.6
implies that these are still recovered, but with a worse approximation error.

Complete recovery from high-frequency data:

One may finally wonder about the setting when
T
is large
and

t
is small.
This is studied in
Theorem

2.7
for the special case where there is only the outer impermeable barrier, and a striking phenomenon is found.

While the rate in the fixed-frequency regime was polynomial as
T\to\infty
, an exponentially fast rate of order
\exp(-c\sqrt{T})
can be achieved if one has high-frequency data.
Moreover, this rate only depends on the geometry of the domain through its size, the curvature of the barrier being asymptotically irrelevant.

For practitioners, these regimes can help to calibrate the expectations for what is possible with available data.
Whether the full barriers or only certain pieces can be recovered depends on the observation period, and the approximation error may depend significantly on the time between samples.
The effect of the environment is also heavily regime-dependent.

Result

t

T

Recovery type

Rate for approximation error

Theorem

2.3

fixed

large

Complete

T^{-2/3}\ln(T)^{2/3}
uniformly as
T\to\infty

Theorem

2.5

small

fixed

Partial

\sqrt{t}\ln(T/t)
for typical points as
t\to 0

Theorem

2.7

small

large

Complete

\exp(-c\sqrt{T})
uniformly as
T\to\infty

Table 1
.
Summary of our main results; see
Section

2.3
for rigorous statements which also make the dependence on environmental factors explicit.

1.2.
Proof techniques

The vast majority of previous work on Brownian motion with semipermeable barriers is only in one-dimensional settings
[
29
,
22
,
21
,
56
,
53
,
40
,
11
]
with a few notable exceptions in work of Lejay
[
32
]
and Schumm and Bressloff
[
10
,
52
]
.
A key challenge is hence that our setting is a planar domain.
Specifically, the main difficulty is that the barriers can be arbitrary smooth closed curves.
If we could pretend as if they were straight lines, then everything would be analytically tractable.

We considered multiple approaches for this and put effort into simplifying these for transparent proofs.
For instance, one initial idea was to use a local diffeomorphism to rectify the barriers and then apply Itô calculus, similar to
[
35
, §2.2]
.
However, while this may work, quantifying environmental dependence grows cumbersome because Itô calculus gives non-constant drift and diffusion terms making the dynamics only “nearly” one-dimensional.
By depending on the arbitrary local diffeomorphism, this introduces irrelevant information to the computation.

Alternatively, one could rectify using a biholomorphism to a half-space via the Riemann mapping theorem and apply conformal invariance.
This is more elegant as it then suffices to consider only a time change.
However, the dependence of the biholomorphism on the rectified barrier is not necessarily transparent, so controlling the time change is still delicate.

The method which we present here is based on coupling techniques instead.
We first show in
Lemma

3.5
that the barriers can be locally approximated by a straight line, and subsequently approximate
X_{t}
by a (classical) reflected Brownian motion reflecting on this line in
Section

3.2.2
.
Crucially, the error in the coupling of these processes and the timescale on which it is valid can be established transparently.
This allows reducing the proofs of
Theorems

2.3
and
2.5
to computations for one-dimensional reflected Brownian motion.
The latter computations are not trivial, but they can be done in a clear manner.

The proof of
Theorem

2.7
is fairly short once one has the correct ideas and does not require the coupling.
Rather, it combines a general-purpose result of Matthews
[
41
]
on covering times with a result by Chen and Friedman
[
15
]
on narrow escape problems.
This actually implicitly relies on the biholomorphism approach mentioned above, as this is used in
[
15
]
.
That this makes the dependence on the curvature of the barrier less explicit does not matter here because it turns out to be asymptotically irrelevant anyways.

1.3.
Application to animal movement data

We give a case study with animal movement data in
Section

7
.
The considered dataset consists of reindeer movements and was made publicly available by Loe et al.
[
39
]
.
The recovery algorithm
1
1

1

Source code is made available at
https://github.com/Alexander-Van-Werde/Brownian-barriers.git
.
here successfully reveals both impermeable and semipermeable natural barriers which are validated using a satellite image.
Impermeable barriers arise at the coastline and at steep slopes bounding the valley wherein the animals live, while rivers give semipermeable barriers which are occasionally crossed.

This is simply an illustrative example, but it may be noted that having a natural model with a notion of barrier permeability could indeed be useful in ecological research.
This could, for instance, allow rigorously studying how much different types of roads impede movements.
Future work about the estimation of the permeability parameter would hence be valuable.
We refer to
[
49
,
8
,
48
,
43
]
and the references therein for methods in current use whose properties have mainly been investigated using simulations so far.

Let us further note that animal movement data was a key motivation for the work of Cholaquidis, Fraiman, Lugosi, and Pateiro–López
[
17
]
which we mentioned earlier.
Specifically, they were concerned with estimating the
home range
, which is the area where the animal does all its normal daily activities.
Previous analyses had assumed i.i.d. data, but that assumption grew unrealistic as technological advances made high-frequency tracking possible, which is what motivated the move to process-based models; see
[
17
, §1.2]
.

Our theoretical results also shed new light on that problem as the special case of our setting where one only has the outer impermeable barrier.
In particular,
Theorems

2.7
and
2.5
are highly relevant as they suggest that the problem may qualitatively change as tracking frequency improves, at least if the Brownian model is a sufficient approximation for reality.
These are novel findings as the rates proved in
[
17
, §4]
were all polynomial with the same exponent as for i.i.d. data, so without the exponential acceleration, nor did they consider that partial recovery may be possible when complete recovery is impossible.

1.4.
Structure of this paper

The results are stated rigorously in
Section

2
.
General preliminaries for the arguments are presented in

Section

3
, and the proofs are given in
Sections

4
,
5
and
6
.
We conclude in
Section

7
with the case study using animal movement data.

Supplementary details are given in the appendices.
If desired, the algorithms and case study can also be read independently of the rigorous results which are our main focus; see
Sections

4.1
and
5.1
as well as
Section

7
.

2.
Results

2.1.
Notation and definitions

Fix an open planar set
D_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose closure
D
is connected, bounded, and has a smooth boundary
\partial D
.
Additionally, consider
m\geq 0
smooth curves
B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}\subseteq D_{0}
and denote
B_{0}\vcentcolon=\partial D
.
In what follows, the
B_{i}
with
i\geq 1
will be semipermeable barriers and
B_{0}
will be an impermeable barrier, keeping the process in
D
.

To simplify the presentation and proofs, let us adopt some additional assumptions.
First, assume that
B_{i}\cap B_{j}=\emptyset
for every
i\neq j
so that there are no intersection points which would require separate consideration.
Second, assume that the
D
is simply connected so that
B_{0}
is a single connected curve.
Finally, assume that the
B_{i}
are simple closed curves so that there are no self-intersections or endpoints.
Then, in particular, the Jordan curve theorem yields that
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{i}
has precisely two connected components of which only one is bounded.

For every
i\leq{m}
let
\vec{n}_{i}:B_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the unique vector field which is orthogonal to
B_{i}
, points towards the bounded component, and has unit length.
We say that
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
on the positive side of
B_{i}
if
x
lies in the closure of the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{i}
.
Similarly, the
negative side of
B_{i}
refers to the closure of the unbounded component.
Note that
x
is then both on the positive and negative sides when
x\in B_{i}
.

The process will be driven by randomness from some auxiliary processes.
To drive the movements when away from all barriers, we consider a
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-valued Wiener process
W_{t}
.
Further, fix scalars
\lambda_{i}^{+},\lambda_{i}^{-}>0
for every
i\geq 1
, specifying the permeability of the two sides of each barrier.
Then, to regulate the random event where the process switches sides, we consider continuous-time càdlàg Markov chains
s_{i}(t)
taking values in
\{+1,-1\}
with transition rate
\lambda_{i}^{+}
(resp.
\lambda_{i}^{-}
) from
-1
to
+1
(resp. from
+1
to
-1
).
Finally, let
s_{0}(t)\vcentcolon=+1
for all
t\geq 0
.

Processes satisfying the following requirements exist and are unique given an initial condition; see
Proposition

3.1
.

Definition 1
.

Let
X_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
be continuous stochastic processes which take values in
D
and
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, respectively.
Then,
X_{t}
is called
a reflected Brownian motion with semipermeable barriers
B_{i}
and local times
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
if the following properties hold with probability one:

(i)

The following stochastic differential equation is satisfied:

\displaystyle{\rm d}X_{t}={\rm d}W_{t}+\sum_{i=0}^{m}s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})\vec{n}_{i}(X_{t})\mathbbm{1}\{X_{t}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}.

(ii)

For every
i\leq{m}
, the process
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
is nondecreasing, satisfies
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{0}=0
, and increases at time
t
if and only if
X_{t}\in B_{i}
.
That is,

\displaystyle L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{X_{r}\in B_{i}\}\,{\rm d}L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{r}.

(iii)

For every
i\leq{m}
, if
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})=+1
then
X_{t}
is on the positive side of
B_{i}
.
Similarly, if
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})=-1
then
X_{t}
is on the negative side of
B_{i}
.

Remark 2.1
.

Readers who are unfamiliar with the theory of local times may benefit from a more definite description.
The local time at the
i
th barrier admits the following expression:

(2.1)

\displaystyle L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\bigl\{\exists y\in B_{i}:\|X_{r}-y\|<\varepsilon\bigr\}\,{\rm d}r,

almost surely; see
Section

A.3
for a proof.
One can hence interpret
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
as measuring how much
X_{r}
interacted with barrier
B_{i}
by time
t
.

The interpretation for the terms
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})
in
Definition

1
is correspondingly that these change sign randomly as the process interacts with a barrier, depending on its permeability and the amount of interaction.
Upon such a sign change,
items

(i)
and
(iii)
express that
X_{t}
passes to the other side of the barrier and that the direction of reflection flips to match this.

Remark 2.2
.

Some clarifications are required to avoid ambiguity.
First, let it be understood that there is no dependence between the considered auxiliary processes except for that which is necessary for
Definition

1
to be well-defined.
That is, we assume that the
s_{i}
are conditionally independent given
(s_{i}(0))_{i=1}^{m}
, and unconditionally independent of
\{W_{t}:t\geq 0\}
.

Second, let us note that the law of the process
X_{t}
not only depends on the initial condition
X_{0}
, but also on
s_{i}(0)
if
X_{0}\in B_{i}
.
For brevity, however, we will typically suppress this from the notation.
So, for instance, one should understand that “for every initial condition
x_{0}\in D
” really means “for every initial condition
x_{0}\in D
and every
s_{i}(0)\in\{+1,-1\}
with
x_{0}
on the positive (resp. negative) side of
B_{i}
if
s_{i}(0)=+1
(resp.
s_{i}(0)=-1
)”.

Finally, the term “smooth” here always means
C^{\infty}
-smooth.
It would of course also be interesting to extend our results to settings with very little regularity, but we do not wish to focus on such issues in the current work.

2.2.
Parameters quantifying the difficulty of the environment

The permeability of the barriers can be quantified by the following parameter:

(2.2)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\max\bigl\{\lambda_{i}^{*}:i\in\{1,\ldots,{m}\},\,*\in\{+,-\}\bigr\}.

If
\lambda_{\max}
is large, then some barriers have little influence and the process is allowed to cross them quickly.
Such barriers are naturally more difficult to detect.

The following two parameters are of a geometric nature, related to the difficulty of the configuration of barriers.
For every
i\leq{m}
let
k_{i}:B_{i}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be the
unsigned curvature
of
B_{i}
.
Denote
\kappa
for the greatest achieved value:

(2.3)

\displaystyle\kappa\vcentcolon=\max\bigl\{k_{i}(x):i\in\{0,1,\ldots,{m}\},\,x\in B_{i}\bigr\}.

A bound on this parameter allows one to avoid scenarios where the curves squiggle wildly.
It is further useful to have a parameter which ensures some minimal spacing between the barriers.
For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
let
\mathscr{B}(x,r)
denote the open ball of radius
r>0
and set

(2.4)

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sup\bigl\{r\geq 0:\mathscr{B}(x,r^{\prime})\cap(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\text{ is connected }\forall r^{\prime}\leq r,\,\forall x\in D\bigr\}.

This parameter serves to avoid pathological scenarios where distinct barriers lie extremely close to each other, or scenarios where a single barrier doubles over on itself with a U-bend.

We finally consider two parameters whose necessity arises from the fact that it is impossible to recover a barrier with which the process had no interactions.
Let
\pi
be the stationary distribution of
X_{t}
.
That is, the probability measure given by

\pi(E)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in E)
for every measurable
E\subseteq D
.
Then, with
\operatorname{Area}(\cdot)
the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the following quantifies if there are infrequently visited regions:

(2.5)

\displaystyle\pi_{\min}\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\bigl\{\pi(E)/\operatorname{Area}(E):\text{measurable }E\subseteq D\text{ with }\operatorname{Area}(E)>0\bigr\}.

Finally, even if the process visits a region frequently in the limit
t\to\infty
, it could take a long time between visits to the region.
To quantify this, consider the
mixing time
,

(2.6)

\displaystyle t_{\rm{mix}}\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\bigl\{t\geq 0:\lvert\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in E\mid X_{0}=x_{0})-\pi(E)\rvert\leq 1/4,\ \forall x_{0}\in D,\,E\subseteq D\bigr\}.

This parameter could be large, for instance, if one of the barriers has low permeability and hence traps the process on the side of
X_{0}
for a long time.

2.3.
Results

2.3.1.
Complete recovery from fixed-frequency data

To start, we consider the regime where the observation period
T
is large and the time between samples
t
is fixed.
It will also be assumed that
t
is not
too
large.
The latter assumption is required for our proofs because the permeability of the barriers will dilute their effect over time.

The goal is
complete recovery
, meaning that we desire an estimator
\hat{B}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that every point of
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
is close to some point in
\hat{B}
and conversely.
In other words, we quantify the approximation error in terms of the
Hausdorff distance
:

(2.7)

\displaystyle d_{\rm{H}}(A,B)\vcentcolon=\max\Bigl\{\sup_{a\in A}\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{b\in B}\|a-b\|,\ \sup_{b\in B}\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{a\in A}\|a-b\|\Bigr\}

where
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
are compact sets and
\|\cdot\|
is the Euclidean norm.

The following result establishes that it is possible to achieve complete recovery given a sufficiently long sample path.
Further—and this is the main content really—it is made explicit how the performance depends on the interpretable parameters from
Section

2.2
.

Theorem 2.3
.

There exist absolute constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
such that the following holds for every
\eta\in(0,1)
, every
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
,
\varepsilon\leq c_{2}\kappa t
, and
T>0
.

Suppose that one is given samples
\{X_{jt}:j=0,1,\ldots,\lfloor T/t\rfloor\}
from a sample path starting in stationarity, i.e., with
X_{0}\sim\pi
.
Assume that the observation time satisfies

(2.8)

\displaystyle T\geq c_{3}\frac{t_{\rm{mix}}}{\pi_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr).

Then, there exists an explicit algorithm whose output
\hat{B}
satisfies the following guarantee:

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(d_{\rm{H}}(\hat{B},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\varepsilon\bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

The proof is given in
Section

4
as is the algorithm.
Rewriting (
2.8
), the error
\varepsilon
decays at a rate of order
T^{-2/3}\ln(T)^{2/3}
as
T\to\infty
.
The exponent
2/3
on
T^{-2/3}
is optimal for recovering the boundary of a
C^{2}
-smooth set given i.i.d. samples
[
1
]
, and we expect that it will also be optimal here as only bounds on the curvature are assumed.
It could be interesting future research to rigorously prove such an optimality result.
Another interesting question is whether algorithms which also assume bounds on the barriers’ higher-order derivatives may achieve better rates of convergence.

Remark 2.4
.

It often occurs in practice that one is given a collection of sequences, say
\{X_{jt}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}:j\leq\lfloor T_{i}/t\rfloor\}
with
i=1,\ldots,r
.
Assuming that the paths and their initial conditions are independent, the same algorithms and guarantees then remain applicable with
T
replaced by the
effective observation period

\sum_{i=1}^{r}T_{i}
.

2.3.2.
Partial recovery from high-frequency data

If
T
is fixed, potentially smaller than the mixing time of the process, then it can not be guaranteed that the process interacted with all parts of the barrier.
Consequently, complete recovery may be impossible.

We here show that the next best thing is possible: one can recover those pieces which were hit by the continuous-time process
(X_{s})_{s\leq T}
using only the discrete samples.
More precisely, let

(2.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{i}\vcentcolon=B_{i}\cap\{X_{t}:t\in[0,T]\}.

The following result then shows that “typical” points in
\cup_{i=0}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{i}
can be recovered up to an approximation error which is small for high-frequency data:

Theorem 2.5
.

For every
\eta\in(0,1)
there exist
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
depending only on
\eta
such that for every
T>0
and initial condition
x_{0}\in D
the following holds.

Suppose that one is given samples
\{X_{jt}:j=0,1,\ldots,\lfloor T/t\rfloor\}
with
t>0
sufficiently small to satisfy
t\leq c_{1}T
and

\ln(T/t)^{4}t\leq c_{2}\min\{T,1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}.

Then, there exists an explicit algorithm whose output
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
satisfies the following guarantees:

(1) Nonoccurrence of false positives:

Every point in
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
is close to a barrier with high probability.
More precisely,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\|p-y\|:y\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}\}\leq c_{3}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t}\,\text{ for all }\,p\in\hat{\mathcal{X}}\bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

(2) Typical points are recovered:

Let
\tau
be a stopping time for the filtration generated by
X_{t}
,
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
, and
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})
.
Then, if
\tau
is such that
X_{\tau}\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
almost surely,

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\|X_{\tau}-p\|:p\in\hat{\mathcal{X}}\}\leq c_{3}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t}\text{ or }\tau>T\bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

Let us emphasize that the algorithm does not require prior knowledge of the stopping time
\tau
.
As a concrete example, one can take
\tau\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}\}
.
Then, the result shows that the first hit of a barrier is recovered with high probability.
Similarly, taking
\tau\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t\geq 0:s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})\neq s_{i}(0)\text{ for some }i\leq{m}\}
shows that the first location where
X_{t}
switches sides for some barrier is recovered with high probability.

The combination of (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) can be interpreted as stating that the approximation error has order
\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t}
for most points, but there can be exceptional points in
\cup_{i=0}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{i}
which are particularly difficult to recover.
Those points are still fairly close to
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
but may suffer from a worse approximation error:

Corollary 2.6
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending on
\varepsilon,\eta
,
\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}
, and
T
such that for
t\leq c_{1}
and
\ln(T/t)^{4}t\leq c_{2}
the estimator of
Theorem

2.5
satisfies

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\|x-p\|:p\in\hat{\mathcal{X}}\}\leq\varepsilon\,\text{ for all }\,x\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{i}\bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

The proofs are given in
Section

5
and come with an explicit algorithm.
The nature of the difficult points is there also made explicit: they occur when the process only spends a small amount of time in the neighborhood.
It would be interesting future research to study these points in more detail as this would be an essential ingredient to determine an optimal rate of convergence for
d_{\rm{H}}(\hat{\mathcal{X}},\cup_{i=0}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{i})
.

2.3.3.
Complete recovery from high-frequency data

Finally, we consider a regime where
T
is large and
t
is small.
Specifically, we study the behavior when one first takes the limit
t\to 0
and subsequently lets
T
grow.
For proof-technical reasons, we focus on the case
m=0
where there is only the outer impermeable barrier which bounds the domain.

By (
2.11
) and (
2.13
), one can recover
\mathcal{X}_{0}
with an arbitrarily small approximation error and without false positives if the sampling rate is sufficiently high.
Hence, it remains to determine the rate of convergence of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
to
B_{0}
when
T
tends to infinity:

Theorem 2.7
.

Suppose that
m=0
and fix some initial condition
x_{0}\in D
.
Then, for every
\eta\in(0,1)
there exists a positive constant
c>0
depending only on
\eta
such that

(2.14)

\displaystyle\liminf_{T\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(d_{\rm{H}}(\mathcal{X}_{0},B_{0})\leq\exp(-\sqrt{cT/\operatorname{Area}(D)})\Bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

We observe a pronounced qualitative difference between the fixed-frequency regime and the high frequency regime: the polynomial rate of convergence in
Theorem

2.3
is replaced by an exponential rate in
Theorem

2.7
.
Further, convergence rate in the high-frequency regime only depends on the size of the domain whereas in the fixed-frequency regime it also depends on the curvature of the barrier among other things.

While not covered by the current analysis, we expect that the qualitative conclusion of
Theorem

2.7
of an exponential rate remains valid in the general case
m>0
.
Such a generalization would be relevant future research as the quantitative formulation would clarify how exactly the environment enters the problem.
For instance, one possible guess is that
1/\operatorname{Area}(D)
may have to be replaced by a quantity involving the stationary distribution.

3.
General preliminaries

Section

3.1
shows that there indeed exists a unique process satisfying
Definition

1
.

Section

3.2
provides the coupling technique mentioned in
Section

1.2
.

3.1.
Well-definedness

Consider a
D
-valued random variable
X_{0}
which is on the positive (resp. negative) side of
B_{i}
if
s_{i}(0)=+1
(resp.
s_{i}(0)=-1
).
Assume that
(X_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})
is independent of
W
and that
X_{0}
is conditionally independent of
(s_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
given
(s_{i}(0))_{i=1}^{m}
.

It will be convenient to ensure that null sets are measurable.
Hence, let it be understood that the
\sigma
-algebra generated by a family of random variables
\{Y_{i}:i\in\mathcal{I}\}
refers to the
\mathbb{P}
-completion
2
2

2

Formally, if
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
is the implicit probability space where upon the processes
\{W_{t}:t\geq 0\}
and
\{s_{i}(t):t\geq 0\}
are defined, then the
\mathbb{P}
-completion
of a
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{G}\subseteq\mathscr{F}
is here defined to be the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing all sets in
\mathscr{G}
and all
\mathscr{F}
-null sets.
of the least
\sigma
-algebra with respect to which all
Y_{i}
are measurable.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{t}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the random variables
X_{0}
,
W_{r}
with
0\leq r\leq t
, and
s_{i}(r)
with
0\leq r<\infty
and
0\leq i\leq{m}
.
Then, there exist
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-adapted processes
X_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
satisfying
Definition

1
with initial condition
X_{0}
.

Moreover, pathwise uniqueness holds: if
(\tilde{X}_{t},\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t},\ldots,\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}${m}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})
is another
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-adapted processes with
\tilde{X}_{0}=X_{0}
which satisfies
Definition

1
with respect to the same
W_{t}
and
s_{i}
, then
\tilde{X}_{t}=X_{t}
and
\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}=L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
for every
t\geq 0
, almost surely.

Let
X_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
be the unique
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-adapted processes satisfying
Definition

1
with initial condition
X_{0}
.
The stochastic differential equation in
Definition

1
then suggests that
X_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
only depend on
s_{j}
through
s_{j}(r)
for
r\leq L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$j$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
, and that
X_{t}
is Markovian.
Indeed:

Proposition 3.2
.

For any
t\geq 0
and
\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{m}\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{F}_{t,\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{m}}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the random variables
X_{0}
,
W_{r}
with
0\leq r\leq t
, and
s_{i}(z_{i})
with
0\leq z_{i}\leq\ell_{i}
.
Then, the event
\{L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}\leq\ell_{i},\,\forall i\leq{m}\}
is in
\mathcal{F}_{t,\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{m}}
.

Further, let
\mathcal{F}_{t}
be the
\sigma
-algebra of events
E
with
E\cap\{L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}\leq\ell_{i},\,\forall i\leq{m}\}\in\mathcal{F}_{t,\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{m}}
for every
\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{m}\geq 0
.
Then, the processes
X_{t}
,
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
, and
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})
are
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted.

Proposition 3.3
.

The process
X_{t}
satisfies the weak Markov property.
That is, for any measurable
E\subseteq D
and fixed
0<u\leq t
, almost surely

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in E\mid\mathcal{F}_{u})=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in E\mid X_{u}).

Moreover, the process
\mathscr{X}_{t}\vcentcolon=(X_{t},(s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})_{i=0}^{m}))
satisfies the strong Markov property.
That is, for every
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-stopping time
\tau
, every fixed
u\geq 0
, and every measurable
E\subseteq D\times\{-1,+1\}^{{m}+1}
, almost surely

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{X}_{\tau+u}\in E\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau})=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{X}_{\tau+u}\in E\mid\mathscr{X}_{\tau})

where
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
is the
\sigma
-algebra consisting of events
\mathcal{E}
with
\mathcal{E}\cap\{\tau\leq t\}\in\mathcal{F}_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
.

Remark 3.4
.

The strong Markov property is not satisfied by
X_{t}
itself when
{m}\geq 1
.
Indeed, consider the stopping time
\tau\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in B_{i}\}
for some
i\geq 1
.
Then, one can not infer from
X_{\tau}
on what side of the barrier
X_{\tau+\varepsilon}
should live.

The proofs of
Propositions

3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
are deferred to
Appendix

A
.

3.2.
Local approximation technique for the barriers and process

We show in
Section

3.2.1
that the barriers can locally be approximated by straight lines.
Subsequently, we approximate the processes
X_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
in
Sections

3.2.2
and
3.2.3
, respectively.

3.2.1.
Local approximation of the barriers

Consider a parameter
\delta>0
, and introduce

(3.3)

\displaystyle r_{\delta}\vcentcolon=\delta\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}.

Recall from (
2.3
) that
\kappa
is an upper bound on the curvature of the barriers.
The relevance of
\delta\kappa^{-1}
is hence that this is the scale at which the barriers are well-approximated by straight lines.
The following elementary result, visualized in
Figure

3
, makes this precise.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose that
\delta<1/2
.
Then, for every
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, there is at most one barrier
B_{i}
which intersects
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
.
Moreover, if such a
B_{i}
exists, then there exists a unit vector
\hat{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
depending on
x_{0}
and
B_{i}
such that the following properties hold:

(1)

There exists some
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\lvert\langle y,\hat{n}\rangle-\mathfrak{c}\rvert<4\delta r_{\delta}
for every
y\in B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
.

(2)

One has
\|\vec{n}_{i}(y)-\hat{n}\|<2\delta
for every
y\in B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
.

(3)

Every point
z\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
on the positive side of
B_{i}
satisfies
\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle>\mathfrak{c}-4\delta r_{\delta}
.
Similarly, every
z\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
the negative side of
B_{i}
satisfies
\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle<\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}
.

Figure 3
.
Visualization of
Lemma

3.5
.
Locally in
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
, the barrier is contained in a strip of points
y
with
\langle y,\hat{n}\rangle\approx\mathfrak{c}
, and the normal vectors
\vec{n}_{i}
are well-approximated by
\hat{n}
.

This lemma follows from the Frenet–Serret formulas
[
20
, p.70–74]
which control the rate of change of the tangent and normal to
B_{i}
in terms of curvature; see
Appendix

B
.

Remark 3.6
.

Lemma

3.5
would remain true if
r_{\delta}
was replaced by
\min\{\delta/\kappa,\rho\}
.
However,
\lambda_{\max}
will be relevant for some of the forthcoming argument following
Corollary

3.11

The current formulation serves to minimize notational burden, as
1/\lambda_{\max}
will become a relevant scale of distance from
Corollary

3.11
onwards.

3.2.2.
Coupling with a process reflecting on a straight line

Let
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\hat{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
be as in
Lemma

3.5
and define a straight line by

(3.4)

\displaystyle A^{+}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\langle y,s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle=s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}\},

Then, we will compare
X_{t}
with a process
Y^{+}_{t}
which reflects on
A^{+}
instead of
B_{i}
.

We have to specify an initial condition.
In the ideal case, we take
Y_{0}^{+}=x_{0}
.
However, this is not always possible because we need the initial condition to lie on the side of
A^{+}
towards which
s_{i}(0)\hat{n}
points.
This motivates the following definition:

(3.5)

\displaystyle y_{0}^{+}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}x_{0}+(s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}-\langle x_{0},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle)s_{i}(0)\hat{n}&\text{ if }\langle x_{0},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\leq s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta},\\
x_{0}&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

In other words, when
x_{0}
lies on the side of
A^{+}
towards which
s_{i}(0)\hat{n}
points we take
y_{0}^{+}=x_{0}
, and otherwise we project onto
A^{+}
.

Let
Y_{t}^{+}
be a reflected Brownian motion with reflection on
A^{+}
, local time
K_{t}^{+}
, and initial condition
y_{0}^{+}
.
That is,
Y^{+}_{0}=y_{0}^{+}
and the following stochastic differential equation holds:

(3.6)

\displaystyle{\rm d}Y_{t}^{+}={\rm d}W_{t}+s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\,{\rm d}K_{t}^{+}

subject to the usual characterizing conditions
3
3

3

That is,
Y_{t}^{+}
is continuous with values in
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\langle y,s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\geq s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}\}
, and
K_{t}^{+}
is a continuous and nondecreasing process with
K_{0}^{+}=0
which only increases when
Y_{t}^{+}\in A^{+}
.
.
Crucially, it should here be understood that the
W_{t}
in (
3.6
) is the same Wiener process as drives
X_{t}
in
Definition

1
; see
Figure

4
.

The following results show that
Y_{t}^{+}
provides a good approximation to
X_{t}
when
t
is sufficiently small.
More precisely, consider the stopping time defined by

(3.7)

\displaystyle\tau\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\bigl\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\not\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\ \text{ or }\ s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})\neq s_{i}(0)\text{ for some }i\leq{m}\bigr\}.

Then, a good approximation will hold when
t\leq\tau
:

Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose that
\delta<1/2
and consider some
x_{0}\in D
and
i\leq{m}
with
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
Then, for every
t\geq 0
,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\langle Y_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle-8\delta r_{\delta}\leq\langle X_{\min\{t,\tau\}},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\leq\langle Y_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle.

Figure 4
.
Visualization of the process
Y_{t}^{+}
from
Section

3.2.2
.
The figures on the left and right correspond to the first and second case of (
3.5
), respectively.

Proof.

Recall that
X_{t}
and
Y_{t}^{+}
are driven by the same Wiener process and recall the stochastic differential equations from
Definition

1
and (
3.6
).
Then, using that
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$j$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}=0
for
t\leq\tau
and
j\neq i
since
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
does not intersect
B_{j}
, it holds for every
t\geq 0
that

(3.9)

\displaystyle X_{\min\{t,\tau\}}{}

\displaystyle{}-Y_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+}

\displaystyle=x_{0}-y_{0}^{+}+{\textstyle\int_{0}^{\min\{t,\tau\}}}s_{i}(0)\vec{n}_{i}(X_{r})\mathbbm{1}\{X_{r}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{r}-s_{i}(0)\hat{n}K_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+}.

In particular, the function
f(t)\vcentcolon=\langle X_{\min\{t,\tau\}}-Y_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle
can only increase at times when
X_{\min\{t,\tau\}}\in B_{i}
and
t<\tau
.
Using
item

(1)
from
Lemma

3.5
and using that
Y^{+}
takes values on the side of
A^{+}
towards which
s_{i}(0)\hat{n}
points, it holds at such times that

(3.10)

\displaystyle\langle X_{\min\{t,\tau\}},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle<s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}\ \text{ and }\ \langle Y_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\geq s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}.

Hence, the function
f(t)
can only increase when
f(t)<0
.
Since
f(0)\leq 0
by definition of
y_{0}^{+}
it follows that
f(t)\leq 0
for all
t
.
The latter yields the upper bound in (
3.8
).

We next consider the lower bound.
Note that
\langle\vec{n}_{i}(x),\hat{n}\rangle>0
for every
x\in B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
by
item

(2)
from
Lemma

3.5
.
Hence, considering (
3.9
), the function
f(t)
can only decrease at times when
Y_{t}^{+}\in A^{+}
and
t<\tau
.
By (
3.4
), we have
Y_{t}^{+}\in A^{+}
if and only if
\langle Y_{t}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle=s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}
.
Further, for every
t<\tau
we have that
X_{t}
lies in
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
on the side of
B_{i}
towards which
s_{i}(0)\hat{n}
points.
Hence, also using
item

(3)
from
Lemma

3.5
,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\langle X_{\min\{t,\tau\}},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle>s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}-4\delta r_{\delta}.

It follows that
f(t)
can only decrease when
f(t)>-8\delta r_{\delta}
.
Combining this with the fact that
f(0)\leq-8\delta r_{\delta}
by definition of
y_{0}^{+}
yields
f(t)\leq-8\delta r_{\delta}
for all
t
, proving the lower bound.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Assume that
\delta<1/2
and consider some
x_{0}\in D
and
i\leq{m}
with
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\hat{n}^{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a unit vector orthogonal to
\hat{n}
.
Then, for every
t\geq 0
,

(3.12)

\displaystyle\bigl\lvert\langle X_{\min\{t,\tau\}},\hat{n}^{\perp}\rangle\rvert-\langle Y_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+},\hat{n}^{\perp}\rangle\bigr\rvert\leq 2\delta L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\min\{t,\tau\}}.

Proof.

Recall from (
3.5
) that
x_{0}-y_{0}^{+}
is a multiple of
\hat{n}
.
Taking the inner product with
\hat{n}^{\perp}
of (
3.9
) and using that
item

(2)
in
Lemma

3.5
implies that
\lvert\langle\vec{n}_{i}(x),\hat{n}^{\perp}\rangle\rvert\leq 2\delta
for every
x\in B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
then yields (
3.12
).
∎

3.2.3.
Local approximation for
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
and consequences for the stopping time
\tau

To make effective use of
Lemmas

3.7
and
3.8
it would be desirable to know that
t\leq\tau
with high probability.
To this end, we start by studying
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
:

Lemma 3.9
.

Suppose that
\delta<1/2
and consider some
x_{0}\in D
and
i\leq{m}
with
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
Then, with
\hat{n}
as in
Lemma

3.5
, for all
t\geq 0

(3.13)

\displaystyle L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\min\{t,\tau\}}

\displaystyle\leq(1-2\delta)^{-1}\bigl\langle X_{\min\{t,\tau\}}-X_{0}-W_{\min\{t,\tau\}},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\bigr\rangle,

(3.14)

\displaystyle L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\min\{t,\tau\}}

\displaystyle\geq(1+2\delta)^{-1}\bigl\langle X_{\min\{t,\tau\}}-X_{0}-W_{\min\{t,\tau\}},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\bigr\rangle.

In particular, with
K_{t}^{+}
as in
Section

3.2.2
,

(3.15)

\displaystyle(1+2\delta)^{-1}(K_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+}-8\delta r_{\delta})\leq L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\min\{t,\tau\}}\leq(1-2\delta)^{-1}(K_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+}+8\delta r_{\delta}).

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.5
,
B_{i}
is the only barrier which intersects
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
and (
3.7
) implies that
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})=s_{i}(0)
for all
t<\tau
.
Hence, by the stochastic differential equation in
Definition

1
,

(3.16)

\displaystyle X_{\min\{t,\tau\}}-X_{0}=W_{\min\{t,\tau\}}+\int_{0}^{\min\{t,\tau\}}s_{i}(0)\vec{n}_{i}(X_{s})\mathbbm{1}\{X_{s}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{s}.

Lemma

3.5
yields that
\|\hat{n}-\vec{n}_{i}(y)\|\leq 2\delta
for every
y\in B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
.
Since
\hat{n}
is a unit vector, it follows that
\langle\vec{n}_{i}(y),\hat{n}\rangle\in[1-2\delta,1+2\delta]
for every
y\in B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
.
The bounds in (
3.13
) and (
3.14
) now follow by rearranging (
3.16
), taking the inner product with
s_{i}(0)\hat{n}
, and using
item

(ii)
from
Definition

1
.

Further, using (
3.8
) at times
0
and
t
,

(3.17)

\displaystyle\langle X_{\min\{t,\tau\}}-X_{0},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\leq\langle Y^{+}_{\min\{t,\tau\}}-y_{0}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle+8\delta r_{\delta}.

Note that
\langle Y_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+}-y_{0}^{+}-W_{\min\{t,\tau\}},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle=K_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+}
by (
3.6
) to conclude the upper bound in (
3.15
).
The lower bound proceeds similarly.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Suppose that
\delta\leq 1/4
and consider some
x_{0}\in D
and
i\leq{m}
with
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
Then, for every
t\geq 0
,

(3.18)

\displaystyle K_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\{\langle-W_{s},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle:s\leq t\},

(3.19)

\displaystyle L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\min\{t,\tau\}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup\{\langle-W_{s},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle:s\leq t\}+16\delta r_{\delta},

(3.20)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\leq\min\{t,\tau\}}\|X_{s}-X_{0}\|

\displaystyle\leq 3\sup_{s\leq t}\|W_{s}\|+16\delta r_{\delta}.

Proof.

Since
Y_{t}^{+}
reflects on a straight line, it is a classical that the local time admits an explicit formula; see e.g.,
[
4
, Proposition 1]
.
Specifically, the unique process satisfying (
3.6
) and the characterization of
footnote

3
on page
3
is

(3.21)

\displaystyle K_{t}^{+}=\max\bigl\{0,\sup\{s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}-\langle y_{0}^{+}+W_{s},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle:s\leq t\}\bigr\}.

In particular, since
\langle y_{0}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\geq s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}
we have (
3.18
).
It was here also used that the right-hand side of (
3.18
) is a nondecreasing function to simplify by letting
s
range up to
t
instead of
\min\{t,\tau\}
.
Now, (
3.15
) with the assumption that
\delta\leq 1/4
implies (
3.19
).
Further, since
\|X_{\min\{t,\tau\}}-X_{0}\|\leq\|W_{\min\{t,\tau\}}\|+L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\min\{t,\tau\}}
by (
3.16
), we then also have (
3.20
).
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

There exist absolute constants
\delta_{0},C>0
such that the following holds.
Suppose that
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
.
Then, for every
x_{0}\in D
and
t\geq 0
, with
\tau
as in (
3.7
),

(3.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tau\leq t)\leq\delta+C(t/r_{\delta}^{2}).

Proof.

This result is stated for
arbitrary

x_{0}\in D
.
If
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})=\emptyset
, then
\tau\leq t
if and only if
\sup_{s\leq t}\|W_{t}\|\geq r_{\delta}
since
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
can only increase when
X_{t}\in B_{i}
; recall (
3.7
) and
Definition

1
.
Then,

\mathbb{P}(\tau\leq t)\leq\mathbb{P}(\sup_{s\leq t}\|W_{s}\|\geq r_{\delta})

from which (
3.22
) follows readily, and one does not require the first term; see also (
3.24
) below.

Now assume that
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
for some
i\leq{m}
.
Then, by the law of total probability,

(3.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\tau\leq t\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\leq t}\|W_{s}\|\geq r_{\delta}/6\Bigr)+\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\tau\leq t\text{ and }\sup_{s\leq t}\|W_{s}\|<r_{\delta}/6\Bigr).

Here, using the scaling principle together with Markov’s inequality
4
4

4

Sharper bounds are also available, of course, but this elementary estimate suffices for our purposes.
,

(3.24)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\leq t}\|W_{s}\|\geq r_{\delta}/6\Bigr)=\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\leq 1}\|W_{s}\|^{2}\geq\bigl(r_{\delta}/(6\sqrt{t})\bigr)^{2}\Bigr)\leq C(t/r_{\delta}^{2})

for some sufficiently large absolute constant
C>0
.

Regarding the second term in (
3.23
), the definition (
3.7
) yields that
\tau\leq t
if and only if
\sup_{s\leq\min\{t,\tau\}}\|X_{s}-X_{0}\|\geq r_{\delta}
or
E\leq L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\min\{t,\tau\}}
with
E\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\ell>0:s_{i}(\ell)\neq s_{i}(0)\}
.
To bound
\|X_{s}-X_{0}\|
, we can here use (
3.19
) from
Lemma

3.10
and take
\delta_{0}
sufficiently small so that
r_{\delta}/2+16\delta r_{\delta}<r_{\delta}
.
Then, using (
3.20
) and some direct calculations to bound
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\min\{t,\tau\}}
,

(3.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\tau\leq t\text{ and }\sup_{s\leq t}\|W_{s}\|<r_{\delta}/6\Bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(E\leq\frac{5}{6}r_{\delta}\Bigr).

If
i=0
, then it was defined that
s_{i}(t)=1
for all
t\geq 0
and hence
\mathbb{P}(E\leq r_{\delta})=0
because
E=\infty
.
Now consider
i\geq 1
.
Then,
s_{i}
is a continuous-time Markov chain on
\{-1,1\}
with transition rates
\lambda_{i}^{+}
and
\lambda_{i}^{-}
.
Hence,
E
is exponentially distributed with mean
1/\lambda_{i}^{+}
or
1/\lambda_{i}^{-}
, depending on
s_{i}(0)
.
In particular,

(3.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(E\leq\frac{5}{6}r_{\delta}\Bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(E\leq r_{\delta}\Bigr)\leq 1-\exp\bigl(-\lambda_{\max}r_{\delta}\bigr)\leq\lambda_{\max}r_{\delta}\leq\delta

where we used (
2.2
) and (
3.3
).
Combine (
3.23
)–(
3.26
) to conclude.
∎

The quality of the coupling is now immediate.
Indeed, combining
Lemmas

3.7
and
3.8
with (
3.19
) from
Lemma

3.10
, the distance between
X_{\min\{t,\tau\}}
and
Y_{\min\{t,\tau\}}^{+}
is at most
C\delta(r_{\delta}+\sup_{s\leq t}\|W_{s}\|)
for some
C>0
.
We may here assume that
\tau>t
and
\sup_{s\leq t}\|W_{s}\|<r_{\delta}
with high probability; recall
Corollary

3.11
and (
3.24
).
This gives the following:

Corollary 3.12
.

There exist absolute constants
\delta_{0},C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that the following holds.
Suppose that
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
and consider some
x_{0}\in D
and
i\leq{m}
with
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
Then, for every
t\geq 0
, with
Y^{+}_{t}
as in
Section

3.2.2
,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\leq t}\|X_{s}-Y_{s}^{+}\|>C_{1}\delta r_{\delta}\Bigr)\leq\delta+C_{2}\bigl(t/r_{\delta}^{2}\bigr).

Moreover, for every
x_{0}\in D
with
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})=\emptyset
,

(3.28)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\leq t}\|X_{s}-(x_{0}+W_{s})\|>0\Bigr)\leq C_{2}\bigl(t/r_{\delta}^{2}\bigr).

Remark 3.13
.

That
X_{t}
starts from the exact center of the ball
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
is merely a notational convenience and is not essential for (
3.27
).
That is, if one instead considers a process starting from some
\tilde{x}_{0}
on the same side as
x_{0}
and with
\|x_{0}-\tilde{x}_{0}\|\leq r_{\delta}/2
, then the proof remains valid up to modifications to the absolute constants due to the fact that a stronger bound on
\sup_{s\leq t}\|W_{s}\|
is then be required in (
3.23
).

4.
Proof of
Theorem

2.3

Algorithms for the fixed-frequency regime are given in
Section

4.1
and the proofs of consistency are given in
Sections

4.2
,
4.3
and
4.4
.
Let us note that, while our theoretical performance guarantees are only when
t\leq c\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
for some small
c>0
, these algorithms are explicitly designed to also cope when the time between samples is large.
It would be interesting future work to also quantify the performance in such regimes.

4.1.
Algorithms

The key concept captured by reflected Brownian motion with semipermeable barriers is that
X_{t}
typically stays on the same side of barriers as
X_{0}
.
This suggests a discontinuity for the transition kernel
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot\mid X_{0}=x_{0})
when
x_{0}
crosses a barrier.
Our estimation procedures will exploit this discontinuity.

For a notion of continuity, we will rely on the
Wasserstein distance with truncation level
\mathfrak{u}>0
.
That is, the metric for probability distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(P,Q)\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{X\sim P,Y\sim Q}\mathbb{E}\bigl[d_{\mathfrak{u}}(X,Y)\bigr]\quad\text{ with }\quad d_{\mathfrak{u}}(x,y)\vcentcolon=\min\{\|x-y\|,\mathfrak{u}\}

where the infimum runs over couplings with marginal distributions
P
and
Q
.
The truncation is here mainly a technical convenience: it avoids worries regarding outliers in the observed data which could, in principle, lead to false positives.

We further require an empirical estimator for the transition kernel.
To this end, suppose that we are given a subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
, set
\mathcal{I}_{S}\vcentcolon=\{0\leq i\leq\lfloor T/t\rfloor-1:X_{it}\in S\}
, and define

(4.2)

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{S}\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\text{the zero measure }&\text{ if }\mathcal{I}_{S}=\emptyset\\
(\#\mathcal{I}_{S})^{-1}\sum_{i\in\#\mathcal{I}_{S}}\delta_{X_{(i+1)t}}&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Having the case
\mathcal{I}_{S}=\emptyset
in mind, let us also extend
\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
by defining that
\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(P,o)\vcentcolon=\mathfrak{u}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(o,o)\vcentcolon=0
when
o
is the zero measure and
P
is a probability measure.

Algorithm

1
discretizes the domain into small boxes and detects a discontinuity by comparing the associated transition kernels.
This estimation procedure is consistent:

Proposition 4.1
.

There exist absolute constants
c_{1},\ldots,c_{5}>0
such that the following holds for every
\eta\in(0,1)
,
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
,
\varepsilon\leq c_{2}\sqrt{t}
, and
T>0
.

Assume that
X_{0}\sim\pi
starts in stationarity and that the observation time satisfies

(4.3)

\displaystyle T\geq c_{3}\frac{t_{\rm{mix}}}{\varepsilon^{2}\pi_{\min}}\ln\Bigl(\frac{1}{\eta}\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Bigr)

Then, the output of
Algorithm

1
with sensitivity threshold
\mathfrak{s}\vcentcolon=c_{4}\sqrt{t}
, discretization scale
\epsilon\vcentcolon=\varepsilon/(3\sqrt{2})
, and truncation level
\mathfrak{u}\vcentcolon=c_{5}\sqrt{t}
satisfies
\mathbb{P}(d_{\rm{H}}(\hat{B},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\varepsilon)\geq 1-\eta
.

Algorithm 1

INPUT:
Observed data sequence
\{X_{it}:i=0,\ldots,\lfloor T/t\rfloor\}
.

INPUT:
Sensitivity threshold
\mathfrak{s}>0
, discretization scale
\epsilon>0
, and truncation level
\mathfrak{u}>0
.

OUTPUT:
Subset
\hat{B}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
approximating
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
.

1.

\hat{B}\leftarrow\emptyset
.

2.

for all
integers
j,k\in\mathbb{Z}

do

3.

for all
integers
-2\leq h_{j},h_{k}\leq 2

do

4.

\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j},k+h_{k})

5.

\leftarrow[(j+h_{j})\epsilon,(j+h_{j}+1)\epsilon]\times[(k+h_{k})\epsilon,(k+h_{k}+1)\epsilon]

6.

if

\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)},\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j},k+h_{k})})\geq\mathfrak{s}
for some
\lvert h_{j}\rvert,\lvert h_{k}\rvert\leq 2

then

7.

\hat{B}\leftarrow\hat{B}\cup\mathcal{S}(j,k)

The performance of
Algorithm

1
is satisfactory if one merely desires a rough estimate with
\varepsilon
fixed.
Indeed, (
4.3
) then only asks that
T
is large relative to
t_{\rm{mix}}/\pi_{\min}
, and a condition of this type is necessary; recall
Section

2.2
.
However, if a precise estimate with
\varepsilon\to 0
is desired, then the condition in (
4.3
) that
T
should be of order
\geq\ln(1/\varepsilon)/\varepsilon^{2}
is suboptimal.
To achieve the rate
\ln(1/\varepsilon)/\varepsilon^{3/2}
claimed in
Theorem

2.3
we next iteratively refine the estimate.

Algorithm

2
uses an initial estimate of the barriers to estimate their direction (line
5
) and subsequently looks for discontinuities in the transition kernel along parallel rectangular regions whose direction approximately matches that of the barrier (lines
6
to
10
).
Each application of the latter algorithm improves the error by a constant factor:

Proposition 4.2
.

There exist absolute constants
c_{1},\ldots,c_{7}>0
such that the following holds for every
\eta\in(0,1)
,
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
,
\varepsilon\leq c_{2}\kappa t
, and
T>0
.
Assume that
X_{0}\sim\pi
, assume that
T
satisfies (
2.8
) with respect to
c_{3}
, and define

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}\vcentcolon=c_{4}\sqrt{t},\ \ \ell\vcentcolon=c_{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon/\kappa},\ \ \epsilon\vcentcolon=\kappa\ell^{2},\ \text{ and }\ \mathfrak{u}=c_{6}\sqrt{t}.

Then, there exists an event
\mathcal{E}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\geq 1-\eta
such that the following holds whenever
\mathcal{E}
occurs: for every
\mathscr{E}>0
satisfying
\varepsilon\leq\mathscr{E}\leq c_{7}\ell
and every
\mathfrak{B}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\mathscr{E}
the output
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
of
Algorithm

2
with the foregoing parameters satisfies

d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\prime},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\mathscr{E}/2.

Algorithm 2

INPUT:
Observed data sequence
\{X_{it}:i=0,\ldots,\lfloor T/t\rfloor\}
.

INPUT:
Parameters
\mathfrak{s},\epsilon,\mathfrak{u},\ell,\mathscr{E}>0
and initial estimate
\mathfrak{B}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

OUTPUT:
Improved estimate
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
for
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
.

1.

\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\leftarrow\emptyset
.

2.

for all
integers
j,k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
0\leq n\leq\lfloor 2\pi\ell/\epsilon\rfloor

do

3.

p_{j,k}\leftarrow(j\epsilon,k\epsilon)

4.

\vec{w}_{n}\leftarrow(\cos({n}\epsilon/\ell),\sin({n}\epsilon/\ell))
,
\vec{w}_{n}^{\perp}\leftarrow(-\sin({n}\epsilon/\ell),\cos({n}\epsilon/\ell))

5.

if

\max_{\pm\in\{+,-\}}\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{y\in\mathfrak{B}}\|p_{j,k}\pm\ell\vec{w}_{n}-y\|\leq\mathscr{E}+2\epsilon

then

6.

for all
integers
-2\leq h\leq 2

do

7.

\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\leftarrow\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\lvert\langle x-p_{j,k},\vec{w}_{n}^{\perp}\rangle-h\epsilon\rvert\leq\epsilon/2,

8.

\lvert\langle x-p_{j,k},\ \vec{w}_{n}\rangle\rvert\leq\ell/10\}

9.

if

\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},0)},\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)})\geq\mathfrak{s}
for some
\lvert h\rvert\leq 2

then

10.

\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\cup\{p_{j,k}\}

Theorem

2.3
then follows by using
Algorithm

1
to produce an initial estimate and subsequently refining with
Algorithm

2
until
\mathscr{E}/2\leq\varepsilon
.
In fact, it suffices to apply
Algorithm

2

\lceil C\ln(1/\varepsilon\kappa)\rceil
-many times for some
C>0
; see
Appendix

C
for detailed computations.
It remains to prove
Propositions

4.1
and
4.2
.
This is done in
Section

4.4
.

Remark 4.3
.

The improved performance of
Algorithm

2
is ultimately because the convergence rate of
\hat{P}_{S}
depends on the size of the set
S
; see
Lemma

4.10
.
A square box with all points at distance
\leq\varepsilon
from a barrier has to have an area of order
\leq\varepsilon^{2}
, while a rectangular region can have area of order
\varepsilon\times\sqrt{\varepsilon/\kappa}=\sqrt{\varepsilon^{3}/\kappa}
if it is oriented parallel to the barrier.

Remark 4.4
.

Computation of Wasserstein distance can be accomplished in practice by reducing to a linear program
[
46
, §2.3]
.

Remark 4.5
.

An alternative algorithm which can work for arbitrarily large intersample spacing
t
is to search for discontinuities in an empirical estimator for the stationary distribution.
However, performance will then only be good if the stationary distribution has a significant discontinuity on the semipermeable barriers.
This will not always be the case.

4.2.
Transition kernel behaves continuously on fixed side of the barriers

To avoid false positives where a barrier is detected though there is none, we show that the transition kernel behaves continuously as a function of the initial condition so long as the latter does not cross any barrier.

Note that the following estimate remains valid when the initial condition is arbitrarily close to a barrier.
This is important to ensure that there is no limit to the resolution which
Algorithms

1
and
2
can achieve.

Proposition 4.6
.

For every
\zeta>0
there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending only on
\zeta
such that for every
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
, every
x_{0},\tilde{x}_{0}\in D
which are on the same side of each barrier and satisfy
\|x_{0}-\tilde{x}_{0}\|\leq c_{2}\sqrt{t}
, and every truncation level
\mathfrak{u}>0
,

(4.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}\Bigl(\mathbb{P}\bigl(X_{t}\in\cdot\mid X_{0}=x_{0}\bigr),\mathbb{P}\bigl(X_{t}\in\cdot\mid X_{0}=\tilde{x}_{0}\bigr)\Bigr)\leq\zeta\bigr(\sqrt{t}+\mathfrak{u}\bigl).

Proof.

Consider processes
X_{t}
and
\tilde{X}_{t}
satisfying
Definition

1
with
X_{0}=x_{0}
and
\tilde{X}_{0}=\tilde{x}_{0}
and driven by the same
W_{t}
and
s_{i}
; see
Figure

5
for a visualization.
Then, recalling the definition of truncated Wasserstein distance from (
4.1
) and using this coupling, it suffices to show that

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|X_{t}-\tilde{X}_{t}\|>\zeta\sqrt{t})\leq\zeta.

To show this, we rely on the tools from
Section

3.2
with

\delta\vcentcolon=c_{3}\sqrt{t}/\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}
where
c_{3}
is a large
\zeta
-dependent constant whose value will be fixed further on.

We start with the case where
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta}/2)\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
for some
i\leq{m}
.
Then, in particular,
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\delta_{0},C_{1},C_{2}
be the constants from
Corollary

3.12
.
The assumption on
t
yields that
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
if
c_{1}
is sufficiently small to ensure that
c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}\leq\delta_{0}
.
Hence,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|X_{t}-Y_{t}^{+}\|>C_{1}\delta r_{\delta})\leq\delta+C_{2}(t/r_{\delta}^{2})\quad\text{ if }c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}\leq\delta_{0}.

Recall the definition of
r_{\delta}
from (
3.3
), the definition of
\delta
after (
4.6
), and the assumption regarding
\|x_{0}-\tilde{x}_{0}\|
.
It follows that
\|x_{0}-\tilde{x}_{0}\|\leq r_{\delta}/2
whenever
c_{2}<c_{3}/2
is sufficiently small.
Hence, by
Corollary

3.12
and
Remark

3.13
, possibly modifying
\delta_{0},C_{1},
and
C_{2}
,

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\tilde{X}_{t}-\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+}\|>C_{1}\delta r_{\delta})\leq\delta+C_{2}(t/r_{\delta}^{2})\quad\text{ if }\ c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}\leq\delta_{0}\ \text{ and }\ c_{2}<c_{3}/2

where
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+}
reflects on the same line
A^{+}
as
Y_{t}^{+}
, but now with initial condition
\tilde{y}_{0}^{+}
defined as in (
3.5
) with
x_{0}
replaced by
\tilde{x}_{0}
.
(
Remark

3.13
is relevant as we want to keep the same reflection line as for
Y_{t}^{+}
, also when
\tilde{x}_{0}\neq x_{0}
.)

By (
3.6
) and the analogous stochastic differential equation for
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+}
,

(4.9)

\displaystyle Y_{t}^{+}-\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+}=y_{0}^{+}-\tilde{y}_{0}^{+}+s_{i}(0)\hat{n}(K_{t}^{+}-\tilde{K}_{t}^{+}).

Since
\tilde{K}_{t}^{+}
is a nondecreasing process, the function
f(t)\vcentcolon=\langle Y_{t}^{+}-\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle
can only increase when
K_{t}^{+}
does so.
The latter occurs if and only if
Y_{t}^{+}\in A^{+}
in which case
f(t)\leq 0
since
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+}
is defined to live on the side of
A^{+}
towards which
s_{i}(0)\hat{n}
points.
Similarly,
f(t)
can only decrease if
f(t)\geq 0
.
It follows that
\lvert f(t)\rvert
is a nonincreasing function, and hence

(4.10)

\displaystyle\lvert\langle Y_{t}^{+}-\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+},\hat{n}\rangle\rvert\leq\lvert\langle y_{0}^{+}-\tilde{y}_{0}^{+},\hat{n}\rangle\rvert.

Let
\hat{n}^{\perp}
be a unit vector which is orthogonal to
\hat{n}
.
Then, (
4.9
) yields that
\langle Y_{t}^{+}-\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+},\hat{n}^{\perp}\rangle=\langle y_{0}^{+}-\tilde{y}_{0}^{+},\hat{n}^{\perp}\rangle
for all
t\geq 0
.
Now, using Pythagoras’ theorem and the triangle inequality,

(4.11)

\displaystyle\|Y_{t}^{+}-\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+}\|\leq\|y_{0}^{+}-\tilde{y}_{0}^{+}\|\leq\|y_{0}^{+}-x_{0}\|+\|\tilde{y}_{0}^{+}-\tilde{x}_{0}\|+\|x_{0}-\tilde{x}_{0}\|.

Using
item

(3)
from
Lemma

3.5
in the definition (
3.5
) of
y_{0}^{+}
, it holds that
\|y_{0}^{+}-x_{0}\|\leq 8\delta r_{\delta}
.
Similarly, also using that
\tilde{x}_{0}
is on the same side of the barrier as
x_{0}
and inside
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
, we have
\|\tilde{y}_{0}^{+}-\tilde{x}_{0}\|\leq 8\delta r_{\delta}
.
Further, recall that
\|x_{0}-\tilde{x}_{0}\|\leq c_{2}\sqrt{t}
by assumption.

Hence, if the complementary events for (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) occur, then

(4.12)

\displaystyle\|X_{t}-\tilde{X}_{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|X_{t}-Y_{t}^{+}\|+\|\tilde{X}_{t}-\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+}\|+\|Y_{t}-\tilde{Y}_{t}^{+}\|\leq 2(C_{1}+8)\delta r_{\delta}+c_{2}\sqrt{t}.

Recall the definition of
\delta
from the paragraph after (
4.6
) and recall the assumed upper bound on
t
.
It follows that
\delta\leq c_{3}\sqrt{c}_{1}
and
r_{\delta}=c_{3}\sqrt{t}
.
Hence, combining (
4.12
) with the probabilities from (
4.7
) and (
4.8
),

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|X_{t}-\tilde{X}_{t}\|>\bigl(2(C_{1}+8)c_{3}^{2}{}

\displaystyle{}\sqrt{c}_{1}+c_{2}\bigr)\sqrt{t}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{3}\sqrt{c}_{1}+2C_{2}/c_{3}^{2}\text{ if }c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}\leq\delta_{0}\text{ and }c_{2}<c_{3}/2.

This implies (
4.6
) if one takes
c_{3}
sufficiently large to ensure that
2C_{2}/c_{3}^{2}\leq\zeta/2
and subsequently takes
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
sufficiently small to ensure that the conditions in (
4.13
) are satisfied, and that
2c_{3}\sqrt{c}_{1}<\zeta/2
and
2(C_{1}+8)c_{3}^{2}\sqrt{c}_{1}+c_{2}\leq\zeta
.

It remains to consider the case where
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta}/2)\cap B_{i}=\emptyset
for all
i\leq{m}
.
If
\mathscr{B}(\tilde{x}_{0},r_{\delta}/2)\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then the preceding proof applies.
Assume that
\mathscr{B}(\tilde{x}_{0},r_{\delta}/2)\cap(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})=\emptyset
.
Then, (
3.28
) in
Corollary

3.12
yields that
X_{t}=x_{0}+W_{t}
and
\tilde{X}_{t}=\tilde{x}_{0}+W_{t}
with high probability.
Hence, (
4.6
) follows similarly to the foregoing.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Figure 5
.

Visualization for the coupling used in the proof of
Proposition

4.6
.

4.3.
Transition kernel has a discontinuity when crossing barriers

Recall that the intuitive reason why we expect a discontinuity is that
X_{t}
typically stays on the same side of all barriers as
X_{0}
when
t
is sufficiently small.
The following result makes this rigorous:

Lemma 4.7
.

For every
\eta\in(0,1)
there exists a constant
c>0
depending only on
\eta
such that for every
t\leq c\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
and
x_{0}\in D
,

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})=s_{i}(0),\forall i\leq{m}\mid X_{0}=x_{0})\geq 1-\eta.

Proof.

This is immediate from
Corollary

3.11
since
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})=s_{i}(0)
when
\tau>t
; recall (
3.7
).
More specifically, let
\delta_{0},C>0
be as in
Corollary

3.11
and set
\delta\vcentcolon=\min\{\delta_{0},\eta/2\}
.
Then, the right-hand side of (
3.22
) is
\leq\eta
if
c
is sufficiently small to ensure that
C(c/\delta^{2})\leq\eta/2
.
∎

Lemma

4.7
is highly suggestive of a discontinuity upon
x_{0}
crossing
B_{i}
but does not yet formally imply one.
In principle, it is still possible that most probability mass is located on the barrier or very close to it.
The following result shows that this does not occur:

Lemma 4.8
.

For every
\eta\in(0,1)
there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending only on
\eta
such that for every
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
and
x_{0}\in D
,

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\forall y\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}:\|X_{t}-y\|\geq c_{2}\sqrt{t}\mid X_{0}=x_{0})\geq 1-\eta.

Similarly to
Proposition

4.6
, this again follows from the approximation technique of
Section

3.2
.
Indeed, the process
Y_{t}^{+}
can easily be shown to be at distance of order
\sqrt{t}
from all barriers since its law is analytically tractable; see
Appendix

D
for detailed computations.

We now get discontinuity when the initial condition crosses a barrier.
Further, this discontinuity persists if the initial conditions are allowed to be random:

Corollary 4.9
.

There exist absolute constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
such that for every
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
and every truncation level
\mathfrak{u}\geq c_{2}\sqrt{t}
,

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot),\mathbb{P}(\tilde{X}_{t}\in\cdot)\bigr)\geq c_{3}\sqrt{t}

where
X_{t}
and
\tilde{X}_{t}
are processes satisfying
Definition

1
for which there is a barrier
B_{i}
with
X_{0}
on the positive side and
\tilde{X}_{0}
on the negative side almost surely.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary coupling
(X_{t},\tilde{X}_{t})
.
Then, by
Lemma

4.7
with
\eta=1/4
and the union bound, if
c_{1}
is sufficiently small,

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\text{ and }\tilde{X}_{t}\text{ are on different sides of }B_{i})\geq 1/2.

By
Lemma

4.8
with
\eta=1/4
, there exists
c_{2}>0
such that when
c_{1}
is sufficiently small,

(4.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\text{ is at distance }\geq c_{2}\sqrt{t}\text{ from }B_{i})\geq 1-1/4.

The combination of the events described in (
4.17
) and (
4.18
) implies that
\|X_{t}-\tilde{X}_{t}\|\geq c_{2}\sqrt{t}
.
Hence, by the law of total expectation, we have with
c_{3}\vcentcolon=c_{2}/4
that for every
\mathfrak{u}\geq c_{2}\sqrt{t}
,

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\min\{\|X_{t}-\tilde{X}_{t}\|,\mathfrak{u}\}]\geq c_{2}\sqrt{t}\mathbb{P}(\|X_{t}-\tilde{X}_{t}\|\geq c_{2}\sqrt{t})\geq c_{3}\sqrt{t}.

Recall the definition of truncated Wasserstein distance from (
4.1
) and use that the coupling
(X_{t},\tilde{X}_{t})
is arbitrary to conclude the proof.
∎

4.4.
Proof of
Propositions

4.1
and
4.2

The behavior established for the transition kernel in
Proposition

4.6
and
Corollary

4.9
persists in the empirical estimator from (
4.2
):

Lemma 4.10
.

For every
\alpha,\beta>0
there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
depending only on
\alpha
and
\beta
such that the following holds for every
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
and
\mathfrak{u}\leq\alpha\sqrt{t}
.

Assume that
X_{0}\sim\pi
starts in stationarity.
Then, for every
S\subseteq D
with
\operatorname{Area}(S)>0
and diameter
\sup_{a,b\in S}\|a-b\|\leq\sqrt{t}
, denoting
P_{S}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot\mid X_{0}\in S)
,

(4.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{S},P_{S})\leq\beta\sqrt{t}\bigr)\geq 1-c_{2}\exp\bigl(-c_{3}\pi_{\min}\operatorname{Area}(S)T/t_{\rm{mix}}\rceil\bigr).

This follows by using a Markovian Bernstein inequality from
[
44
]
to show that the number of transitions from
S
to a fixed small target region concentrates, and subsequently taking a union bound over a large but fixed number of target regions; see
Appendix

E
.

Proposition

4.1
regarding the performance of
Algorithm

1
now follows readily.
First, using
Lemma

4.10
with the lower bound on
T
from (
4.3
), it may be ensured that
\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)}\approx P_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)}
for all boxes
\mathcal{S}(j,k)\subseteq D
.
If
\mathcal{S}(j,k)
is at a sufficient distance from all barriers so that
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j},k+h_{k})
is on the same side for every
\lvert h_{j}\rvert,\lvert h_{k}\rvert\leq 2
, then
Proposition

4.6
implies that continuity holds for the estimated transition kernel and
Algorithm

1
will not add
\mathcal{S}(j,k)
to
\hat{B}
.
Conversely,
Corollary

4.9
yields a discontinuity when
\mathcal{S}(j,k)
is sufficiently close to some barrier
B_{i}
, in which case
Algorithm

1
adds
\mathcal{S}(j,k)
to
\hat{B}
.
Detailed computations making this outline rigorous are given in
Appendix

F
.

The proof idea for
Proposition

4.2
is similar, although more technical in execution.
Fix some small
\beta>0
and let
\mathcal{E}
be the event where concentration occurs:

(4.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\vcentcolon=\{\omega:\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)},P_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)})\leq\beta\sqrt{t},\ \ \forall j,k,{n},h\text{ with }\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\subseteq D\}.

Lemma

4.10
with the union bound then ensures that
\mathcal{E}
holds with high probability, so it remains to show that
Algorithm

2
improves the error by a constant factor whenever
\mathcal{E}
occurs.

The idea is again to rely on
Proposition

4.6
and
Corollary

4.9
.
In order for those results to be applicable, however, it has to be shown that the orientation of the barriers is estimated sufficiently precisely in
Algorithm

2
to ensure that the rectangular regions
\{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h):\lvert h\rvert\leq 2\}
all remain on a fixed side when
p_{j,k}
is at distance
>\mathscr{E}/2
from the barriers, or that they change sides when
p_{j,k}
is close to a barrier.
The latter follows by using the assumption that
d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\mathscr{E}
together with the fact that the curvature of the barriers is bounded.
Specifically, this follows from the variant of
Lemma

3.5
outlined in
Remark

3.6
.
Details for the proof of
Proposition

4.2
are given in
Appendix

G
.

5.
Proof of
Theorem

2.5
and
Corollary

2.6

An algorithm for the high-frequency regime is given in
Section

5.1
and the proofs of consistency are given in
Sections

5.2
and
5.3
.

5.1.
Algorithm

Consider a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and a unit vector
\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Then, with
\vec{v}^{\perp}
a unit vector orthogonal to
\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu
and
\ell>0
a parameter to be chosen later, we define a parallel rectangular strip and half-space by

(5.1)

\displaystyle R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\bigl\{q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\langle q-p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\rangle\in[\sqrt{t},2\sqrt{t}],\,\langle q-p,\vec{v}^{\perp}\rangle\in[-\ell,\ell]\bigr\},

(5.2)

\displaystyle H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\bigl\{q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\langle q-p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\rangle<-\sqrt{t}\bigr\}.

It is intuitive that
X_{t}
will then only rarely transition from
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
to
H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
if a barrier separates these sets.
Correspondingly,
Algorithm

3
employs a grid search over
p
and
\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu
and detects a barrier if
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
was visited many times but there were few transitions to
H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
.
This algorithm has the desired properties:

Proposition 5.1
.

Adopt the notation of
Theorem

2.5
.
Then there are constants
c_{4},\ldots,c_{7}
depending only on
\eta
such that the performance guarantees in
Theorem

2.5
and
Corollary

2.6
are satisfied if
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
is the output of
Algorithm

3
with parameters

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}\vcentcolon=c_{4}\ \ \epsilon\vcentcolon=c_{5}\sqrt{t},\ \ \ell\vcentcolon=c_{6}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t}\ \text{ and }\ \mathfrak{n}_{0}\vcentcolon=c_{7}\ln(T/t).

Algorithm 3

INPUT:
Observed data sequence
\{X_{it}:i=0,\ldots,\lfloor T/t\rfloor\}
.

INPUT:
Parameters
\mathfrak{s},\epsilon,\ell,\mathfrak{n}_{0}>0
.

OUTPUT:
Subset
\hat{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
approximating
\cup_{i=0}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{i}
.

1.

\hat{\mathcal{X}}\leftarrow\emptyset
.

2.

for all
integers
j,k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
