0\leq n\leq\lfloor 2\pi\ell/\epsilon\rfloor

do

3.

p_{j,k}\leftarrow(j\epsilon,k\epsilon)
;
\vec{v}_{n}\leftarrow(\cos(n\epsilon/\ell),\sin(n\epsilon/\ell))

4.

N_{j,k,n}\leftarrow\#\{0\leq i\leq\lfloor T/t\rfloor-1:X_{it}\in R_{+}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})\}

5.

M_{j,k,n}\leftarrow\#\{0\leq i\leq\lfloor T/t\rfloor-1:X_{it}\in R_{+}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n}),\,X_{(i+1)t}\in H_{-}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})\}

6.

if

N_{j,k,n}\geq\mathfrak{n}_{0}
and
M_{j,k,n}/N_{j,k,n}<\mathfrak{s}

then

7.

\hat{\mathcal{X}}\leftarrow\hat{\mathcal{X}}\cup\{p_{j,k}\}

Remark 5.2
.

Algorithm

3
with appropriate parameters can also be shown to be consistent in the fixed-frequency setting of
Section

4.1
, but will be more sensitive to the sampling rate.
Specifically, it is intuitive that
Algorithms

1
and
2
should typically continue working when
t\gg\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
, although a greater observation period may be needed to compensate for the discontinuity in the transition kernel becoming less pronounced.

On the other hand,
Algorithm

3
will generally stop working altogether when
t
is large, no matter how great the observation period, as the linear approximation of the barriers fails at the spatial scale which is then necessary.

5.2.
Concentration for the number of transitions and number of visits

Random fluctuations can cause deviations from the expected behavior in the transition or visit counts used in
Algorithm

3
.
This could potentially result in false positives or negatives.

The following result will be used to control such fluctuations despite the fact that the considered time frame does not necessarily exceed the mixing time of the Markov chain.
The proof amounts to an application of Hoeffding’s inequality and is deferred to
Appendix

H
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots
be a sequence of
\{0,1\}
-valued random variables, not necessarily independent or identically distributed, such that

\mathbb{P}(Y_{n}=1\mid Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-1})\geq q

for some
q>0
and every
n\geq 1
.
Then, for every
\eta\in(0,q)
and
n_{0}\geq 1
,

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\geq n(q-\eta)\ \text{ for all }\ n\geq n_{0}\Bigr)\geq 1-2\eta^{-2}\exp\Bigl(-2\eta^{2}n_{0}\Bigr).

5.2.1.
Transitions from
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
to
H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)

The following result shows that the number of transitions behaves “as expected” if
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
was visited sufficiently many times.
This explains the role of the parameter
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
in
Algorithm

3
: it reduces false positives by pruning locations which were not visited enough and could hence suffer from large fluctuations.

Corollary 5.4
.

There exist absolute constants
c_{1},\ldots,c_{7}>0
such that the following holds for every
\sqrt{t}\leq\ell\leq c_{1}(t\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\})^{1/4}
,
n_{0}\geq 1
, and initial condition
x_{0}\in D
.

Given some
T>0
, a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and a unit vector
\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, denote

(5.5)

\displaystyle N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\#\{0\leq i\leq\lfloor T/t\rfloor-1:X_{it}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\},

(5.6)

\displaystyle M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\#\{0\leq i\leq\lfloor T/t\rfloor-1:X_{it}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu),X_{(i+1)t}\in H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}.

Then, if
p
satisfies
\|p-y\|\geq c_{2}\ell
for every
y\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
,

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)/N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)<c_{3}\text{ and }N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq n_{0}\bigr)\leq c_{4}\exp(-c_{5}n_{0}).

On the other hand, if
\|p-y\|\leq c_{6}\sqrt{t}
and
\min_{\pm\in\{+,-\}}\|\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\pm\vec{n}_{i}(y)\|\leq c_{7}\sqrt{t}/\ell
for some
y\in B_{i}
,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)/N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq c_{3}\text{ and }N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq n_{0}\bigr)\leq c_{4}\exp(-c_{5}n_{0}).

The proof is given in
Appendix

I
and relies on an application of
Lemma

5.3
with
Y_{n}
the random variable indicating if the
n
th visit to
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
was followed by a transition to
H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
.
Such transitions are unlikely in the setting of (
5.8
) where the sets are separated by a barrier, but not unlikely in the setting of (
5.7
) where there is no barrier in the way.

5.2.2.
Many visits to
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)

Corollary

5.4
will suffice to avoid false positives where some
p_{j,k}
far away from all barriers is added to
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
by
Algorithm

3
.
However, an algorithm which does not result in false positives is only useful if it also returns some true positives where points on the barrier are recovered.
To this end, it has to be shown that the count
N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
from (
5.5
) is large with high probability.

Again, the tools from
Section

3.2
can be used to approximate the barrier with a straight line.
This leads to the following preliminary reduction; see
Appendix

J
for the proof.

Lemma 5.5
.

For every
\zeta<1/2
and
\eta\in(0,1)
there exist
c_{1},\ldots,c_{5}>0
such that the following holds.
Consider
t,\ell>0
,
x_{0}\in B_{i}
,
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and a unit vector
\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with

(5.9)

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}\leq\ell

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}(t\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\})^{1/4},\qquad

\displaystyle\ell\leq c_{2}\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\},

\displaystyle\|x_{0}-p\|

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\sqrt{t},

\displaystyle\|\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu-s_{i}(0)\vec{n}_{i}(x_{0})\|\leq c_{4}\sqrt{t}/\ell.

Then, for every integer
J\geq 1
with
J\leq c_{5}\ell^{2}/t
, every
n_{0}\geq 0
, and every
0\leq\gamma<1
,

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\#\{0\leq j\leq J:{}

\displaystyle{}X_{(\gamma+j)t}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}\geq n_{0}\mid X_{0}=x_{0})\geq\mathbb{P}(N_{+}\geq n_{0})-\eta

where, with
(\mathcal{B}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
a one-dimensional Brownian motion,

(5.11)

\displaystyle N_{+}\vcentcolon=\#\{0\leq j\leq J:\lvert\mathcal{B}_{\gamma+j}\rvert\in[1+\zeta,2-\zeta]\}.

The relevance of the parameter
\gamma
in (
5.10
) for the proof is of a technical nature, arising in the arguments for (
2.12
).
In particular, the case
\gamma\neq 0
is used to deal with the fact that the stopping time does not necessarily take values in
t\mathbb{Z}
so that we may incur a non-integer multiple of
t
as a time shift after using strong Markovianity; see (
K.18
) in
Appendix

K
.

It remains to show that the random variable
N_{+}
from (
5.11
) is typically large.
This is the content of the following result, and may be the most interesting part of the proof of
Theorem

2.5
as the arguments give insight on the key difficulties for partial recovery; see also
Remark

5.7
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For every
\zeta<1/2
there exist
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
such that the following holds for every
\eta\in(0,1)
,
J\geq c_{1}\ln(c_{2}/\eta)^{2}/\eta^{2}
, and
0\leq\gamma<1
.
With
N_{+}
as in (
5.11
),

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(N_{+}\geq c_{3}\eta\sqrt{J})\geq 1-\eta.

Proof.

The idea in the following argument is to exploit that there is a nonzero probability for a contribution to the count
N_{+}
each time that the process
\mathcal{B}_{t}
moves from
0
to
2
.
This reduces us to studying the number of times
\mathcal{B}_{t}
moves from
0
to
2
.

More precisely, define a sequence of stopping times
0=\vcentcolon\sigma_{1}<\tau_{1}<\sigma_{2}<\tau_{2}<\cdots
as well as
\{0,1\}
-valued random variables
Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots
by

(5.13)

\displaystyle\tau_{i}\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle{}\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t>\sigma_{i}:\mathcal{B}_{t}=2\},\qquad\sigma_{i+1}\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t>\tau_{i}:\mathcal{B}_{t}=0\},

(5.14)

\displaystyle Y_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathbbm{1}\{\exists j\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[\sigma_{i},\tau_{i}-\gamma]:\lvert\mathcal{B}_{\gamma+j}\rvert\in[1+\zeta,2-\zeta]\}.

Let it here be understood that
[\sigma_{i},\tau_{i}-\gamma]=\emptyset
if
\tau_{i}-\gamma<\sigma_{i}
.
At any rate, if
i_{0}\geq 1
satisfies
\tau_{i_{0}}\leq J
then the intervals
[\sigma_{i},\tau_{i}-\gamma]
with
i\leq i_{0}
are disjoint subsets of
[0,J]
.
Then, comparing (
5.11
) and (
5.14
), we have
N_{+}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{i_{0}}Y_{i}
.
Hence, it holds for every
i_{0}\geq 1
that

(5.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(N_{+}\geq c_{3}\eta\sqrt{J}\Bigr)\geq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\tau_{i_{0}}\leq J\Bigr)-\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{i_{0}}Y_{i}<c_{3}\eta\sqrt{J}\Bigr).

We next estimate the two terms on the right-hand side of (
5.15
), starting with the first one.

One can represent
\tau_{i_{0}}
as a sum of
2i_{0}-1
independent copies of
\tau_{1}
as follows:

(5.16)

\displaystyle\tau_{i_{0}}=\tau_{1}+(\sigma_{2}-\tau_{1})+(\tau_{2}-\sigma_{2})+\cdots+(\sigma_{i_{0}}-\tau_{i_{0}-1})+(\tau_{i_{0}}-\sigma_{i_{0}}).

On the other hand, the hitting time
\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t>0:\mathcal{B}_{t}=2(2i_{0}-1)\}
also admits a natural decomposition as a sum of
2i_{0}-1
independent copies of
\tau_{1}
.
Consequently, these random variables have the same distribution.
In particular,

(5.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\tau_{i_{0}}\leq J\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t>0:\mathcal{B}_{t}=2(2i_{0}-1)\}\leq J\bigr).

Hence, using the scaling principle as well as the reflection principle,

(5.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\tau_{i_{0}}\leq J\bigr)=\mathbb{P}(\sup\{\mathcal{B}_{t}:t\leq J\}\geq 2(2i_{0}-1))=1-\mathbb{P}(\lvert G\rvert<2(2i_{0}-1)/\sqrt{J})

with
G
a standard Gaussian random variable.
In particular, since the density of the Gaussian distribution is bounded, there exists an absolute constant
c_{4}>0
such that

(5.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tau_{i_{0}}\leq J)\geq 1-\eta/2\ \ \text{ if }\ \ i_{0}\leq c_{4}\eta\sqrt{J}.

From here on, let
i_{0}\vcentcolon=\lfloor c_{4}\eta\sqrt{J}\rfloor
.

Note that the
Y_{i}
depend on
\mathcal{B}_{t}
through non-overlapping periods of time due to the substraction of
\gamma
in the time interval in (
5.14
).
The strong Markovianity of one-dimensional Brownian motion now implies that there exists
q>0
depending only on
\zeta
with

(5.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}=1\mid Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{i-1})\geq\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{s\geq 0}\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}=1\mid\sigma_{i}=s)\geq q.

The value
q
can be taken to be independent of
\gamma
since the worst case would be
\gamma=1
.

Lemma

5.3
now yields
c_{5},c_{6}>0
depending only on
\zeta
such that

(5.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{i_{0}}Y_{i}<(q/2)i_{0}\Bigr)\leq c_{5}\exp\Bigl(-c_{6}i_{0}\Bigr)<\eta/2\ \ \text{ if }\ \ i_{0}\geq\ln(2c_{5}/\eta)/c_{6}.

Recall that
i_{0}=\lfloor c_{4}\eta\sqrt{J}\rfloor
and
J\geq c_{1}\ln(c_{2}/\eta)^{2}/\eta^{2}
.
Taking
c_{1},c_{2}>0
sufficiently large, it can hence be ensured that the condition in (
5.21
) is satisfied.
Then, taking
c_{3}
sufficiently small in (
5.12
), the combination of (
5.15
) with (
5.19
) and (
5.21
) concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

The random variable
\tau_{i_{0}}
in (
5.16
) is Lévy-distributed.
In particular, it is heavy-tailed suggesting that optimal rates for
d_{\rm{H}}(\hat{\mathcal{X}},\cup_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{X}_{i})
may be significantly slower than for typical points.
The latter involves many points simultaneously, giving heavy tails many opportunities to realize.

5.3.
Proof of
Proposition

5.1

The key ingredients are now in place.
We here give an outline of the key ideas and defer the detailed calculations to
Appendix

K
.
Recall that
Proposition

5.1
refers to
Theorem

2.5
and
Corollary

2.6
.
Hence, it has to be shown why the performance guarantees in (
2.11
)–(
2.13
) hold.

First, using (
5.7
) from
Corollary

5.4
with
n_{0}=C\ln(T/t)
for some large
C>0
together with a union bound over all
j,k,n
for which
R_{+}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})
was visited at least once, it can be established that
Algorithm

3
does not add any
p_{j,k}
which is distant from all barriers to
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
with high probability.
This yields (
2.11
) regarding the nonoccurrence of false positives.
The reason to run the union bound over those rectangular regions which were visited at least once, instead of simply all
R_{+}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})\subseteq D
, is that this allows for an estimate which does not degrade when
\operatorname{Area}(D)
is large.
This is more efficient as
T
may be small.

Conversely, if
p_{j,k}
is close to a barrier, then taking
\vec{v}_{n}
to be an approximation of the normal vector to the barrier ensures that (
5.8
) from
Corollary

5.4
is applicable so that
Algorithm

3
adds
p_{j,k}
to
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
if
N(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})\geq\mathfrak{n}_{0}
.
Hence, (
2.12
) follows if we show that
X_{\tau}
is close to some
p_{j,k}
with at least logarithmically many visits to
R_{+}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})
.
The latter follows by using the strong Markovianity together with the results in
Section

5.2.2
if we take
J
of order
\ln(T/t)^{2}
.
The condition that
J
is of order
\leq\ell^{2}/t
in
Lemma

5.5
then requires that we take
\ell
of order
\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t}
.

Finally, the uniform recovery of
\cup_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{X}_{i}
in (
2.13
) is a relatively straightforward consequence of (
2.12
).
Specifically, considering some large integer
M\geq 1
, it can be ensured that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\sup\{\|X_{t}-X_{jT/M}\|:t\in[jT/M,(j+1)T/M]\}\leq\varepsilon/2\ \text{ for every }\ j=0,\ldots,M-1

with probability greater than
1-\eta/2
.
In particular, it then holds with

\tau_{j}\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t\geq jT/M:X_{t}\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}\}

that
\|X_{\tau_{j}}-X_{t}\|\leq\varepsilon/2
for every
t\in[jT/M,(j+1)T/M]
with
X_{t}\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
.
We can use (
2.12
) to ensure that
X_{\tau_{j}}
is at distance
\leq\varepsilon/2
from
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
with probability
\geq 1-\eta/2M
.
The desired result then follows from the triangle inequality and a union bound over all
j\leq M-1
.

6.
Proof of
Theorem

2.7

Throughout this section, we adopt the assumptions of
Theorem

2.7
.
In particular, we assume that
m=0
.
Then,
Definition

1
reduces to the definition of reflected Brownian motion (without semipermeable barriers) in
D
.
Recall from (
2.10
) that
\mathcal{X}_{0}=B_{0}\cap\{X_{t}:t\in[0,T]\}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
define a random time
\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{T\geq 0:d_{\rm{H}}(\mathcal{X}_{0},B_{0})\leq\varepsilon\}
.
Then,

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)/\ln(\varepsilon)^{2}]\leq 2\operatorname{Area}(D)/\pi.

Proof.

Let
\{A_{i}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\varepsilon$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}:1\leq i\leq N(\varepsilon)\}
be a cover for
B_{0}
of minimal cardinality consisting of arcs of length
\varepsilon
.
Here,
N(\varepsilon)
denotes the number of arcs in the cover.
Then,

\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)\leq\max_{i\leq N(\varepsilon)}\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in A_{i}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\varepsilon$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\}
.
Hence, by
[
41
, Eq.(2.2) & (2.7)]
,

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)]\leq\mu_{+}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\varepsilon$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N(\varepsilon)}\frac{1}{i}\ \text{ where }\ \mu_{+}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\varepsilon$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\leq\max_{i\leq N(\varepsilon)}\sup_{y\in D}\mathbb{E}[\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in A_{i}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\varepsilon$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\}\mid X_{0}=y].

The time
\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in A_{i}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\varepsilon$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\}
has been studied in the context of narrow escape problems in the physics literature
[
25
]
and also in the mathematical literature
[
15
]
.
In particular, with
A\subseteq B_{0}
an arc of length
\varepsilon
, it is shown in
[
15
, Eq. (1.2) and/or Theorem 5.2]
that

(6.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\ln(1/\varepsilon)}\mathbb{E}[\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in A\}\mid X_{0}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(D)]=\frac{2}{\pi}\operatorname{Area}(D).

Moreover, the convergence in (
6.2
) is uniform
5
5

5

Specifically, this follows from the proof of
[
15
, Theorem 5.2]
as the conformal map
X
introduced preceding (5.11) in
[
15
]
extends
C^{\infty}
smoothly to the boundary by the Kellog–Warschawski theorem
[
47
, Theorem 3.6]
.
This implies that all error terms in Section 5.2 of
[
15
]
are uniform on the domain.

in the choice of
A
.

To replace the initial condition by a deterministic one, we can exploit that the transition density of reflected Brownian motion on a smooth domain converges to the uniform distribution at an exponential rate (see e.g.,
[
13
, Eq. (1.4)]
).
By applying
[
3
, Theorem A]
to the discrete-time process
(X_{it})_{i=1}^{\infty}
for some arbitrary fixed
t>0
, it then follows that there exists a stopping time
\tau
with
\sup_{x_{0}\in D}\mathbb{E}[\tau\mid X_{0}=x_{0}]<\infty
such that
X_{\tau}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(D)
.
Hence,

(6.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{x_{0}\in D}{}

\displaystyle{}\mathbb{E}[\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in A\}\mid X_{0}=x_{0}]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x_{0}\in D}\mathbb{E}[\tau\mid X_{0}=x_{0}]+\mathbb{E}[\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in A^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\varepsilon$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\}\mid X_{0}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(D)].

Since the first term is bounded, it is negligible relative to the second term in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Hence, combining (
6.2
)–(
6.3
), we have that

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mu_{+}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\varepsilon$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}/\ln(1/\varepsilon)\leq 2\operatorname{Area}(D)/\pi.

Recall that the cover
\{A_{i}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\varepsilon$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}:i=1,\ldots,N(\varepsilon)\}
was chosen to have minimal cardinality.
It follows that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon N(\varepsilon)=C
with
C>0
the arc length of
B_{0}
.
In particular,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\ln(1/\varepsilon)/\ln(N(\varepsilon))=1
.
Consequently, since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\ln(n)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}1/i=1
,

(6.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\ln(1/\varepsilon)}\sum_{i=1}^{N(\varepsilon)}\frac{1}{i}=1.

Combine (
6.4
) with the preceding estimate on
\mu_{+}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\varepsilon$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
and (
6.1
) to conclude the proof.
∎

The desired result is now essentially immediate by rewriting the definition of
\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)
.
Further, we also get an explicit value for the constant
c
in
Theorem

2.7
:

Proof of
Theorem

2.7
.

By Markov’s inequality, it holds for any
\varepsilon>0
that

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(d_{\rm{H}}(\mathcal{X}_{0},B_{0})>\varepsilon\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)>T\bigr)\leq\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon)]/T.

Let
\varepsilon_{T}\vcentcolon=\exp(-\sqrt{cT/\operatorname{Area}(D)})
.
Then, for any fixed
\delta>0
it follows from
Lemma

6.1
that for
T
sufficiently large, or equivalently
\varepsilon_{T}
sufficiently small,

(6.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon_{T})]\leq(2+\delta)\operatorname{Area}(D)\ln(\varepsilon_{T})^{2}/\pi=c(2+\delta)T/\pi.

Combine (
6.5
) with (
6.6
) and take
c\leq\eta\pi/(2+\delta)
to conclude that (
2.14
) holds.
∎

7.
Case study with animal movement data

To conclude, we illustrate the applicability of the model and algorithms using data due to Loe et al.
[
39
]
.
The data contains tracks of reindeer in the period from 2009 to 2022 in the high-Arctic environment of Nordenskiöld Land, Svalbard.
The study area is dominated by two valleys and we will focus our discussion on the southern valley which is the largest of the two; see the left-hand side of
Figure

6
.
To give an indication of scale, the sides of the highlighted square region are approximately 20 kilometers long.

We decided to apply
Algorithm

1
because this algorithm is the most user-friendly, only requiring a small number of parameters.
These parameters were here picked on an ad-hoc basis; see
Appendix

L
for the details, and for similar findings in the northern valley.
The dataset comes with 116 distinct animal tracks, of which we removed three in preprocessing due to missing data.
As each of the tracks is of nontrivial length, most spanning a period of multiple years, it may be reasonable to here consider ourselves to be in a regime with large effective observation period and to hope for complete recovery; recall
Remark

2.4
.
We aggregated the remaining 113 tracks when estimating the empirical transition distributions and further used
Algorithm

1
unchanged.
The output is displayed on the right-hand side of
Figure

6
together with one of the employed tracks.

We recognize both impermeable and semipermeable barriers in the algorithm’s output.
The coastline and the slopes bordering the valley yield impermeable barriers, constraining the animals, while semipermeable barriers arise from rivers that are crossed with nontrivial frequency.
One of these rivers, the rightmost one, is so wide that the algorithm separately identifies the two sides of the river, leaving the middle open as it lacks recorded animal positions.
Other rivers are less wide and are recovered in one piece; see e.g., the vertical river on the center-left.
At any rate, we conclude that the algorithms can successfully recover barriers from real-life data.

Figure 6
.

On the left: a satellite image of Nordenskiöld Land.
On the right: one of the 113 used animal tracks in dark blue, the barriers detected by
Algorithm

1
as the red points, and a satelite image with manually highlighted rivers in light blue.

Code and data availability

Source code for
Algorithm

1
as well as the simulation scheme used for
Figure

1
have been made available at
https://github.com/Alexander-Van-Werde/Brownian-barriers.git
.
The animal movement data used in
Section

7
is available at
https://www.movebank.org/cms/webapp?gwt_fragment=page=studies,path=study2608802883
.

Appendix A
Proofs for the preliminaries of
Section

3.1

A.1.
Proof of
Proposition

3.1
and
Proposition

3.2

We start with the proof of
Proposition

3.1
.
Recall that this result concerns the existence and uniqueness of processes satisfying
Definition

1
.

Figure 7
.
Visualization of the construction which is used in the proof of existence for
Proposition

3.1
.
The process
X
starts by following a classical reflected Brownian motion with initial condition
X_{0}
, which we denote by
Y^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
.
When a random variable
\tau_{0}
indicates that the local time exceeds a threshold,
X
switches to a classical reflected Brownian motion
Y^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$1$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
which lives on the other side of the barrier and has initial condition
Y^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}_{\tau_{0}}
.
One can continue similarly, defining
X_{t}
in terms of
Y^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
when
t\in(\tau_{{n}-1},\tau_{n}]
.

Proof of existence.

We rely on the classical fact that reflected Brownian motion in a smooth domain (without semipermeable barriers) exists and use a gluing procedure to accomplish the semipermeable barriers.
A visualization of this construction may be found in
Figure

7
.

Denote
C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
for the closure of the connected component of
D\setminus(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})
indicated by the
s_{i}(0)
.
That is, the set
C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
consists of all points
x\in D
such that for every
i\leq m
the point
x
is on the positive (resp. negative) side of
B_{i}
if
s_{i}(0)=+1
(resp.
s_{i}(0)=-1
).
Here, recall from
Section

2.1
that
x
is both on the positive and negative sides if
x\in B_{i}
.

The set
C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
is a smooth domain.
Hence, classical reflected Brownian motion in
C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
and its local time exist uniquely; see
[
4
, Proposition 4]
or
[
38
, Theorem 4.3]
.
That is, there exist unique continuous processes
Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
which are adapted to the filtration generated by
X_{0}
, the
s_{i}(0)
with
1\leq i\leq{m}
, and
W_{s}
with
s\leq t
and satisfy the following properties:

(1)

The process
Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
takes values in
C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
and satisfies
Y_{0}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}=X_{0}
. The process
\mathcal{L}_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
takes values in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and satisfies
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}=0
.

(2)

The process
\mathcal{L}_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
is nondecreasing and increases at time
t
if and only if
Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in\partial C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
.
Equivalently, since
Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
for every
t\geq 0
and
C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\cap\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}=\partial C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
, the process
\mathcal{L}_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
only increases when
Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
.

(3)

The following stochastic differential equation is satisfied for all
t\geq 0
:

(A.1)

\displaystyle{\rm d}Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}={\rm d}W_{t}+\sum_{i=0}^{m}s_{i}(0)\vec{n}_{i}(Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}})\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}.

We next define
X_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
for sufficiently small values of
t
.

The local time
\mathcal{L}_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
of
Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
increases every time
Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
is in
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
.
To disambiguate the contributions corresponding to the different curves
B_{i}
, we may consider the process
\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}

for every

\displaystyle\tau_{0}\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\bigl\{t\geq 0:s_{i}\bigl({\textstyle\int_{0}^{t}}\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\bigr)\neq s_{i}(0)\text{ for some }i\leq{m}\bigr\}.

Then, we define

\displaystyle X_{t}\vcentcolon=Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\ \text{ and }\ L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}\vcentcolon={\textstyle\int_{0}^{t}}\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\ \text{ for }t\leq\tau_{0}.

Then, we define

\displaystyle X_{t}\vcentcolon=Y_{t-\tau_{n}}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}+1$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\ \text{ and }\ L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}\vcentcolon=L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau_{n}}+{\textstyle\int_{0}^{t-\tau_{n}}}\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}+1$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}+1$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\text{ for }t\in(\tau_{n},\tau_{{n}+1}].

Technically, to ensure that the processes are defined for all

almost surely.
In this context, let us note that essentially the same arguments as were given for

almost surely for every

The latter implies that

tends to infinity almost surely, as required.

That the properties in

Proof of uniqueness.

This follows from the pathwise uniqueness of reflected Brownian motion in a smooth domain (without semipermeable barriers).
The proof has a similar structure to the proof that was used in the existence part.
We next make this precise.

Suppose that we are given
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-adapted
(X_{t},L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t},\ldots,L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$m$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})
and
(\tilde{X}_{t},\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t},\ldots,\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$m$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})
with

satisfying
Definition

1
with respect to the same
W_{t}
and
s_{i}
.
We show that it then holds that
X_{t}=\tilde{X}_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}=\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
, almost surely.
Define
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-stopping times by

(A.7)

\displaystyle\tau_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\bigl\{t\geq 0:s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})\neq s_{i}(0)\text{ for some }i\in\{0,1,\ldots,{m}\}\bigr\},

(A.8)

\displaystyle\tilde{\tau}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\bigl\{t\geq 0:s_{i}(\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})\neq s_{i}(0)\text{ for some }i\in\{0,1,\ldots,{m}\}\bigr\}.

Recall the definition of the sets
C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
from the preceding proof of existence and, using
[
4
, Proposition 4.1]
, pick a reflected Brownian motion

in

and initial condition

satisfying

(A.9)

\displaystyle{\rm d}Z_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}={\rm d}W_{\tau_{0}+t}+\sum_{i=0}^{m}s_{i}(0)\vec{n}_{i}(Z_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}})\mathbbm{1}\{Z_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}K_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}.

Here, note that while the sum in (

Then, the processes defined by

(A.10)

\displaystyle Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}X_{t}&\text{ if }t\leq\tau_{0},\\

\sum_{i=0}^{m}L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau_{0}}+K_{t-\tau_{0}}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}&\text{ if }t>\tau_{0}\end{cases}

define a reflected Brownian motion in
C^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
with local time

.
One can similarly define
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
and

(A.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}=\tilde{Y}_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}},\ \forall t\geq 0)=1\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}},\ \forall t\geq 0)=1.

In particular,
\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{\tilde{Y}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
for every

.
Note that
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
for

and similarly for
\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
.
Hence, since definitions (
A.7
) and (
A.8
) refer to the same

.
It further follows from (

) that

(A.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{t}=\tilde{X}_{t},\ \forall t\leq\tau_{0})=1\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{P}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}=\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t},\ \forall t\leq\tau_{0})=1.

One can now repeat this argument for the processes

and

to conclude that equality holds for all

, and so on.
∎

Finally, we consider the adaptedness statement in

3.2
.

Proof of
Proposition

3.2
.

The uniqueness statement in
Proposition

3.1
yields that the
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-adapted processes
X_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
are uniquely defined up to null sets.
Hence, recalling from
Section

3.1
that the considered
\sigma
-algebras were all completed so that these null sets do not matter, the statement of
Proposition

3.2
is well-defined.
In particular, it suffices to prove that
\{L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}\leq\ell_{i},\,\forall i\leq m\}\in\mathcal{F}_{t,\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{m}}
and that
X_{t},L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
, and
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})
are
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted for one specific construction of these processes.

The latter follows by inspecting the proof of existence for
Proposition

3.1
.
The constructions for
X_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
given there namely only depend on
(W_{r})_{r\geq 0}
and
(s_{i}(r))_{r\geq 0}
through
(W_{r})_{r\leq t}
and
(s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{r}))_{r\leq t}
.
More precisely, recall from (
A.3
) and (
A.6
) that the definition of
X_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
for
t\in(\tau_{n},\tau_{{n}+1}]
was stated in terms of
Y^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}+1$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}_{t-\tau_{n}}
and
\{\mathcal{L}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}+1$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}_{s-\tau_{n}}:s\leq t\}
and recall from the paragraphs preceding (
A.1
) and (
A.4
) that the definition of
Y^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}+1$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}_{t}
and
\mathcal{L}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}+1$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}_{t}
only refers to
W
and
s_{i}
through
(W_{r})_{r\leq\tau_{n}+t}
and
(s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{r}))_{r\leq\tau_{n}}
.
Further, recall the definition of
\tau_{{n}}
from (
A.2
) and (
A.5
) and note that the event
t\in(\tau_{n},\tau_{{n}+1}]
only refers to
(s_{i}(r))_{r\geq 0}
through
(s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{r}))_{r\leq t}
.

Combining the foregoing observations, the event
\{L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}\leq\ell_{i},\,\forall i\leq{m}\}
only depends on
(W_{r})_{r\geq 0}
and
(s_{i}(r))_{r\geq 0}
through
(W_{r})_{r\leq t}
and
(s_{i}(r))_{r\leq\ell_{i}}
.
This shows that
\{L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}\leq\ell_{i},\,\forall i\leq{m}\}\in\mathcal{F}_{t,\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{m}}
, as desired.
Further, restricted to this event the definitions of
X_{t}
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
only depend on
W
and
s_{i}
through
(W_{r})_{r\leq t}
and
(s_{i}(r))_{r\leq\ell_{i}}
.
This shows that
X_{t}
,
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
, and
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})
define
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted processes, as desired.
∎

A.2.
Proof of
Proposition

3.3

The main goal is to establish strong Markovianity for the process
\mathscr{X}_{t}
defined in
Proposition

3.3
.
The weak Markovianity for
X_{t}
then follows with a small additional argument; see
Section

A.2.2
.

We will exploit that the processes
W_{t}
and
s_{i}(t)
in
Definition

1
are Markovian.
A possible delicate point is that
Definition

1
does not refer to
s_{i}(t)
directly, but rather to
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})
.
Some care is hence required to rigorously show that no problems enter through the local times
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
.
Preparatory work related to this is done in
Section

A.2.1
.

A.2.1.
Generalized strong Markovianity

If
\tau
is a stopping time for
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, then this means by definition of the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}
that
\{\tau\leq t,L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}\leq\ell_{i},\,\forall i\leq{m}\}\in\mathcal{F}_{t,\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{m}}
.
This suggests that
\tau
and
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
together again behave like a stopping time in a suitably generalized sense:

Definition 2
.

Fix an integer
d\geq 1
and consider a collection of
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{H}_{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{d}}
indexed by
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
.
Then,
(\mathcal{H}_{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{d}})_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}\geq 0}
is called a
multivariate filtration
if it holds that
\mathcal{H}_{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{d}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{d}}
whenever
t_{i}\leq r_{i}
for every
i\leq d
.

Definition 3
.

An
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
-valued random variable
\mathscr{T}\vcentcolon=(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{d})
is said to be a
multivariate stopping time
for a multivariate filtration
(\mathcal{H}_{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{d}})_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}\geq 0}
if
\{\tau_{i}\leq t_{i},\,\forall i\leq d\}\in\mathcal{H}_{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{d}}
for every
t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}\geq 0
.

If
\mathscr{T}
is a multivariate stopping time, then we denote
\mathcal{H}_{\mathscr{T}}
for the
\sigma
-algebra of all events
E
with
E\cap\{\tau_{i}\leq t_{i},\,\forall i\leq d\}\in\mathcal{H}_{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{d}}
for every
t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}\geq 0
.

Lemma

A.1
below establishes a multivariate generalization of the classical result that Feller processes are strongly Markovian.
Indeed, the classical result is recovered as the case
d=1
.
The usefulness for our purposes, however, is exactly in the multivariate nature of the lemma as this is what will allow us to rigorously show that no problems enter through the local times; see the proof of
Lemma

A.2
.

Definition 4
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a Polish space and fix an integer
d\geq 1
.

(1)

A function
M:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathscr{P}
is said to be
marginally right-continuous
if for any
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
-valued sequence
(t^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$1$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{n},\ldots,t^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$d$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
with nonincreasing coordinates,

(A.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}M\bigl(t^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$1$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{n},\ldots,t^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$d$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{n}\bigr)=M\bigl(\lim_{n\to\infty}t^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$1$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{n},\ldots,\lim_{n\to\infty}t^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$d$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{n}\bigr).

(2)

Consider a random function
M
from
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
to
\mathscr{P}
and let
\mathcal{H}_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}}\vcentcolon=\sigma\{M(r_{1},\ldots,r_{d}):r_{i}\leq t_{i}\}
.
Then, we call
M

weakly multivariate Markovian
if for every fixed
t,\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
and every measurable
E\subseteq\mathscr{P}
it holds with
P^{\delta}_{y}(E)\vcentcolon=\mathbb{P}(M(\delta)\in E\mid M(0)=y)
that

(A.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(M(t+\delta)\in E\mid\mathcal{H}_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}}\bigr)=P^{\delta}_{M(t)}(E).

(3)

Suppose that
M
is weakly multivariate Markovian with marginally right-continuous sample paths.
Then,
M
is said to have the
Feller property
if for every
p_{0}\in\mathscr{P}
, every sequence
(y_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}y_{n}=p_{0}
, and every fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
it holds that

(A.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigl[f(M(t))\mid M(0)=y_{n}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[f(M(t))\mid M(0)=p_{0}\bigr]

for every bounded continuous function
f:\mathscr{P}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma A.1

(Strong multivariate Markovianity)
.

Suppose that
M
is weakly multivariate Markovian, marginally right-continuous, and has the Feller property.
Then, for every measurable
E\subseteq\mathscr{P}
, multivariate stopping time
\mathscr{T}
, and
t\vcentcolon=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{d})
,

(A.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(M\bigl(\mathscr{T}+t\bigr)\in E\mid\mathcal{H}_{\mathscr{T}}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(M\bigl(\mathscr{T}+t\bigr)\in E\mid M(\mathscr{T})\bigr)

where
\mathcal{H}_{\mathscr{T}}
is the
\sigma
-algebra resulting from
Definition

3
applied to the multivariate filtration in
item

(2)
of
Definition

4
.

Proof.

This is a direct generalization of the classical proof for
d=1
, but we give details for completeness.
We start with a reduction to a statement about continuous functions; see (
A.19
) below.
By the characterizing property of conditional probability, (
A.16
) is equivalent to showing that for every
A\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathscr{T}}
,

(A.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}\mathbbm{1}\{M(\mathscr{T}+t)\in E\}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}\{M(\mathscr{T}+t)\in E\}\mid M(\mathscr{T})\bigr]\bigr].

Further, it suffices to consider the special case where
E\subseteq\mathscr{P}
is an open set.
Then, there exists a nondecreasing sequence of continuous and bounded functions
f_{n}:\mathscr{P}\to E
converging pointwise to
\mathbbm{1}_{E}
.
So, applying the monotone convergence theorem twice,

(A.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}\{M(\mathscr{T}+{}

\displaystyle{}t)\in E\}\mid M(\mathscr{T})\bigr]\bigr]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}\mathbb{E}\bigl[f_{n}\bigl(M(\mathscr{T}+t)\bigr)\mid M(\mathscr{T})\bigr]\bigr].

Similarly,
\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}\mathbbm{1}\{M(\mathscr{T}+t)\in E\}]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}f_{n}(M(\mathscr{T}+t))]
.
It hence suffices to show that for every bounded and continuous function
f:\mathscr{P}\to\mathbb{R}
and every event
A\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathscr{T}}
,

(A.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}f\bigl(M(\mathscr{T}+t)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}\mathbb{E}\bigl[f\bigl(M(\mathscr{T}+t)\bigr)\mid M(\mathscr{T})\bigr]\bigr].

To prove (
A.19
), we start with the special case where
\mathscr{T}
takes values in a countable set
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
.
For any fixed
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in\mathcal{I}
one can readily verify that
A\cap\{\mathscr{T}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\}\in\mathcal{H}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}
using that
A\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathscr{T}}
and that
\mathscr{T}
is a multivariate stopping time.
Then, also using that
A=\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}A\cap\{\mathscr{T}=i\}
together with the tower property,

(A.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}f\bigl(M(\mathscr{T}+t)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A\cap\{\mathscr{T}=i\}}f\bigl(M(i+t)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A\cap\{\mathscr{T}=i\}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[f\bigl(M(i+t)\bigr)\mid\mathcal{H}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}\bigr]\bigr].

Here, since
M
was assumed to be weakly multivariate Markovian,

(A.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[f\bigl(M(i+t)\bigr)\mid\mathcal{H}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[f\bigl(M(i+t)\bigr)\mid M(i)\bigr].

Combine (
A.20
) and (
A.21
) to conclude that (
A.19
) holds when
\mathscr{T}
takes values in a countable set.

We recover the general case using a discretization argument.
For any
{n}\geq 0
, consider the partition of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
into intervals
I_{j,{n}}\vcentcolon=((j-1)2^{-{n}},j2^{-{n}}]
for
j\geq 0
.
Then, writing
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{d}
for the components of
\mathscr{T}
, we define

(A.22)

\displaystyle\tau_{i}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\vcentcolon=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j2^{-{n}}\mathbbm{1}\{\tau_{i}\in I_{j,{n}}\}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathscr{T}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\vcentcolon=\bigl(\tau_{1}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}},\tau_{2}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}},\ldots,\tau_{n}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\bigr).

Note that
\mathscr{T}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
is then again a multivariate stopping time, but now has values in a countable set.
Hence, by the previously established case,

(A.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}f\bigl(M(\mathscr{T}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}+t)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}\mathbb{E}\bigl[f\bigl(M(\mathscr{T}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}+t)\bigr)\mid M(\mathscr{T}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})\bigr]\bigr]

Note that
\tau_{i}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
is a nonincreasing in
{n}
and that
\lim_{{n}\to\infty}\tau_{i}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}=\tau_{i}
with probability one.
Consequently, using that
M
is marginally right-continuous,

\lim_{n\to\infty}M(\mathscr{T}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})=M(\mathscr{T}).

Hence, by the dominated convergence theorem and the Feller property, which are applicable since the function
f
was assumed to be bounded and continuous,

(A.24)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}\mathbb{E}\bigl[f\bigl(M(\mathscr{T}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}+t)\bigr)\mid M(\mathscr{T}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})\bigr]\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}\mathbb{E}\bigl[f\bigl(M(\mathscr{T}+t)\bigr)\mid M(\mathscr{T})\bigr]\bigr].

Similarly, by the dominated convergence theorem,

(A.25)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}f\bigl(M(\mathscr{T}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}+t)\bigr)\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{A}f\bigl(M(\mathscr{T}+t)\bigr)\bigr].

Combine (
A.23
), (
A.24
), and (
A.25
) to conclude that (
A.19
) also holds in the general case.
This concludes the proof.
∎

A.2.2.
Proof of Markovianity

Recall the definition of the filtrations
\mathcal{F}_{t,\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{m}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{t}
from
Proposition

3.2
.

Lemma A.2
.

Define an
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted process
\mathscr{X}_{t}\vcentcolon=(X_{t},(s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}))_{i=0}^{m})
as in
Proposition

3.3
.
Then, for every measurable
E\subseteq D\times\{+1,-1\}^{m+1}
, every
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-stopping time
\tau
, and every fixed
u\geq 0
,

(A.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{X}_{\tau+u}\in E\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau})=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{X}_{\tau+u}\in E\mid\mathscr{X}_{\tau}).

Proof.

Let
u\geq 0
and define
\tilde{X}_{u}\vcentcolon=X_{\tau+u}
,
\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{u}\vcentcolon=L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau+u}-L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau}
,
\tilde{W}_{u}\vcentcolon=W_{\tau+u}-W_{\tau}
,
\tilde{s}_{i}(u)\vcentcolon=s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau}+u)
.
Then, direct verification of
Definition

1
shows that
\tilde{X}_{u}
is a reflected Brownian motion with semipermeable barriers and local times
\tilde{L}^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{u}
driven by
\tilde{W}_{u}
and
\tilde{s}_{i}(u)
.
We next show that
\tilde{W}_{u}
and
\tilde{s}_{i}(u)
are again Markovian processes from the same distribution.

Let
d\vcentcolon={m}+2
and
\mathscr{T}\vcentcolon=(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau},\ldots,L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}${m}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau},\tau)
.
Then, using that
\tau
is a
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-stopping time together with
Proposition

3.2
, expanding the definitions shows that
\mathscr{T}
is a multivariate stopping time for the multivariate filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t,\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{m}})_{t,\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{m}\geq 0}
in the sense of
Definition

3
.
Further, the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{\mathscr{T}}
defined using
Definition

3
is equal to
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
.
Consider the process defined by

(A.27)

\displaystyle M(t_{1},\ldots,t_{d})\vcentcolon=(s_{0}(t_{1}),s_{1}(t_{2}),\ldots,s_{m}(t_{d-1}),W_{t_{d}}).

This process has values in the Polish space
\mathscr{P}\vcentcolon=\{-1,1\}^{m+1}\times\mathbb{R}
and it is readily verified that it has the Feller property and marginally right-continuous sample paths.
Further, weak multivariate Markovianity follows using that the processes
s_{i}
and
W
are independent and individually weakly (even strongly) Markovian.

Hence, the strong multivariate Markovianity from
Lemma

A.1
is applicable to the process
M
.
Using this, it follows that conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
the processes
\tilde{W}
and
\tilde{s}_{i}
are again a Wiener process and
\{-1,+1\}
-valued Markov chain with the same transition rates as they had initially.
In particular, the law of the processes
\tilde{W}
and
\tilde{s}_{i}
only depends on the initial conditions
\tilde{s}_{i}(0)
.

Proposition

3.1
implies pathwise uniqueness for
\tilde{X}_{u}
.
Hence, since pathwise uniqueness implies uniqueness in law, the law of
\tilde{X}_{u}
conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
is uniquely determined by the initial condition
\tilde{X}_{0}
and the processes
\tilde{s}_{i}(u)
and
\tilde{W}_{u}
.
Recall that the law of the latter processes is determined by the initial condition
\tilde{s}_{i}(0)
.
This shows that the law of
\tilde{X}_{u}
is uniquely determined in terms of
X_{\tau}
and
s_{i}(\tau)
, as desired.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.3
.

The strong Markovianity of
\mathscr{X}_{t}
is shown in
Lemma

A.2
so it remains to establish weak Markovianity for
X_{t}
.

When
u=0
there is nothing to prove, so assume that
u>0
.
Then, for every
\eta\in(0,1)
conditioning on
X_{u-\varepsilon}
and
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{u-\varepsilon})
for some sufficiently small
\varepsilon
depending on
\eta
and using the strong Markovianity,
Lemma

4.8
yields that
X_{u}\not\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
with probability
\geq 1-\eta
.
(This is not circular as the arguments for
Lemma

4.8
do not specifically require the weak Markovianity.)
Hence, since
\eta
is arbitrary, we have that
X_{u}\not\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
with probability
1
.

When
X_{u}\not\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
, then
item

(iii)
in
Definition

1
implies that one can deduce the value of
s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{u})
from
X_{u}
.
Hence, the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X_{u}
is equal to the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathscr{X}_{u}=(X_{t},(s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})_{i=0}^{m}))
.
The weak Markovianity of
X_{t}
hence follows from the strong Markovianity of
\mathscr{X}_{t}
.
∎

A.3.
Expression for the local time

Recall that (
2.1
) claimed a more explicit description for the local time.
It is now a convenient location to give a formal proof.

Let us remark that this formula was only provided for the definiteness and will not be required for any of the other results of this paper.
The reader may hence feel free to skip this section.

Proposition A.3
.

Fix some
T>0
.
Then, almost surely, for every
i\in\{0,1\ldots,{m}\}
the local time at the
i
th barrier satisfies

(A.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Bigl\lvert L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{\exists y\in B_{i}:\|X_{s}-y\|<\varepsilon\}\,{\rm d}s\Bigr\rvert=0.

Proof.

That (
A.28
) holds for reflected Brownian motion on a smooth domain without semipermeable barriers is classical; see, e.g.,
[
14
, Theorem 2.6]
for a proof.
Our case with semipermeable barriers can be reduced to this classical setting by a stopping time argument.

More precisely, recall that the construction from the proof of existence in
Section

A.1
used an increasing sequence of stopping times
(\tau_{n})_{{n}=0}^{\infty}
to define
X_{t}
and the local times
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}
in terms of a classical reflected Brownian motion
Y^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
and its local time
\mathcal{L}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
.
In particular, by the expression for the local time in (
A.3
) and (
A.6
),

(A.29)

\displaystyle L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}=\begin{cases}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}&\text{ for }t\leq\tau_{0},\\
L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau_{n}}+\int_{0}^{t-\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}+1$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}+1$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}&\text{ for }t\in(\tau_{n},\tau_{{n}+1}].\end{cases}

Expanding (
A.29
) recursively when
t\in(\tau_{n},\tau_{{n}+1}]
yields a sum of
{n}+1
integrals.
To match this, one can similarly decompose
\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{\exists y\in B_{i}:\|X_{s}-y\|<\varepsilon\}\,{\rm d}s
into a sum of integrals.
Then, using that
X_{t}=Y_{t-\tau_{{n}-1}}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
for
t\in(\tau_{{n}-1},\tau_{n}]
by (
A.3
) and (
A.6
), we have the following coarse upper bound:

(A.30)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Bigl\lvert L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t}-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{\exists y\in B_{i}:\|X_{s}-y\|<\varepsilon\}\,{\rm d}s\Bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{n}=0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{1}\{T\leq\tau_{{n}}\}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Bigl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}\,{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{\exists y\in B_{i}:\|Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}${n}$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}-y\|<\varepsilon\}\,{\rm d}s\Bigr\rvert.

Here,
Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}
is a classical reflected Brownian motion in a smooth domain.
By using the aforementioned classical result, it may then be shown that for every
{n}\geq 0
,

(A.31)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Bigl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}\in B_{i}\}\,{\rm d}\mathcal{L}_{r}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{\exists y\in B_{i}:\|Y_{s}^{{\text{{$[$}{\hskip-0.3pt}$n$\hskip-0.3pt{$]$}}}}-y\|<\varepsilon\}\,{\rm d}s\Bigr\rvert=0,

almost surely.
Now, since there are almost surely finitely many terms on the right-hand side of (
A.30
), the combination of (
A.30
) and (
A.31
) concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of
Lemma

3.5

Proof.

Recall from
Section

2.1
that it was assumed that the barriers
B_{i}
do not intersect.
Consequently, since
(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r)
is connected for every
r\leq\rho
by definition of
\rho
in (
2.4
), there exists at most one
B_{i}
with
B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r)\neq\emptyset
.
In particular, this holds for
r=r_{\delta}
since
\delta<1/2\leq 1
.

Assume that a barrier
B_{i}
intersecting
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
exists.
Pick some
y_{0}\in B_{i}
with
\|y_{0}-x_{0}\|=\min\{\|y-x_{0}\|:y\in B_{i}\}
.
The minimality of the distance to
x_{0}
together with the assumption that
B_{i}
intersects
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
then yields
y_{0}\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
.
Define
\hat{n}\vcentcolon=\vec{n}_{i}(y_{0})
and
\mathfrak{c}\vcentcolon=\langle y_{0},\hat{n}\rangle
.

Pick an arc-length parametrization
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to B_{i}
with
\phi(0)=y_{0}
.
Here, since
B_{i}
is a closed curve, it should be understood that
\phi
is periodic with period given by the arc length of
B_{i}
.
We next construct a small neighborhood of
0
such that the restriction of
\phi
to the neighborhood parametrizes
B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
and subsequently verify items
(1)
–
(3)
.

The minimality of the distance of
y_{0}
to
x_{0}
implies that
\langle{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0),y_{0}-x_{0}\rangle=0
.
Recall from (
2.3
) that
\kappa
is a bound on the curvature of
B_{i}
.
Hence, the first Frenet–Serret formula
[
20
, p.70, Eq. (7.1)]
implies that
\|\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}t^{2}}\phi(t)\|\leq\kappa
and consequently
\|{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(s)-{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)\|\leq\kappa s
for every
s>0
.
Then, by the fundamental theorem of calculus,

(B.1)

\displaystyle\langle{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0),\phi(s)-\phi(0)\rangle={\textstyle\int_{0}^{s}}\langle{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0),{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(t)\rangle\,{\rm d}t\geq s-\frac{\kappa}{2}s^{2}.

It follows that
\langle{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0),\phi(2r_{\delta})-\phi(0)\rangle\geq 2(1-\kappa r_{\delta})r_{\delta}
.
Here, it holds that
2(1-\kappa r_{\delta})>1
due to the assumption that
\delta<1/2
and the fact that
\kappa r_{\delta}\leq\delta
by (
3.3
).
Considering that
\langle{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0),y_{0}-x_{0}\rangle=0
and
\phi(0)=y_{0}
, it now follows that
\langle{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0),\phi(2r_{\delta})-x_{0}\rangle>r_{\delta}
.
Consequently, since
{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)
is a unit vector by
\phi
being an arc-length parametrization, we have
\|\phi(2r_{\delta})-x_{0}\|>r_{\delta}
.

Recall that
\phi(0)=y_{0}
is an element of
B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
.
Let
s_{+}\vcentcolon=\min\{t>0:\phi(t)\not\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\}
and note that the preceding shows that
s_{+}<2r_{\delta}
.
It can similarly be shown that
s_{-}>-2r_{\delta}
for
s_{-}\vcentcolon=\min\{t<0:\phi(t)\not\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\}
.
Since
\phi
is surjective and
B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
is connected, the restriction of
\phi
to
(s_{-},s_{+})
then yields a parametrization for
B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
.

Let us proceed to the verification of
item

(1)
.
Recall that
\mathfrak{c}=\langle\phi(0),\hat{n}\rangle
.
Hence, it suffices to show that
\lvert\langle\phi(s)-\phi(0),\hat{n}\rangle\rvert<4\delta r_{\delta}
for every
s\in(s_{-},s_{+})
.
For every such
s
,

(B.2)

\displaystyle\phi(s)-\phi(0)={\textstyle\int_{0}^{s}}{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(t)\,{\rm d}t=s{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)+{\textstyle\int_{0}^{s}}({\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(t)-{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0))\,{\rm d}t.

Recall that
\hat{n}
is the normal vector to
B_{i}
at
y_{0}=\phi(0)
and hence orthogonal to the tangent vector
{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)
.
Consequently, using that
\|{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(s)-{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)\|\leq\kappa\lvert s\rvert
together with the fact that
\lvert s\rvert<2r_{\delta}
for
s\in(s_{-},s_{+})
,

(B.3)

\displaystyle\lvert\langle\phi(s)-\phi(0),\hat{n}\rangle\rvert\leq 2r_{\delta}\hskip-2.5pt\sup_{s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]}\|{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(s)-{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)\|<4\kappa r_{\delta}^{2}\leq 4\delta r_{\delta}.

This concludes the proof of
item

(1)
.

The second Frenet–Serret formula
[
20
, p.74, Eq. (7.1’)]
yields that
\|{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\vec{n}_{i}(\phi(t))\|\leq\kappa
.
Hence, since
\hat{n}=\vec{n}_{i}(\phi(0))
, it holds that
\|\vec{n}_{i}(\phi(s))-\hat{n}\|\leq\lvert s\rvert\kappa
for every
s
.
In particular, for every
s\in(s_{-},s_{+})
we have
\|\vec{n}_{i}(\phi(s))-\hat{n}\|<2\delta
.
This proves
item

(2)
.

Finally, let us prove
item

(3)
.
For brevity, we focus on the first part of the claim.
(The second part follows similarly if one replaces the roles of positive and negative sides in the arguments.)
We start by considering points of the form
y_{0}+\alpha\hat{n}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and subsequently employ a reduction argument.

As a preparatory result, we claim that the only point in
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap B_{i}
of the form
y_{0}+\alpha\hat{n}
is
y_{0}
itself.
Recalling that
y_{0}=\phi(0)
and that
\hat{n}=\vec{n}_{i}(y_{0})
is orthogonal to
{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)
, this claim is equivalent to showing that
\langle{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0),\phi(0)-\phi(s)\rangle=0
for
s\in(s_{-},s_{+})
if and only if
s=0
.
The latter holds true.
Indeed, one can check that the right-hand side of (
B.1
) is
>0
for
s\in(0,s_{+})
, and it may similarly be shown that
\langle{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0),\phi(0)-\phi(s)\rangle<0
for
s\in(s_{-},0)
.

Recall from
Section

2.1
that
\vec{n}_{i}
points to the positive side of
B_{i}
.
This means that
y_{0}+\varepsilon\hat{n}
(resp.
y_{0}-\varepsilon\hat{n}
) is strictly on the positive (resp. negative) side of
B_{i}
for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.
However, the side can only change when crossing
B_{i}
, so, using the foregoing preparatory result, it follows that a point of the form
y_{0}+\alpha\hat{n}
in
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
lies on the positive side of
B_{i}
if and only if
\alpha\geq 0
.

Now, consider an arbitrary
z\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
with
\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle\leq\mathfrak{c}-4\delta r_{\delta}
.
Then, by considering the contrapositive, the desired claim from
item

(3)
follows if we show that such a
z
is
not
on the positive side.

We will apply the foregoing result to the point
\hat{z}\vcentcolon=y_{0}+\alpha\hat{n}
with
\alpha\vcentcolon=\langle z-y_{0},\hat{n}\rangle
.
First, let us check that this point is again in
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
.
Recall from paragraph preceding (
B.1
) that
x_{0}-y_{0}
is orthogonal to
{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)
.
Equivalently
x_{0}-y_{0}=\langle x_{0}-y_{0},\hat{n}\rangle\hat{n}
, and hence

(B.4)

\displaystyle\hat{z}=y_{0}+\langle z-y_{0},\hat{n}\rangle\hat{n}=y_{0}+(x_{0}-y_{0})+\langle z-x_{0},\hat{n}\rangle\hat{n}=x_{0}+\langle z-x_{0},\hat{n}\rangle\hat{n}.

Consequently, we have
\|\hat{z}-x_{0}\|\leq\|z-x_{0}\|
which implies that
\hat{z}\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
, as desired.
Further, since
\mathfrak{c}=\langle y_{0},\hat{n}\rangle
, we have that
\alpha\leq-4\delta r_{\delta}
.
This value is strictly negative implying that
\hat{z}
is not on the positive side of
B_{i}
.

Finally, to transfer this to
z
, consider the line segment connecting
z
to
\hat{z}
.
All points in this line segment are again elements of
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
because balls are convex sets.
Now recall from
item

(1)
that every
y\in B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
satisfies
\langle y,\hat{n}\rangle>\mathfrak{c}-4\delta r_{\delta}
.
Considering that
\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle=\langle\hat{z},\hat{n}\rangle\leq\mathfrak{c}-4\delta r_{\delta}
it hence follows that the line segment can not intersect
B_{i}
.
This implies that
z
is also not on the positive side, concluding the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Proof that
Theorem

2.3
follows from
Propositions

4.1
and
4.2

Proof of
Theorem

2.3
.

To avoid ambiguity with other parts of the proofs, let us denote
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{3}>0
for the constants appearing in the statement of
Theorem

2.3
.
Then, the assumption is that

(C.1)

\displaystyle t\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\},\ \ \varepsilon\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\kappa t,\ \ T\geq\mathcal{C}_{3}\frac{t_{\rm{mix}}}{\pi_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr).

Let
\mathscr{E}_{0}\vcentcolon=\sqrt{2}(\varepsilon^{3}/\kappa)^{1/4}
.
Then, in particular,

(C.2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{0}\leq\sqrt{2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/4}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{3/4}\sqrt{t}\quad\text{ and }\quad T\geq\mathcal{C}_{3}\frac{t_{\rm{mix}}}{\pi_{\min}}\frac{2}{\mathscr{E}_{0}^{2}}\ln\Bigl(\frac{2}{\eta}\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\mathscr{E}_{0}^{2}}\Bigr).

Consequently, taking
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
sufficiently small and
\mathcal{C}_{3}
sufficiently large, we may assume that
Proposition

4.1
is applicable when
\varepsilon
is replaced by
\mathscr{E}_{0}
and
\eta
is replaced by
\eta/2
.
That is, the parameters in
Algorithm

1
can be chosen such that the output
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
satisfies

(C.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B}_{0},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\mathscr{E}_{0}\bigr)\geq 1-\eta/2.

If
\mathscr{E}_{0}<\varepsilon
then we are done, so let us assume that
\mathscr{E}_{0}\geq\varepsilon
.

A preliminary estimate is required.
Recall that
B_{0}=\partial D
and that the curvature of
B_{0}
is bounded by
\kappa
.
This implies that
\operatorname{Area}(D)\geq\pi/\kappa^{2}
; see e.g.,
[
42
]
.
In particular, taking
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\leq 1
and using (
C.1
), we have
\operatorname{Area}(D)\geq\pi\sqrt{\varepsilon^{3}/\kappa}
, and hence

(C.4)

\displaystyle\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr)=\ln\Bigl(\frac{2\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr)-\ln(2)\geq c\ln\Bigl(\frac{2\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr)

with
c>0
an absolute constant which is sufficiently small such that
(1-c)\ln(2\pi)\geq\ln(2)
.

We will apply
Proposition

4.1
with
\eta
replaced by
\eta/2
and next verify that the constraints can be satisfied by choosing
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{3}
appropriately.
First, using (
C.4
), the constraint on
T
referring to (
2.8
) may be satisfied by taking
\mathcal{C}_{3}
sufficiently large in (
C.1
).
The conditions on
t
and
\varepsilon
can further also be satisfied by taking
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
sufficiently small; specifically smaller than the constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
from
Proposition

4.1
, respectively.
Finally, the required upper bound on
\mathscr{E}_{0}
may also be assumed to hold by taking
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
sufficiently small: recall the definition of
\ell
in (
4.4
) and note that
\sqrt{2}(\varepsilon^{3}/\kappa)^{1/4}\leq\sqrt{2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/4}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/4}\sqrt{\varepsilon/\kappa}
due to (
C.1
).

It follows that there exists an event
\mathcal{E}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\geq 1-\eta/2
such that the following holds.
Define scalars
\mathscr{E}_{1},\mathscr{E}_{2},\ldots
by
\mathscr{E}_{j}=\max\{\mathscr{E}_{j-1}/2,\varepsilon\}
for every
j\geq 1
, and recursively define
\mathfrak{B}_{1},\mathfrak{B}_{2},\ldots\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
by applying
Algorithm

2
on the previous estimate.
Then, for all
j\geq 1
,

(C.5)

\displaystyle d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B}_{j},\cup_{i=0}^{{m}}B_{i})\leq\max\{\mathscr{E}_{0}/2^{j},\varepsilon\}\ \text{ if }\mathcal{E}\text{ occurs and }d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B}_{0},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\mathscr{E}_{0}.

In particular, considering (
C.3
) and that
\mathcal{E}
occurs with probability
\geq 1-\eta/2
,

(C.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B}_{J},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\varepsilon\bigr)\geq 1-\eta\text{ for }J\vcentcolon=\lceil\log_{2}(\mathscr{E}_{0}/\varepsilon)\rceil.

Let
\hat{B}\vcentcolon=\mathfrak{B}_{J}
to conclude the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of
Lemma

4.8

Proof of
Lemma

4.8
.

We rely on the tools from
Section

3.2
.
Let
c_{1},c_{2}
be as in
Lemma

4.8
and set
\delta\vcentcolon=c_{3}\sqrt{t}/\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}
with
c_{3}
a constant, depending on
\eta
, to be chosen later.

Let us start with the case where
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
for some
i\leq{m}
.
By
item

(1)
from
Lemma

3.5
, every point in
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap B_{i}
satisfies
\langle y,s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\leq s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}
.
Hence, using that the distance to any point outside
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
is
\geq r_{\delta}-\|X_{t}-X_{0}\|
, it suffices to show that

(D.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\langle X_{t},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle>s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}+c_{2}\sqrt{t}\ \text{ and }\ \|X_{t}-X_{0}\|\leq r_{\delta}-c_{2}\sqrt{t}\Bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

A visualization of the event in (
D.1
) may be found in
Figure

8
.

Figure 8
.
Visualization of the event from (
D.1
) in the proof of
Lemma

4.8
.
Note that every point in the blue spherical cap is at distance
\geq c_{2}\sqrt{t}
from
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
.

Let
\delta_{0},C_{1},C_{2}
be as in
Corollary

3.12
.
By definition of
\delta
, we have
r_{\delta}=c_{3}\sqrt{t}
.
Further, by the assumption on
t
, we have
\delta\leq c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}
.
Hence, using
Corollary

3.12
,

(D.2)

\displaystyle{}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\langle X_{t},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\leq s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}+c_{2}\sqrt{t}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\langle Y_{t}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\leq s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}+(C_{1}c_{3}^{2}\sqrt{c_{1}}+c_{2})\sqrt{t}\Bigr)+c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}+C_{2}/c_{3}^{2}\ \text{ if }\ c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}\leq\delta_{0}.

Here, note that
\langle Y_{t}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle-(s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta})
is a one-dimensional reflected Brownian motion with initial condition
z_{0}\vcentcolon=\langle y_{0}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle-(s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta})
.
In particular, for every
x\geq 0
,

(D.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\langle Y_{t}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\leq s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}+x\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert\sqrt{t}N+z_{0}\rvert\leq x\bigr)

with
N
a standard normal random variable.
Here, since the density of the standard normal distribution is bounded,
\mathbb{P}(N\in[(z_{0}-x)/\sqrt{t},(z_{0}+x)/\sqrt{t}])\leq C_{4}x/\sqrt{t}
for some absolute constant
C_{4}>0
.
Combining (
D.2
) and (
D.3
) then yields that

(D.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\langle X_{t},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\leq s_{i}(0)\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}{}

\displaystyle{}+c_{2}\sqrt{t}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}\bigl(C_{1}c_{3}^{2}\sqrt{c_{1}}+c_{2}\bigr)+c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}+C_{2}/c_{3}^{2}\ \text{ if }\ c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}\leq\delta_{0}.

As for the second part of the event in (
D.1
), using that
r_{\delta}=c_{3}\sqrt{t}
,

(D.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\|X_{t}-X_{0}\|>r_{\delta}-c_{2}\sqrt{t}\Bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\|X_{t}-X_{0}\|>r_{\delta}/2\Bigr)\quad\text{ if }\ c_{2}\leq c_{3}/2.

Recall (
3.3
) and note that
r_{\delta}/2=r_{\delta/2}
.
Hence, by
Corollary

3.11
,

(D.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\|X_{t}-X_{0}\|>r_{\delta}-c_{2}\sqrt{t}\Bigr)\leq c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}/2+4C_{5}/c_{3}^{2}\quad\text{ if }c_{2}\leq c_{3}/2\text{ and }c_{3}\sqrt{c_{1}}/2\leq\delta_{0}^{\prime}

with
\delta_{0}^{\prime},C_{5}
the absolute constants from
Corollary

3.11
.

The combination of (
D.2
) and (
D.6
) yields (
D.1
) by choosing
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
appropriately, similarly to the argument after (
4.13
).
That is, one takes
c_{3}
sufficiently large to ensure that
C_{2}/c_{3}\leq\eta/3
and
4C_{5}/c_{3}^{2}\leq\eta/3
, and subsequently takes
c_{1},c_{2}
to be sufficiently small to ensure that the contribution of all remaining terms is
\leq\eta/3
and that the conditions hold.

It remains to consider the case where
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\cap(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})=\emptyset
.
Then,
X_{t}
is of distance
\geq c_{2}\sqrt{t}
from all barriers if
\|X_{t}-X_{0}\|\leq r_{\delta}-c_{2}\sqrt{t}
, so one can proceed similarly to (
D.5
).
This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of
Lemma

4.10

Fix some measurable subset
S\subseteq D
with
\operatorname{Area}(S)>0
and recall that our goal is to show that
\hat{P}_{S}\approx P_{S}
with
\hat{P}_{S}
as in (
4.2
) and
P_{S}
given by

(E.1)

\displaystyle P_{S}(E)=\pi(S)^{-1}\int_{S}\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in E\mid X_{0}=x_{0})\ {\rm d}\pi(x_{0})\ \ \text{ for every measurable }E\subseteq D.

Here,
\pi
denotes the stationary distribution; recall
Section

2.2
.

Proof-technically, it is convenient to introduce an additional discretization.
Fix a small constant
\mathfrak{d}>0
and, for any probability measure
Q
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[Q]
be the probability measure found by averaging over the bins of the grid
\mathfrak{d}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
That is, the measure whose density is constant on
B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\vcentcolon=[x,x+\mathfrak{d})\times[y,y+\mathfrak{d})
for every
x,y\in\mathfrak{d}\mathbb{Z}
and which satisfies
\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[Q](B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y))=Q(B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y))
.
A visualization may be found in
Figure

9
.
Then, it suffices to show that
\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\hat{P}_{S}]\approx\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[P_{s}]
:

A probability distribution
Q

The binned version
\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[Q]

Figure 9
.

Visualization of the binning operation used in
Appendix

E
.

Lemma E.1
.

Fix constants
\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{u}>0
.
Then, for every probability measure
Q
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(E.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}\bigl(Q,\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[Q]\bigr)\leq\min\{\sqrt{2}\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{u}\}.

Proof.

Consider a
Q
-distributed random variable
X
and let
Y
be a
\operatorname{Unif}(B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y))
-distributed random variable conditional on the event
X\in B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)
.
Then, recalling the definition of truncated Wasserstein from (
4.1
) and using that
Y
is
\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[Q]
-distributed,

(E.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(Q,\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[Q])\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl[d_{\mathfrak{u}}(X,Y)\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\min\{\|X-Y\|,\mathfrak{u}\}\bigr]\leq\min\{\sqrt{2}\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{u}\}

where the final inequality used that
\|a-b\|\leq\sqrt{2}\mathfrak{d}
for every
a,b\in B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)
.
∎

Given two finite measures
\mu,\nu
defined on the same measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
, the
total variation distance
is defined by

(E.4)

\displaystyle\|\mu-\nu\|_{{\rm{TV}}}\vcentcolon=\sup_{E\in\mathcal{F}}\lvert\mu(E)-\nu(E)\rvert.

The following result, whose proof is given in
Section

E.1
, establishes concentration of
\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\hat{P}_{S}]
for the total variation distance and, as a consequence, also for truncated Wasserstein.

Lemma E.2
.

For every
\eta\in(0,1)
and
\gamma>0
there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
depending only on
\eta
and
\gamma
such that the following holds when
\mathfrak{d}\vcentcolon=\gamma\sqrt{t}
.

Assume that
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
and assume that
\|a-b\|\leq\sqrt{t}
for every
a,b\in S
.
Further, suppose that
X_{0}\sim\pi
starts from the stationary distribution.
Then,

(E.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\hat{P}_{S}]-\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[P_{S}]\|_{{\rm{TV}}}>\eta\bigr)\leq c_{2}\exp\bigl(-c_{3}\pi(S)T/t_{\rm{mix}}\bigr).

In particular, for every truncation level
\mathfrak{u}>0
,

(E.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\hat{P}_{S}],\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[P_{S}])>\eta\mathfrak{u}\bigr)\leq c_{2}\exp\bigl(-c_{3}\pi(S)T/t_{\rm{mix}}\bigr).

The technical advantage of the
\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\cdot]
-operation is now also clear: concentration with respect to total variation would be blatantly false without this operation.
Indeed, it holds that
\|\hat{P}_{S}-P_{S}\|_{{\rm{TV}}}=1
with probability one since
\hat{P}_{S}
is finitely supported and
P_{S}
assigns no mass to finite sets.

Combining
Lemma

E.1
and
Lemma

E.2
with the triangle inequality now yields
Lemma

4.10
:

Proof of
Lemma

4.10
.

Using that
\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
is a metric,

(E.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{S},P_{s})\leq\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{S},\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\hat{P}_{S}])+\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\hat{P}_{S}],\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[P_{S}])+\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[P_{S}],P_{S}).

The first and final term can here be controlled by choosing
\mathfrak{d}
appropriately in
Lemma

E.1
:

(E.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{S},\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\hat{P}_{S}])+\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[P_{S}],P_{S})\leq\beta\sqrt{t}/2\ \ \text{ if }\ \ \sqrt{2}\mathfrak{d}=\beta\sqrt{t}/4.

Further, recall the assumption that
\mathfrak{u}\leq\alpha\sqrt{t}
in
Lemma

4.10
.
Hence, using (
E.6
) with
\gamma=\beta/(4\sqrt{t})
and
\eta=\beta/(2\alpha)
, it holds for
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
that

(E.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\hat{P}_{S}],\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[P_{S}])>\beta\sqrt{t}/2\bigr)\leq c_{2}\exp\bigl(-c_{3}\pi(S)T/t_{\rm{mix}}\bigr)

where
c_{1},c_{2}
, and
c_{3}
only depend on
\alpha
and
\beta
.
Combine (
E.7
)–(
E.9
) and note that
\pi(S)\geq\pi_{\min}\operatorname{Area}(S)
by (
2.5
) to conclude the proof.
∎

E.1.
Proof of
Lemma

E.2

Throughout this section, we assume that
X_{0}
starts from stationarity and let
S\subseteq D
be a fixed subset with nonzero volume.

Lemma E.3
.

Consider an integer
n\geq 1
with
nt\geq t_{\rm{mix}}
and a measurable set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Denote
N(E)\vcentcolon=\#\{0\leq i\leq n-1:X_{it}\in S,X_{(i+1)t}\in E\}
.
Then, for every
\zeta\leq 1
,

(E.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert N(E)-n\pi(S)P_{S}(E)\rvert>n\zeta\pi(S)\bigr)\leq 2\exp\bigl(-c_{S}n\zeta^{2}\bigr)

where
c_{S}\vcentcolon=\pi(S)/(90\lceil t_{\rm{mix}}/t\rceil)
.

Proof.

The quantity of interest can be represented as

N(E)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathbbm{1}\{X_{it}\in S,X_{(i+1)t}E\}.

This brings us to a setting where general-purpose concentration inequalities such as
[
44
, Theorem 3.4]
7
7

7

For readers consulting the arXiv version (i.e., arXiv:1212.2015v5), let us note that the numbering is there different from the published version.
The cited result is there Theorem 3.11.
are applicable.
That result uses some parameters which we next estimate.

The mixing properties of the chain are quantified in
[
44
]
by the
pseudo-spectral gap

\gamma_{\operatorname{ps}}
.
For our purposes, a bound on this quantity will be sufficient.
Denote
i_{\rm{mix}}
for the mixing time of the discrete-time Markov chain
(X_{it})_{i=0}^{\infty}
:

(E.11)

\displaystyle i_{\rm{mix}}\vcentcolon=\min\{i\geq 1:\lvert\mathbb{P}(X_{it}\in A\mid X_{0}=x_{0})-\pi(E)\rvert\leq 1/4,\ \forall x_{0}\in D,\,A\subseteq D\}

where it is to be understood that
A
runs only over measurable subsets of
D
.
Then, by
[
44
, Proposition 3.4]
, we have
1/\gamma_{\operatorname{ps}}\leq 2i_{\rm{mix}}
.

Since the chain is assumed to start from its stationary distribution, the expectation and variance of the terms is fairly explicit:

(E.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}\{X_{it}\in S,X_{(i+1)t}\in E\}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}\{X_{it}\in S,X_{(i+1)t}\in E\}\bigl)=\pi(S)P_{S}(E),

(E.13)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}\{X_{it}\in S,X_{(i+1)t}\in E\}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl(1-\pi(S)P_{S}(E)\bigr)\pi(S)P_{S}(E)\leq\pi(S)P_{S}(E).

Further, since the terms are
\{0,1\}
-valued,

(E.14)

\displaystyle\bigl\lvert\mathbbm{1}\{X_{it}\in S,X_{(i+1)t}\in E\}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}\{X_{it}\in S,X_{(i+1)t}\in E\}]\bigr\rvert\leq 1.

Now, by
[
44
, Theorem 3.4]
,
for every
x>0
,

(E.15)
