\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert N(E)-n\pi(S)P_{S}(E)\rvert>x\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-\frac{x^{2}/(2i_{\rm{mix}})}{8(n+2i_{\rm{mix}})\pi(S)P_{S}(E)+20x}\Bigr).

Here, comparing (
E.11
) with (
2.6
), we have
i_{\rm{mix}}\leq\lceil t_{\rm{mix}}/t\rceil
.
Consequently, by the assumption that
nt\geq t_{\rm{mix}}
, we have
8(n+2i_{\rm{mix}})\leq 24(n-1)
.
Now, taking
x=\zeta n\pi(S)
,

(E.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert N(E)-n\pi(S)

\displaystyle P_{S}(E)\rvert>\zeta n\pi(S)\bigr)\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\zeta^{2}n^{2}\pi(S)^{2}/(2\lceil t_{\rm{mix}}/t\rceil)}{24n\pi(S)P_{S}(E)+20\zeta n\pi(S)}\Bigr).

Simplify (
E.16
) using that
24P_{S}(E)+20\zeta\leq 45
to conclude the proof.
∎

Recall the estimator
\hat{P}_{S}
from (
4.2
).
Then, we have the following concentration inequality:

Corollary E.4
.

There exist absolute constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that the following holds for every
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
,
T>0
, measurable
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
\zeta\leq 1
,

(E.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert\hat{P}_{S}(E)-P_{S}(E)\rvert>\zeta\bigr)\leq 4\exp\bigl(-c_{2}\pi(S)T\zeta^{2}/t_{\rm{mix}}\bigr)

Proof.

Note that
\pi(S)\zeta^{2}\leq 1
.
Hence, taking
c_{2}
sufficiently small to ensure that the bound is trivial otherwise, we may assume that
T\geq 2t_{\rm{mix}}
.
Further, it is not difficult to deduce from
Lemma

3.5
that
t_{\rm{mix}}\geq c\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
for some
c>0
.
Hence, taking
c_{1}
sufficiently small, we may assume that
t\leq t_{\rm{mix}}
.

The preceding discussion implies that
\lfloor T/t\rfloor\geq T/2t\geq 1
and
\lfloor T/t\rfloor t\geq t_{\rm{mix}}
.
Hence, by
Lemma

E.3
with
E=D
and
n=\lfloor T/t\rfloor
, also using that
P_{S}(D)=1
since
X_{t}
is
D
-valued,

(E.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert\#\{0\leq i\leq\lfloor T/t\rfloor-1:X_{it}\in S\}-\lfloor T/t\rfloor\pi(S)\rvert>\lfloor{}

\displaystyle{}T/t\rfloor\pi(S)\zeta/2\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\bigl(-c^{\prime}\pi(S)\lfloor T/t\rfloor\zeta^{2}/\lceil t_{\rm{mix}}/t\rceil\bigr)

for some absolute constant
c^{\prime}>0
.
Hence, using that
\lceil t_{\rm{mix}}/t\rceil\leq 2t_{\rm{mix}}/t
since
t\leq t_{\rm{mix}}
and recalling that
\lfloor T/t\rfloor\geq T/2t
, also rewriting the event on the left-hand side of (
E.18
),

(E.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\Bigl\lvert\frac{\#\{i\leq\lfloor T/t\rfloor-1:X_{it}\in S\}}{\lfloor T/t\rfloor\pi(S)}-1\Bigr\rvert>\frac{\zeta}{2}\Bigr)\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-c^{\prime\prime}\pi(S)T\zeta^{2}/t_{\rm{mix}}\Bigr)

with
c^{\prime\prime}\vcentcolon=c^{\prime}/4
.
Similarly, by another application of
Lemma

E.3
, using the definition of
\hat{P}_{S}
,

(E.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\Bigl\lvert\frac{\#\{i\leq\lfloor T/t\rfloor-1:X_{it}\in S\}}{\lfloor T/t\rfloor\pi(S)}\hat{P}_{S}(E)-P_{S}(E)\Bigr\rvert>\frac{\zeta}{2}\Bigr)\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-c^{\prime\prime}\pi(S)T\zeta^{2}/t_{\rm{mix}}\Bigr).

Combine (
E.19
) and (
E.20
) using that
\hat{P}_{S}(E)\leq 1
to conclude that (
E.17
) holds.
∎

Recall from the discussion preceding
Lemma

E.1
that
\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\hat{P}_{S}]
was defined by averaging over the bins
B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)
of the grid
\mathfrak{d}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
The idea is now to apply
Corollary

E.4
with
E=B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)
.
However, it is too inefficient use a union bound over all (infinitely many) such sets.
To reduce the combinatorial costs, the following result will be used:

Lemma E.5
.

For every
\eta\in(0,1)
and
\zeta>0
there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending only on
\eta
and
\gamma
such that the following holds for every
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
if
\mathfrak{d}\vcentcolon=\gamma\sqrt{t}
.

Assume that
\|a-b\|\leq\sqrt{t}
for every
a,b\in S
.
Then, there exists a set of gridpoints
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathfrak{d}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with cardinality
\#\mathcal{G}\leq c_{2}
such that

(E.21)

\displaystyle P_{S}\Bigl(\cup_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}}B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\Bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

Proof.

It follows from
Corollary

3.11
that there exist constants
c_{1},C_{1}>0
depending only on
\eta
such that for every
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
and every
x_{0}\in D

(E.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|X_{t}-x_{0}\|\leq C_{1}\sqrt{t}\mid X_{0}=x_{0}\bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

In view of this, let us define

(E.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\vcentcolon=\bigl\{(x,y)\in\mathfrak{d}\mathbb{Z}^{2}:B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},C_{1}\sqrt{t})\neq\emptyset\text{ for some }x_{0}\in S\bigr\}.

Then, recalling the definition of
P_{S}
from (
E.1
) and using the law of total probability,

(E.24)

\displaystyle P_{S}\Bigl(\cup_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}}B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{x_{0}\in S}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(X_{t}\in\cup_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}}B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\mid X_{0}=x_{0}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|X_{t}-x_{0}\|\leq C_{1}\sqrt{t}\mid X_{0}=x_{0}\bigr).

Combine (
E.22
) and (
E.24
) to find (
E.21
).

It remains to show that
\#\mathcal{G}\leq c_{2}
with
c_{2}
only depending on
\eta
and
\gamma
.
Fix some arbitrary
a_{0}\in S
.
Then, by the assumption that
\|x_{0}-a_{0}\|\leq\sqrt{t}
for every
x_{0}\in S
and
\mathfrak{d}=\gamma\sqrt{t}
, any point
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
for which
B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)
intersects
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},C_{1}\sqrt{t})
for some
x_{0}\in S
must satisfy that
\|(x,y)-a_{0}\|\leq(C_{1}+\sqrt{2}\gamma+1)\sqrt{t}
.
Rescaling by
\sqrt{t}
, it follows that
\#\mathcal{G}
is bounded by the number of points in
\gamma\mathbb{Z}^{2}
contained in a ball of radius
C_{1}+\sqrt{2}\gamma+1
.
Since
C_{1}
only depends on
\eta
, the resulting upper bound only depends on
\gamma
and
\eta
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

E.2
.

Using that total variation distance is half of the
L^{1}
distance
[
37
, Proposition 4.2]
together with the definition of the
\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\cdot]
-operation,

(E.25)

\displaystyle\bigl\|\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[\hat{P}_{S}]-\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[P_{S}]\bigr\|_{{\rm{TV}}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x,y\in\mathfrak{d}\mathbb{Z}}\bigl\lvert\hat{P}_{S}\bigl(B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\bigr)-P_{S}\bigl(B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\bigr)\bigr\rvert.

We next rewrite the right-hand side of (
E.25
) to prepare for an application of
Corollary

E.4
.

Lemma

E.5
provides constants
c_{1}>0
and
c_{2}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$1$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}>0
depending only on
\eta
and
\gamma
such that there exists a set of gridpoints
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathfrak{d}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
\#\mathcal{G}\leq c_{2}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$1$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
and

(E.26)

\displaystyle P_{S}\bigl(E_{\mathcal{G}}\bigr)<\eta/2\quad\text{ where }\quad E_{\mathcal{G}}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\cup_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}}B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)

whenever
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
.
Now, by (
E.25
) together with (
E.26
) and two application of the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\bigl\|\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[{}

\displaystyle{}\hat{P}_{S}]-\operatorname{bin}_{\mathfrak{d}}[P_{S}]\bigr\|_{{\rm{TV}}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}}\bigl\lvert\hat{P}_{S}\bigl(B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\bigr)-P_{S}\bigl(B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\bigr)\bigr\rvert+\frac{1}{2}\hat{P}_{S}(E_{\mathcal{G}})+\frac{1}{2}P_{S}(E_{\mathcal{G}})

(E.27)

\displaystyle\ \leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}}\bigl\lvert\hat{P}_{S}\bigl(B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\bigr)-P_{S}\bigl(B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y)\bigr)\bigr\rvert+\frac{1}{2}\lvert\hat{P}_{S}(E_{\mathcal{G}})-P_{S}(E_{\mathcal{G}})\rvert+\frac{\eta}{2}.

Let
E\in\{B_{\mathfrak{d}}(x,y):(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}\}\cup\{E_{\mathcal{G}}\}
be one of the measurable sets which occurs on the right-hand side of (
E.27
).
Then, by
Corollary

E.4
there exists some
c_{3}>0
depending only on
\eta
and
\gamma
such that,

(E.28)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\bigl\lvert\hat{P}_{S}\bigl(E\bigr)-P_{S}\bigl(E\bigr)\bigr\rvert>\frac{\eta}{c_{2}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$1$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}+1}\Bigr)\leq 4\exp\Bigl(-c_{3}\pi(S)T/t_{\rm{mix}}\Bigr).

Let
c_{2}=4(c_{2}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$1$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}+1)
and apply the union bound to conclude that (
E.5
) holds.

Finally, (
E.6
) follows from (
E.5
).
Indeed, note that
d_{\mathfrak{u}}(x,y)\leq\mathfrak{u}
for every
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
This implies that
\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\mu,\nu)\leq\mathfrak{u}\|\mu-\nu\|_{{\rm{TV}}}
for all probability measures
\mu,\nu
; see
[
55
, p.103]
8
8

8

Note that the definition of total variation distance in
[
55
]
differs by a factor two from (
E.4
).
.
∎

Appendix F
Proof of
Proposition

4.1

The following preparatory lemma follows by approximating the barrier with straight lines using
Lemma

3.5
; see
Section

F.1
for the details.

Lemma F.1
.

There exists an absolute constant
c>0
such that the following holds whenever the discretization scale in
Algorithm

1
satisfies
\epsilon\leq c\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}
.

Fix some
i\leq{m}
and integers
j,k
with
\mathcal{S}(j,k)\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
Then, there exist
-2\leq h_{j}^{+},h_{k}^{+}\leq 2
such that every point in
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})
is on the positive side of
B_{i}
.
Moreover, one can ensure that
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})
does not intersect
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.

Similarly, there exist
-2\leq h_{j}^{-},h_{k}^{-}\leq 2
such that every point in
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{-},k+h_{k}^{-})
is on the negative side of
B_{i}
, and such that
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{-},k+h_{k}^{-})
does not intersect
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.

Further, the following combines
Lemma

4.10
with the union bound; see
Section

F.2
.

Lemma F.2
.

For every
\alpha,\beta>0
there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
depending only on
\alpha
and
\beta
such that the following holds for every
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
,
\varepsilon\leq c_{2}\sqrt{t}
,
\mathfrak{u}\leq\alpha\sqrt{t}
, and
\eta\in(0,1)
.

Assume that
X_{0}\sim\pi
starts in stationarity and that the observation time
T
satisfies (
4.3
) with respect to
c_{3}
.
Then, if the discretization scale in
Algorithm

1
satisfies
\epsilon=\varepsilon/(3\sqrt{2})
,

(F.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)},P_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)})\leq\beta\sqrt{t}\text{ for all integers }j,k\text{ with }\mathcal{S}(j,k)\subseteq D\bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

Proposition

4.1
now follows by a direct computation with the results from
Section

4.1
:

Proof of
Proposition

4.1
.

To avoid notational ambiguity with other parts of the proof, let us denote
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{5}>0
for the constants in
Proposition

4.1
.
Then,

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\},\qquad\varepsilon\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\sqrt{t},\ \qquad T\geq\mathcal{C}_{3}\frac{t_{\rm{mix}}}{\pi_{\min}\varepsilon^{2}}\ln\Big(\frac{1}{\eta}\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Big),

(F.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{4}\sqrt{t},\hphantom{\qquad\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda^{2},\rho^{2}\}}\epsilon=\varepsilon/(3\sqrt{2}),\,\quad\mathfrak{u}=\mathcal{C}_{5}\sqrt{t}.

The values of
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and
\mathcal{C}_{5}
will soon be fixed.
Subsequently, we show that the result follows if
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are taken sufficiently small, and
\mathcal{C}_{3}
sufficiently large.

Recall the definition of
P_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)}
from
Lemma

4.10
and let it be understood that
P_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)}
is the zero measure if
\mathcal{S}(j,k)\cap D=\emptyset
.
Then, if
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is sufficiently small and
\mathcal{C}_{5}
is sufficiently large,
Corollary

4.9
provides an absolute constant
c>0
such that

(F.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(P_{\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})},P_{\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{-},k+h_{k}^{-})})\geq c\sqrt{t}

when all points in
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})
are on the positive side of some barrier
B_{i}
and all points of
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{-},k+h_{k}^{-})
are on the negative side of
B_{i}
.
From here on, we fix
\mathcal{C}_{5}
and let
\mathcal{C}_{4}\vcentcolon=c/3
.

For every
j,k
with
\mathcal{S}(j,k)\cap(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})=\emptyset
we either have
\mathcal{S}(j,k)\subseteq D
or
\mathcal{S}(j,k)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus D
.
In the second case,
\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)}
and
P_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)}
are both equal to the zero measure.
In the first case,
Lemma

F.2
is applicable.
Hence, it holds with probability
\geq 1-\eta
that

(F.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)},P_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)})\leq\frac{c}{100}\sqrt{t}\ \text{ for all integers }\ j,k\ \text{ with }\ \mathcal{S}(j,k)\cap(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})=\emptyset

whenever
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are sufficiently small and
\mathcal{C}_{3}
is sufficiently large.
From here on, we assume that the event in (
F.4
) occurs.
We will prove that the event implied in (
2.9
) follows.

Consider some
j,k
such that there exists some point in
\mathcal{S}(j,k)
at distance
>\varepsilon
from every point in
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
.
Pythagoras’ theorem implies that
\|x_{0}-\tilde{x}_{0}\|\leq 3\sqrt{2}\epsilon=\varepsilon
for every
x_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(j,k)
and
\tilde{x}_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j},k+h_{k})
with
\lvert h_{j}\rvert,\lvert h_{k}\rvert\leq 2
.
Hence, the square region
\cup_{-2\leq h_{j},h_{k}\leq 2}\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j},k+h_{k})
does not intersect
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
, implying that all points in this region are on the same side of every barrier.
Hence, by
Proposition

4.6
with
\zeta=c/(100(1+\mathcal{C}_{5}))
, also using (
F.4
) and the triangle inequality,

(F.5)

\displaystyle\max\bigl\{\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}\bigl(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)},\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j},k+h_{k})}\bigr):\lvert h_{j}\rvert,\lvert h_{k}\rvert\leq 2\bigr\}\leq 2\frac{c}{100}+\zeta(\sqrt{t}+\mathfrak{u})\leq\frac{3c}{100}\sqrt{t}

if
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are sufficiently small such that
Proposition

4.6
is applicable.

Recalling that we took
\mathcal{C}_{4}=c/3
and considering (
F.2
), the right-hand side of (
F.5
) does not exceed the sensitivity threshold
\mathfrak{s}
in
Algorithm

1
.
This shows that no region
\mathcal{S}(j,k)
which contains a point at distance
>\varepsilon
from
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
is added to
\hat{B}
by the algorithm.
Hence,

(F.6)

\displaystyle\sup_{a\in\hat{B}}\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{b\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}}\|a-b\|\leq\varepsilon.

In the other direction, consider
j,k
with
\mathcal{S}(j,k)\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
Then, if
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are sufficiently small,
Lemma

F.1
allows us to find
\lvert h_{j}^{+}\rvert,\lvert h_{k}^{+}\rvert\leq 2
with
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})
on the positive side of
B_{i}
not intersecting
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.
Similarly, we can find
\lvert h_{j}^{-}\rvert,\lvert h_{k}^{-}\rvert\leq 2
with
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{-},k+h_{k}^{-})
on the negative side of
B_{i}
not intersecting
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.
Then, by (
F.3
) and (
F.4
),

(F.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})},\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{-},k+h_{k}^{-})})\geq\Bigl(c-\frac{2c}{100}\Bigr)\sqrt{t}.

In particular, since the triangle inequality would be violated otherwise,

(F.8)

\displaystyle\max\bigl\{\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}\bigl(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)},\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j},k+h_{k})}\bigr):\lvert h_{j}\rvert,\lvert h_{k}\rvert\leq 2\Bigr\}\geq\Bigl(\frac{c}{2}-\frac{c}{100}\Bigr)\sqrt{t}

This exceeds the sensitivity threshold
\mathfrak{s}
in
Algorithm

1
, and hence every
\mathcal{S}(j,k)
intersecting
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
is added to
\hat{B}
by the algorithm.
Then,
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}\subseteq\hat{B}
and hence

(F.9)

\displaystyle\sup_{b\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}}\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{a\in\hat{B}}\|a-b\|=0.

Combine (
F.6
) with (
F.9
) and recall the definition of the Hausdorff metric from (
2.7
) to conclude the proof.
∎

F.1.
Proof of
Lemma

F.1

We start with a preparatory result:

Lemma F.3
.

There exists an absolute constant
c>0
such that the following holds.
Consider a point
x\in B_{i}
for some
i\leq{m}
and some
\epsilon\leq c\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}
.

Then, there exists
\xi^{+}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\|x-\xi^{+}\|\leq 2\epsilon
such that every point
z
with
\|z-\xi^{+}\|\leq\sqrt{2}\epsilon
is on the positive side of
B_{i}
.
Moreover, it may be assumed that any such
z
is not in
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.

Similarly, there exists
\xi^{-}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\|x-\xi^{-}\|\leq 2\epsilon
such that every point
z
with
\|z-\xi^{-}\|\leq\sqrt{2}\epsilon
is on the negative side of
B_{i}
, and it may be assumed that any such
z
is not in
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.

Proof.

Let
\delta\vcentcolon=4\epsilon/\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}
and let
\hat{n}
be the vector which arises from
Lemma

3.5
applied to
\mathscr{B}(x,r_{\delta})
.
We claim that the following points have the desired properties:

(F.10)

\displaystyle\xi^{+}\vcentcolon=x+2\epsilon\hat{n}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\xi^{-}\vcentcolon=x-2\epsilon\hat{n}.

Let us verify this for
\xi^{+}
.
The proof for
\xi^{-}
proceeds similarly.

That
\|\xi^{+}-x\|\leq 2\epsilon
is immediate from the fact that
\hat{n}
is a unit vector, so it remains to show that every
z
with
\|z-\xi^{+}\|\leq\sqrt{2}\epsilon
is on the positive side of
B_{i}
and not in
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.
By the triangle inequality, every such
z
satisfies

(F.11)

\displaystyle\|z-x\|\leq(2+\sqrt{2})\epsilon<4\epsilon=r_{\delta}

which is to say that
z\in\mathscr{B}(x,r_{\delta})
.

Let
\mathfrak{c}
be as in
Lemma

3.5
.
We claim that it suffices to show that
\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle\geq\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}
.
Indeed,
item

(1)
from
Lemma

3.5
then implies that
z\not\in B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x,r_{\delta})
which implies that
z\not\in\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
since
z\in\mathscr{B}(x,r_{\delta})
.
Further,
item

(3)
from
Lemma

3.5
then implies that
z
is not on the negative side of
B_{i}
, which implies that
z
is on the positive side.

Using bilinearity of the inner product as well as
item

(1)
from
Lemma

3.5
,

(F.12)

\displaystyle\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle\geq\langle\xi^{+},\hat{n}\rangle-\sqrt{2}\epsilon=\langle x,\hat{n}\rangle+(2-\sqrt{2})\epsilon\geq\mathfrak{c}+(2-\sqrt{2})\epsilon-4\delta r_{\delta}.

The assumed upper bound on
\epsilon
and the definition of
\delta
imply that
\delta\leq 4c
and
r_{\delta}=4\epsilon
.
Hence, it can be ensured that

(2-\sqrt{2})\epsilon-4\delta r_{\delta}\geq 4\delta r_{\delta}
by taking
c
to be sufficiently small.
This yields the desired sufficient condition and consequently concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

F.1
.

Let
c
be as in
Lemma

F.3
.
We prove the existence of
-2\leq h_{j}^{+},h_{k}^{+}\leq 2
with
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})
on the positive side of
B_{i}
; the existence of a box on the negative side proceeds similarly.

By assumption, there exists some
x\in\mathcal{S}(j,k)
with
x\in B_{i}
.
Let
\xi^{+}
be as in
Lemma

F.3
.
Then, in particular,
\|x-\xi^{+}\|\leq 2\epsilon
.
This implies that there exist
-2\leq h_{j}^{+},h_{k}^{+}\leq 2
, not necessarily unique if
\xi^{+}
is on the boundary between two squares, with
\xi^{+}\in\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})
.

Recall that
\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})
is an
\epsilon\times\epsilon
box.
Hence, every
z\in\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})
satisfies
\|\xi^{+}-z\|\leq\sqrt{2}\epsilon
.
The desired properties that every
z\in\mathcal{S}(j+h_{j}^{+},k+h_{k}^{+})
is on the positive side and not on any barrier now follow from the conclusion of
Lemma

F.3
.
∎

F.2.
Proof of
Lemma

F.2

Proof of
Lemma

F.2
.

Each of the regions
\mathcal{S}(j,k)
is an
\epsilon\times\epsilon
square and consequently satisfies
\operatorname{Area}(\mathcal{S}(j,k))=\epsilon^{2}
.
It follows that the number of integers
j,k
with
\mathcal{S}(j,k)\subseteq D
is at most
\operatorname{Area}(D)/\epsilon^{2}
.
Hence, combining
Lemma

4.10
with the union bound,

(F.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)},P_{\mathcal{S}(j,k)})\leq\beta\sqrt{t}{}

\displaystyle{}\text{ for all integers }j,k\text{ with }\mathcal{S}(j,k)\subseteq D\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\geq 1-C\bigl(\operatorname{Area}(D)/\epsilon^{2}\bigr)\exp\bigl(-c\pi_{\min}\epsilon^{2}T/t_{\rm{mix}}\bigr)

with
c,C>0
constants depending only on
\alpha
and
\beta
.
We next use the assumed lower bound on
T
from (
4.3
) to estimate the right-hand side of (
F.13
).

Recall from the discussion preceding (
C.4
) that
\operatorname{Area}(D)\geq\pi/\kappa^{2}
due to the assumption that the curvature of
B_{0}
is bounded by
\kappa
.
In particular, taking
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
sufficiently small, we may assume that
\operatorname{Area}(D)\geq 2\varepsilon^{2}\geq 2\varepsilon^{2}\eta
.
Then, using that
\epsilon=\varepsilon/(3\sqrt{2})
,

(F.14)

\displaystyle\ln\Bigl(\frac{C\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\epsilon^{2}\eta}\Bigr)=\ln\bigl(C(3\sqrt{2})^{2}\bigr)+\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\Bigr)\leq C^{\prime}\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\Bigr)

for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
which should be sufficiently large to satisfy
\ln(C(3\sqrt{2})^{2})\leq(C^{\prime}-1)\ln(2)
.
Hence, for
c_{3}
sufficiently large,

(F.15)

\displaystyle c_{3}\frac{1}{\pi_{\min}\varepsilon^{2}}\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\Bigr)\geq\frac{1}{c\pi_{\min}\epsilon^{2}}\ln\Bigl(C\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\epsilon^{2}\eta}\Bigr).

Combine (
F.13
) and (
F.15
) with the assumption (
4.3
) to find (
F.1
).
∎

Appendix G
Proof of
Proposition

4.2

The following two preparatory lemmas state the direction of the barriers is estimated sufficiently well by
Algorithm

2
to ensure that the rectangular regions
\{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h):\lvert h\rvert\leq 2\}
are all on a fixed side of the barriers if and only if
p_{j,k}
is distant from all barriers:

Lemma G.1
.

There exists an absolute constant
c_{1}>0
such that the following holds.
Assume that the parameters in
Algorithm

2
satisfy the following for some
\mathscr{E}\geq 0
:

(G.1)

\displaystyle\ell\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\},\ \epsilon=\kappa\ell^{2},\ \text{ and }\ d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\mathscr{E}.

Then, for every
y\in B_{i}
there exists some
0\leq{n}\leq\lfloor 2\pi\ell/\epsilon\rfloor
as well as integers
j,k
such that the following properties are satisfied:

(1)

The point
p_{j,k}
has distance
\leq\epsilon/\sqrt{2}
from
y
.
Further, the points
p_{j,k}+\ell\vec{w}_{n}
and
p_{j,k}-\ell\vec{w}_{n}
both have distance
\leq\mathscr{E}+2\epsilon
from
\mathfrak{B}
.

(2)

There exists
-2\leq h_{+}\leq 2
such that every point in
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h_{+})
is on the positive side of
B_{i}
, and such that
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h_{+})
does not intersect
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.

(3)

Similarly, there exists
-2\leq h_{-}\leq 2
such that every point in
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h_{-})
is on the negative side of
B_{i}
, and such that
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h_{-})
does not intersect
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.

Lemma G.2
.

There exist absolute constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
such that the following holds.
Assume that the parameters in
Algorithm

2
satisfy (
G.1
) with respect to
c_{1}
and assume that
c_{2}\epsilon\leq\mathscr{E}\leq c_{3}\ell
.
Further, consider integers
j,k
with
\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\|p_{j,k}-y\|:y\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}\}>\mathscr{E}/2
.

Then, for every
0\leq{n}\leq\lfloor 2\pi\ell/\epsilon\rfloor
with
p_{j,k}+\ell\vec{w}_{n}
and
p_{j,k}-\ell\vec{w}_{n}
both at distance
\leq\mathscr{E}+2\epsilon
from
\mathfrak{B}
it holds that the rectangular region
\cup_{h=-2}^{2}\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)
does not intersect
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
.

Proofs are given in
Sections

G.2
and
G.3
.
The following estimate will play a similar role in the proof of
Proposition

4.2
as
Lemma

F.2
did in the proof of
Proposition

4.1
:

Lemma G.3
.

For every
\alpha,\beta,\gamma>0
there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
depending only on
\alpha,\beta
and
\gamma
such that the following holds for every
t\leq c_{1}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
,
\varepsilon\leq c_{2}\kappa t
,
\mathfrak{u}\leq\alpha\sqrt{t}
, and
\eta\in(0,1)
.

Assume that
X_{0}\sim\pi
and that (
2.8
) holds with respect to
c_{3}
.
Then, if the parameters in
Algorithm

2
satisfy
\ell=\gamma\sqrt{\varepsilon/\kappa}
and
\epsilon=\kappa\ell^{2}
,

(G.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)},P_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)})\leq\beta\sqrt{t},\ \forall j,k,{n},h\text{ with }\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\subseteq D\bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

The proof is given in
Section

G.4
.

Proposition

4.2
now follows by combining these preliminaries with the results from
Section

4.1
.
This proceeds almost exactly like the proof of
Proposition

4.1
, but let us give the details for completeness:

Proof of
Proposition

4.2
.

To avoid ambiguity with other results, let us denote
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{7}>0
for the constants in the statement of
Proposition

4.2
.
Then, the assumption is that

\displaystyle t\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}{}

\displaystyle{}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\},\quad\varepsilon\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\kappa t,\quad T\geq\mathcal{C}_{3}\frac{t_{\rm{mix}}}{\pi_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr),

(G.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}=\mathcal{C}_{4}\sqrt{t},\quad\ell=\mathcal{C}_{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon/\kappa},\quad\epsilon=\kappa\ell^{2},\quad\mathfrak{u}=\mathcal{C}_{6}\sqrt{t},\ \text{ and }\ \varepsilon\leq\mathscr{E}\leq\mathcal{C}_{7}\ell.

The values of
\mathcal{C}_{4},\mathcal{C}_{5},
and
\mathcal{C}_{6}
will soon be fixed.
Subsequently, we show that the result follows if
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{7}
are taken sufficiently small, and
\mathcal{C}_{3}
is taken sufficiently large.

Recall from
Lemma

4.10
that
P_{S}=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot\mid X_{0}\in S)
for
S\subseteq D
and
X_{0}\sim\pi
.

Corollary

4.9
hence ensures that there is an absolute constant
c>0
such that, when
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is sufficiently small and
\mathcal{C}_{6}
is sufficiently large,

(G.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(P_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h^{+})},P_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h^{-})})\geq c\sqrt{t}

when all points of
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h^{+})
are on the positive side of some barrier
B_{i}
, and all points of
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h^{-})
are on the negative side of
B_{i}
.
From here on, fix
\mathcal{C}_{6}
and let
\mathcal{C}_{4}\vcentcolon=c/3
.

Let us take
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
sufficiently small so that they are bounded by an absolute constant, say
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\leq 1
.
Then, combining the inequalities in (
G.3
) we have that
\ell\leq\mathcal{C}_{5}\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}
.
Further, note that
\mathscr{E}\geq\varepsilon=\epsilon/\mathcal{C}_{5}^{2}
.
Let us fix
\mathcal{C}_{5}
at a value which is
\leq 1/\sqrt{2}
and sufficiently small to ensure that the upper bounds on
\ell
in
Lemma

G.1
and
Lemma

G.2
are satisfied, and that the lower bound on
\mathscr{E}
in
Lemma

G.2
is satisfied.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event where concentration occurs:

(G.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\vcentcolon=\{\omega:\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)},P_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)})\leq\frac{c}{100}\sqrt{t},\,\forall j,k,{n},h\text{ with }\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\subseteq D\}.

Then, considering that
\hat{P}_{S}
and
P_{S}
are both equal to the zero measure when
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus D
,

(G.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)},P_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)})\leq\frac{c}{100}\sqrt{t},\,\forall j,k,{n},h\text{ with }\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\cap(\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})=\emptyset

whenever
\mathcal{E}
occurs.

Lemma

G.3
implies that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\geq 1-\eta
if the constants
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are taken sufficiently small, and the constant
\mathcal{C}_{3}
is taken sufficiently large.
From here on out, we work on the event where
\mathcal{E}
occurs.
It remains to show that the output of
Algorithm

2
then satisfies
d_{\rm{H}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\mathscr{E}/2
for every
\mathscr{E}>0
and
\mathfrak{B}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\mathscr{E}
subject to the constraints in (
G.3
) and
\mathfrak{B}
satisfying
d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\mathscr{E}
.

Consider some
p_{j,k}
at distance
>\mathscr{E}/2
from
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
.
Then, taking
\mathcal{C}_{7}
sufficiently small and recalling the previous discussion regarding the choice of
\mathcal{C}_{5}
ensures that the conditions of
Lemma

G.2
are satisfied.
Hence,
\cup_{h=-2}^{2}\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)
does not intersect
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
for every
0\leq{n}\leq\lfloor 2\pi\ell/\epsilon\rfloor
with
p_{j,k}+\ell\vec{w}_{n}
and
p_{j,k}-\ell\vec{w}_{n}
sufficiently close to
\mathfrak{B}
to satisfy the constraint in
Algorithm

2
.
In particular, the rectangular sets
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)
for varying
\lvert h\rvert\leq 2
are then all on the same side of every barrier.
Also note that the diameter of
\cup_{h=-2}^{2}\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)
can be made an arbitrarily small multiple of
\sqrt{t}
by taking
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
sufficiently small in (
G.3
).

The preceding implies that the conditions of
Proposition

4.6
with
\zeta=(c/100)/(\mathcal{C}_{6}+1)
can be satisfied by taking
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
and
\mathcal{C}_{7}
sufficiently small.
Combining the conclusion (
4.5
) with (
G.6
) and the triangle inequality,

(G.7)

\displaystyle\max\bigl\{\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},0)}),\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)}:\lvert h\rvert\leq 2\bigr\}\leq\frac{3c}{100}\sqrt{t}.

Considering the definition of the sensitivity threshold
\mathfrak{s}
in (
G.3
) and recalling that we took
\mathcal{C}_{4}=c/3
, the right-hand side of (
G.7
) does not exceed the sensitivity threshold in
Algorithm

2
.
This shows that no point
p_{j,k}
at distance
>\mathscr{E}/2
from
\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
is added to
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
by the algorithm.
Hence,

(G.8)

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{j,k}\in\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}}\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{y\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}}\|p_{j,k}-y\|\leq\mathscr{E}/2.

In the other direction, consider some arbitrary point
y\in B_{i}
.
Then, recalling that
\mathcal{C}_{5}
was chosen such that
\ell
satisfies the constraint in (
G.1
),
Lemma

G.1
ensures that there will be some
p_{j,k}\in\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
with
\|y-p_{j,k}\|\leq\epsilon/\sqrt{2}
and some
0\leq{n}\leq\lfloor 2\pi\ell/\epsilon\rfloor
such that
p_{j,k}+\ell\vec{w}_{n}
and
p_{j,k}-\ell\vec{w}_{n}
are sufficiently close to
\mathfrak{B}
to satisfy the constraint in
Algorithm

2
, and such that there exist
\lvert h_{+}\rvert,\lvert h_{-}\rvert\leq 2
with
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h_{+})
on the positive side of some barrier
B_{i}
, and
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h_{-})
on the negative side.
Then, combining (
G.4
) and (
G.6
),

(G.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h_{+})},\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h_{-})})\geq\Bigl(c-\frac{2c}{100}\Bigr)\sqrt{t}.

In particular, since the triangle inequality would be violated otherwise,

(G.10)

\displaystyle\max\bigl\{\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},0)},\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)}):\lvert h\rvert\leq 2\bigr\}\geq\Bigl(\frac{c}{2}-\frac{c}{100}\Bigr)\sqrt{t}.

This exceeds the sensitivity threshold implying that
p_{j,k}
will be added to
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
by
Algorithm

2
.

Recalling that we took
\mathcal{C}_{5}\leq 1/\sqrt{2}
, it follows from (
G.3
) that
\epsilon\leq\varepsilon/2
.
In particular, since
\|p_{j,k}-y\|\leq\epsilon/\sqrt{2}<\epsilon
, this implies that
\|p_{j,k}-y\|\leq\varepsilon/2
.
Recall that
\varepsilon\leq\mathscr{E}
from (
G.3
) to conclude that

(G.11)

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}}\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{p_{j,k}\in\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}}\|y-p_{j,k}\|\leq\mathscr{E}/2.

Combine (
G.8
) with (
G.11
) and recall the definition of the Hausdorff metric from (
2.7
) to conclude the proof.
∎

G.1.
Preliminary: a variant of
Lemma

3.5

Recall from
Remark

3.6
that
Lemma

3.5
admits a stronger variant which was inconvenient in the main text for notational reasons.
The variant will be convenient in the proofs of
Lemmas

G.1
and
G.2
.
Let us give a precise statement for easy reference.
Denote
r_{\delta}^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\min\{\delta/\kappa,\rho\}.

Lemma G.4
.

For every
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\delta>0
, there is at most one barrier
B_{i}
which intersects
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta}^{\prime})
.
Moreover, if such a
B_{i}
exists and
\delta<1/2
, then there exists a unit vector
\hat{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
depending on
x_{0}
and
B_{i}
such that the following properties hold:

(a)

There exists some
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\lvert\langle y,\hat{n}\rangle-\mathfrak{c}\rvert<4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}
for every
y\in B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta}^{\prime})
.

(b)

One has
\|\vec{n}_{i}(y)-\hat{n}\|<2\delta
for every
y\in B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta}^{\prime})
.

(c)

Every point
z\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta}^{\prime})
on the positive side of
B_{i}
satisfies
\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle>\mathfrak{c}-4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}
.
Similarly, every
z\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
the negative side of
B_{i}
satisfies
\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle<\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}
.

The proof is identical to that of
Lemma

3.5
; see
Appendix

B
.

G.2.
Proof of
Lemma

G.1

A direct computation shows that the properties claimed in
Lemma

G.1
hold if
p_{j,k}
and
\vec{w}_{n}
are chosen appropriately:

Lemma G.5
.

There exists an absolute constant
c_{1}>0
such that the following is satisfied.
Assume that (
G.1
) holds with respect to
c_{1}
.
Then, for every
y\in B_{i}
there exists a unit vector
\vec{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that the following properties are satisfied for every unit vector
\vec{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\|\vec{w}-\vec{W}\|\leq\epsilon/2\ell
and every point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\|p-y\|\leq\epsilon/\sqrt{2}
:

(1)

The points
p+\ell\vec{w}
and
p-\ell\vec{w}
have distance
\leq\mathscr{E}+2\epsilon
from
\mathfrak{B}
.

(2)

Let
\vec{w}^{\perp}
be a unit vector orthogonal to
\vec{w}
, and for every
-2\leq h\leq 2
define

(G.12)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(h)\vcentcolon=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\lvert\langle x-p,\vec{w}^{\perp}\rangle-h\epsilon\rvert\leq\epsilon/2,\lvert\langle x-p,\vec{w}\rangle\rvert\leq\ell/10\}.

Then, there is some
-2\leq h_{+}\leq 2
such that every point in
\mathfrak{R}(h_{+})
is on the positive side of
B_{i}
, and such that
\mathfrak{R}(h_{+})
does not intersect
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.

(3)

Similarly, there is some
-2\leq h_{-}\leq 2
such that every point in
\mathfrak{R}(h)
is on the negative side of
B_{i}
, and such that
\mathfrak{R}(h_{-})
does not intersect
\cup_{l=0}^{m}B_{l}
.

Proof.

Recall the assumption that
d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\mathscr{E}
from (
G.1
).

Item

(1)
hence follows if we prove that
p+\ell\vec{w}
and
p-\ell\vec{w}
have distance
\leq 2\epsilon
from
B_{i}
.
Let us show this for
p+\ell\vec{w}
, the proof for
p-\ell\vec{w}
proceeds similarly.

We proceed as in the proof of
Lemma

3.5
.
Pick an arc-length parametrization
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to B_{i}
with
\phi(0)=y
and let
\vec{W}\vcentcolon={\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)
.
In particular, it then holds that
\phi(\ell)\in B_{i}
, so it suffices to show that
\|(p+\ell\vec{w})-\phi(\ell)\|\leq 2\epsilon
.
By the triangle inequality and the assumed upper bounds on
\|p-y\|
and
\|\vec{w}-\vec{W}\|
,

(G.13)

\displaystyle\|(p+\ell\vec{w}\mkern 2.0mu)-\phi(\ell)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|p-\phi(0)\|+\|\ell\vec{w}-\ell\vec{W}\|+\|\phi(\ell)-\phi(0)-\ell\vec{W}\|

\displaystyle\leq(1/\sqrt{2}+1/2)\epsilon+\|\phi(\ell)-\phi(0)-\ell\vec{W}\|.

Recall from the discussion preceding (
B.1
) that
\|{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(t)-{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)\|\leq\kappa t
by the first Frenet–Serret formula.
Hence, also using that
\vec{W}={\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)
and that
\epsilon=\kappa\ell^{2}
by (
G.1
),

(G.14)

\displaystyle\|\phi(\ell)-\phi(0)-\ell\vec{W}\|\leq\int_{0}^{\ell}\|{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(t)-{\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)\|\,{\rm d}t\leq\kappa\ell^{2}/2=\epsilon/2.

Use that
1/\sqrt{2}+1/2+1/2<2
to conclude that
item

(1)
holds true.

We next prove
item

(2)
.
The idea is to rely on
Lemma

G.4
applied to a ball with a radius slightly greater than
\ell/10
, say a ball of
\ell/9
.
Let us start with a preparatory estimate to ensure that
\mathfrak{R}(h)\subseteq\mathscr{B}(y,\ell/9)
.
Fix some arbitrary
z\in\mathfrak{R}(h)
and
\lvert h\rvert\leq 2
.
Then, using the definition of
\mathfrak{R}(h)
with the assumed upper bound on
\|p-y\|
and the triangle inequality,

(G.15)

\displaystyle\|z-y\|\leq\|p-y\|+\|z-p\|\leq\epsilon/\sqrt{2}+(\lvert h\rvert+1/2)\epsilon+\ell/10\leq C\epsilon+\ell/10

with
C>0
an absolute constant.
Recall that
\epsilon=\kappa\ell^{2}
.
Hence, taking
c_{1}
in (
G.1
) to be sufficiently small so that
C\kappa\ell<1/9-1/10
, (
G.15
) yields
\mathfrak{R}(h)\subseteq\mathscr{B}(y,\ell/9)
, as desired.

Set
\delta\vcentcolon=\kappa\ell/9
.
Then, taking
c_{1}
in (
G.1
) sufficiently small ensures that
r_{\delta}^{\prime}=\min\{\delta/\kappa,\rho\}=\ell/9
and
\delta<1/2
.
Combining
\mathfrak{R}(h)\subseteq\mathscr{B}(y,\ell/9)
with
Lemma

G.4
, we certainly have
\mathfrak{R}(h)\cap B_{j}=\emptyset
for every
j\neq i
.

Item

(2)
hence follows if we ensure that one can pick
h_{+}
such that
\mathfrak{R}(h_{+})\cap B_{i}=\emptyset
and that every
z\in\mathfrak{R}(h_{+})
is on the positive side.
To show this, we exploit
item

(c)
from
Lemma

G.4
.

Assume that
\langle\vec{w}^{\perp},\vec{n}_{i}(y)\rangle\geq 0
; the other case follows similarly except that
h_{+}
should be replaced by
-h_{+}
.
We claim that
h_{+}=2
then does the job.
Pick some arbitrary
z\in\mathfrak{R}(2)
and let
\hat{n}
and
\mathfrak{c}
be as in
Lemma

G.4
applied to
\mathscr{B}(y,r_{\delta}^{\prime})
.
Then, by bilinearity,

(G.16)

\displaystyle\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle z-y,\vec{w}^{\perp}\rangle+\langle z-y,\hat{n}-\vec{w}^{\perp}\rangle+\langle y,\hat{n}\rangle.

Here, by the assumption that
\|p-y\|\leq\epsilon/\sqrt{2}
and the definition of
\mathfrak{R}(2)
,

(G.17)

\displaystyle\langle z-y,\vec{w}^{\perp}\rangle\geq\langle z-p,\vec{w}^{\perp}\rangle-\epsilon/\sqrt{2}\geq(2-1/\sqrt{2}-1/2)\epsilon.

Note that
\vec{W}={\textstyle{\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}}}\phi(0)
is the tangent vector to
B_{i}
at
y
and recall that
\langle\vec{w}^{\perp},\vec{n}_{i}(y)\rangle\geq 0
.
The assumption that
\|\vec{w}-\vec{W}\|\leq\epsilon/(2\ell)
then implies that also the normal vectors satisfy
\|\vec{w}^{\perp}-\vec{n}_{i}(y)\|\leq\epsilon/(2\ell)
.
Hence, since
\|\vec{n}_{i}(y)-\hat{n}\|<2\delta
by
item

(b)
from
Lemma

G.4
, we have
\|\vec{w}^{\perp}-\hat{n}\|\leq\epsilon/(2\ell)+2\delta
.
Further, note that
\mathfrak{R}(h)\subseteq\mathscr{B}(y,r_{\delta}^{\prime})
implies
\|z-y\|\leq r_{\delta}^{\prime}=\ell/9
.
Combining these estimates with the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

(G.18)

\displaystyle\lvert\langle z-y,\hat{n}-\vec{w}^{\perp}\rangle\rvert\leq(\epsilon/(2\ell)+2\delta)r_{\delta}^{\prime}=(1/18+2/81)\epsilon

where the equality used that
r_{\delta}^{\prime}=\ell/9
,
\delta=\kappa\ell/9
, and
\kappa\ell^{2}=\epsilon
.
As for the final term in (
G.16
), using
item

(a)
from
Lemma

G.4
on
y\in\mathscr{B}(y,r_{\delta}^{\prime})\cap B_{i}
,

(G.19)

\displaystyle\langle y,\hat{n}\rangle\geq\mathfrak{c}-4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{c}-(4/81)\epsilon.

Combine (
G.16
)–(
G.19
) to find that

(G.20)

\displaystyle\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle\geq\mathfrak{c}+(2-1/\sqrt{2}-1/2-1/18-2/81-4/81)\epsilon>\mathfrak{c}+\epsilon/2>\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}.

Hence, due to
item

(c)
from
Lemma

G.4
, we have that
z
is not on the negative side of
B_{i}
.
Equivalently, we have
z\not\in B_{i}
and
z
is on the positive side of
B_{i}
.
Considering that
z
was an arbitrary element of
\mathfrak{R}(h_{+})
, this proves
item

(2)
.
The same arguments can be used to establish
item

(3)
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

G.1
.

This follows from
Lemma

G.5
.
Indeed, note that vectors of the form
\vec{w}_{n}=(\cos(\epsilon{n}/\ell),\sin(\epsilon{n}/\ell))
with
0\leq{n}\leq\lfloor 2\pi\ell/\epsilon\rfloor
divide the unit circle in arcs of length
\leq\epsilon/\ell
.
In particular, for every unit vector
\vec{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, there exist some
{n}
with
\|\vec{W}-\vec{w}_{n}\|\leq\epsilon/2\ell
.
Further, since every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is at distance
\leq\epsilon/\sqrt{2}
from some point in the lattice
\epsilon\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, there always exists some
j,k
with
\|p_{j,k}-y\|\leq\epsilon/\sqrt{2}
.
∎

G.3.
Proof of
Lemma

G.2

Again, the proof amounts to an application of
Lemma

G.4
.
More specifically, we apply that lemma to a ball of radius somewhat greater than
\ell
:

Proof of
Lemma

G.2
.

Let
\delta\vcentcolon=2\kappa\ell
and recall the definition of
r_{\delta}^{\prime}
from
Section

G.1
.
Taking
c_{1}
sufficiently small in the assumption (
G.1
) then ensures that
r_{\delta}^{\prime}=2\ell
and
\delta<1/2
.
Further, recall that
\mathscr{E}\leq c_{3}\ell
and note that
\epsilon=\kappa\ell^{2}\leq c_{1}\ell
by (
G.1
).
Hence, taking
c_{1}
and
c_{3}
sufficiently small, it can additionally be ensured that

(G.21)

\displaystyle r_{\delta}^{\prime}>\ell/10+(2+1/2)\epsilon\quad\text{ and }\quad r_{\delta}^{\prime}>\ell+2\mathscr{E}+2\epsilon.

In particular, recalling the definition of
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)
from
Algorithm

2
, the first inequality implies that
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\subseteq\mathscr{B}(p_{j,k},r_{\delta}^{\prime})
for every
\lvert h\rvert\leq 2
.
For future use, also note that

(G.22)

\displaystyle\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}=4\kappa\ell^{2}=4\epsilon.

The assumption that
d_{\rm{H}}(\mathfrak{B},\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i})\leq\mathscr{E}
in
eq.

G.1
together with the assumption that
p_{j,k}\pm\ell\vec{w}_{n}
have distance
\leq\mathscr{E}+2\epsilon
to
\mathfrak{B}
then implies that there exist
y_{+},y_{-}\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
with

(G.23)

\displaystyle\bigl\|(p_{j,k}+\ell\vec{w}_{n})-y_{+}\bigr\|\leq 2\mathscr{E}+2\epsilon\quad\text{ and }\quad\bigl\|(p_{j,k}-\ell\vec{w}_{n})-y_{-}\bigr\|\leq 2\mathscr{E}+2\epsilon.

The triangle inequality then implies that the points
y_{+},y_{-}
are at distance
\leq\ell+2\mathscr{E}+2\epsilon<r_{\delta}^{\prime}
from
p_{j,k}
.
Thus, at least one barrier
B_{i}
intersects
\mathscr{B}(p_{j,k},r_{\delta}^{\prime})
and
Lemma

G.4
implies that this barrier is unique.

Let
\hat{n}
and
\mathfrak{c}
be as in
Lemma

G.4
applied to
\mathscr{B}(p_{j,k},r_{\delta}^{\prime})
.
Then, using (
G.23
) together with the fact that
item

(a)
yields
\lvert\langle y_{+},\hat{n}\rangle-\mathfrak{c}\rvert\leq 4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}
and
\lvert\langle y_{-},\hat{n}\rangle-\mathfrak{c}\rvert\leq 4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}
,

(G.24)

\displaystyle 2\ell\lvert\langle\vec{w}_{n},\hat{n}\rangle\rvert=\lvert\langle p_{j,k}+\ell\vec{w}_{n},\hat{n}\rangle-\langle p_{j,k}-\ell\vec{w}_{n},\hat{n}\rangle\rvert\leq 8\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}+4\mathscr{E}+4\epsilon.

The assumption that
p_{j,k}
is at distance
>\mathscr{E}/2
from all barriers implies that all points in a ball of radius
\mathscr{E}/2
lie on the same side of
B_{i}
.
In particular, if
p_{j,k}
lies on the positive side then so should
p_{j,k}-(\mathscr{E}/2)\hat{n}
, and hence
item

(c)
in
Lemma

G.4
yields
\langle p_{j,k},\hat{n}\rangle-\mathscr{E}/2>\mathfrak{c}-4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}
.
Similarly, if
p_{j,k}
lies on the negative side then
\langle p_{j,k},\hat{n}\rangle+\mathscr{E}/2<\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}

.
Taking
c_{2}
sufficiently large in the assumption that
\mathscr{E}\geq c_{2}\epsilon
and using (
G.22
), it may here be assumed that
\mathscr{E}/2-4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}>0
.
Hence, regardless of what side
p_{j,k}
lies on,

(G.25)

\displaystyle\lvert\langle p_{j,k},\hat{n}\rangle-\mathfrak{c}\rvert\geq\mathscr{E}/2-4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}.

Now note that every point
z\in\cup_{h=-2}^{2}\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)
can be represented as
z=p_{j,k}+a\vec{w}_{n}+b\vec{w}_{n}^{\perp}
for
a\in[-\ell/10,\ell/10]
and
b\in[-(2+1/2)\epsilon,(2+1/2)\epsilon]
.
Consequently, for every such
z
, using (
G.25
), the bound on
\lvert\langle\vec{w}_{n},\hat{n}\rangle\rvert
resulting from (
G.24
), and the fact that
\lvert\langle\vec{w}_{n}^{\perp},\hat{n}\rangle\rvert\leq 1
by
\vec{w}_{n}^{\perp}
being a unit vector,

(G.26)

\displaystyle\lvert\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle-\mathfrak{c}\rvert

\displaystyle\geq\lvert\langle p_{j,k},\hat{n}\rangle-\mathfrak{c}\rvert-(\ell/10)\lvert\langle\vec{w}_{n},\hat{n}\rangle\rvert-(2+1/2)\epsilon\lvert\langle\vec{w}_{n}^{\perp},\hat{n}\rangle\rvert

\displaystyle\geq(1/2-4/20)\mathscr{E}-(4+8/20)\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}-(4/20+2+1/2)\epsilon.

In particular, also using (
G.22
) to rewrite
\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}
in terms of
\epsilon
, there exist absolute constants
c,C>0
such that

(G.27)

\displaystyle\lvert\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle-\mathfrak{c}\rvert=4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}+(\lvert\langle z,\hat{n}\rangle-\mathfrak{c}\rvert-4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime})\geq 4\delta r_{\delta}^{\prime}+(c\mathscr{E}-C\epsilon).

Here, taking
c_{2}
sufficiently large, it can be ensured that
c\mathscr{E}-C\epsilon>0
.
Then,
item

(a)
from
Lemma

G.4
implies that
z\not\in B_{i}
.
The latter lemma is applicable since
\cup_{h=-2}^{2}\mathcal{R}(j,k,n,h)\subseteq\mathscr{B}(p_{j,k},r_{\delta}^{\prime})
by the discussion after (
G.21
).

Considering that
z
was an arbitrary element, this shows that
\cup_{h=-2}^{2}\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)
does not intersect the unique barrier
B_{i}
with
B_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}(p_{j,k},r_{\delta}^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
.
Equivalently, since
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\subseteq\mathscr{B}(p_{j,k},r_{\delta}^{\prime})
, we have that
\cup_{h=-2}^{2}\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)
does not intersect any barrier.
This concludes the proof.
∎

G.4.
Proof of
Lemma

G.3

This proceeds similarly to the proof of
Lemma

F.2
:

Proof.

Temporarily fix some
0\leq{n}\leq\lfloor 2\pi\ell/\epsilon\rfloor
and
\lvert h\rvert\leq 2
.
Taking
c_{1},c_{2}
sufficiently small in the assumption, we may assume that
\ell>\epsilon
.
Then,
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\subseteq D
necessitates that
\mathscr{B}(p_{j,k}+h\epsilon\vec{w}_{n}^{\perp},\epsilon/2)\subseteq D
.
Considering that the
p_{j,k}
associated with different
j,k
are at distance
\geq\epsilon
from each other, the balls
\mathscr{B}(p_{j,k}+h\epsilon\vec{w}_{n}^{\perp},\epsilon/2)
are all disjoint.
Hence, there can be at most
\operatorname{Area}(D)/(\pi\epsilon^{2}/4)
integers
j,k
with
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\subseteq D
.

There are additionally five possible choices for
h
, and at most
2\pi\ell/\epsilon+1
possible choices for
{n}
.
Recall that we may assume that
\ell>\epsilon
.
In particular, this yields that
2\pi\ell/\epsilon+1\leq 3\pi\ell/\epsilon
.
The number of rectangular regions with
\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\subseteq D
is hence at most
60\operatorname{Area}(D)\ell/\epsilon^{3}=60\operatorname{Area}(D)/(\kappa^{3}\ell^{5})
.

Note that
\operatorname{Area}(\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h))=\epsilon\ell=\kappa\ell^{3}
.
Further, taking
c_{2}
sufficiently small, we may assume that the diameter
\sup_{a,b\in\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)}\|a-b\|
is at most
\sqrt{t}
.
Hence, by
Lemma

4.10
and the union bound,

(G.28)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}(\hat{P}_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)},P_{\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)})\leq\beta\sqrt{t},\

\displaystyle\forall j,k,{n},h\text{ with }\mathcal{R}(j,k,{n},h)\subseteq D\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq 1-C\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\kappa^{3}\ell^{5}}\exp\Bigl(-c\frac{T\pi_{\min}\kappa\ell^{3}}{t_{\rm{mix}}}\Bigr)

for certain constants
C,c>0
depending only on
\alpha,\beta
and
\gamma
.
We will rely on the assumption (
2.8
) regarding
T
to estimate the right-hand side of (
G.28
).
First, however, we consider some preliminary estimates to rewrite the logarithmic factor in (
2.8
).

As in the discussion preceding (
C.4
), the assumption that the curvature of
B_{0}
is bounded by
\kappa
implies that
\operatorname{Area}(D)\geq\pi/\kappa^{2}
.
Consequently, also using that
\eta<1
,

(G.29)

\displaystyle\ln\Bigl(\pi\sqrt{\frac{1}{\kappa^{3}\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr)\leq\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr).

Hence, first using that
\ell=\gamma\sqrt{\varepsilon/\kappa}
and some direct calculations using the properties of the logarithm,

(G.30)

\displaystyle\ln\Bigl(\frac{C\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\frac{1}{\kappa^{3}\ell^{5}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr)+\frac{3}{2}\ln\Bigl(\pi\sqrt{\frac{1}{\kappa^{3}\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr)+\ln\Bigl(\pi^{-2/3}\frac{C}{\gamma^{5}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{2}\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr)+\ln\Bigl(\pi^{-2/3}\frac{C}{\gamma^{5}}\Bigr).

Taking
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
sufficiently small, it can be ensured that
\sqrt{\varepsilon^{3}/\kappa}\leq\pi/(2\kappa^{2})\leq\operatorname{Area}(D)/(2\eta)
so that the first term on the right-hand side of (
G.30
) is
\geq(5/2)\ln(2)\geq 1
.
Hence, picking some large constant
C^{\prime}>0
depending on
\gamma
to also cover the second term in (
G.30
),

(G.31)

\displaystyle\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\frac{1}{\kappa^{3}\ell^{5}}\Bigr)\leq C^{\prime}\ln\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{3}}}\Bigr).

Now, taking
c_{3}\geq C^{\prime}/c
sufficiently large in the assumption (
2.8
) and using (
G.31
) as well as the assumption that
\ell=\gamma\sqrt{\varepsilon/\kappa}
, it can be ensured that

(G.32)

\displaystyle T\geq\frac{1}{c}\frac{t_{\rm{mix}}}{\pi_{\min}}\frac{1}{\kappa\ell^{3}}\ln\Bigl(\frac{C\operatorname{Area}(D)}{\eta}\frac{1}{\kappa^{3}\ell^{5}}\Bigr).

Combine (
G.28
) and (
G.32
) to conclude the proof.
∎

Appendix H
Proof of
Lemma

5.3

Proof.

Consider the sequence of independent and identically distributed
\{0,1\}
-valued random variables
Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots
whose joint law with the
Y_{n}
is defined by

(H.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Z_{n}=1\mid Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n},Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n-1})\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }Y_{n}=0,\\
q/\mathbb{P}(Y_{n}=1\mid Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-1})&\text{ if }Y_{n}=1.\end{cases}

Then, we have
Z_{n}\leq Y_{n}
with probability one, so it suffices to prove (
5.4
) for
\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}
.
Note that
\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}=1)=q
.
Hence, by the union bound and Hoeffding’s inequality,

(H.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\leq n(q-\eta)\text{ for some }n\geq n_{0}\Bigr)\leq\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\leq n(q-\eta)\Bigr)\leq\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}\exp(-2\eta^{2}n).

Lemma

5.3
now follows by summing the series and using that
\exp(2\eta^{2})/(\exp(2\eta^{2})-1)\leq 2\eta^{-2}
for all
\eta\leq 1
.
∎

Appendix I
Proof of
Corollary

5.4

The following lemma makes it rigorous that transitions from
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
to
H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
are unlikely if and only if there is a barrier in the way.

Lemma I.1
.

There exist absolute constants
q,c_{1},\ldots,c_{4}>0
such that for every
\sqrt{t}\leq\ell\leq c_{1}(t\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\})^{1/4}
and unit vector
\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the following holds.

For every point
p\in D
satisfying
\|p-y\|\geq c_{2}\ell
for every
y\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
,

(I.1)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\mid X_{0}=x_{0}):x_{0}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}\geq q.

On the other hand, if
\|p-y\|\leq c_{3}\sqrt{t}
and
\min_{\pm\in\{+,-\}}\|\vec{v}\pm\vec{n}_{i}(y)\|\leq c_{4}\sqrt{t}/\ell
for some
y\in B_{i}
,

(I.2)

\displaystyle\sup\{\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\mid X_{0}=x_{0}):x_{0}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}\leq q/2.

Proof.

Let us start with the proof of (
I.1
).
Recall (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) and note that there exists an absolute constant
c>0
such that

\operatorname{Area}\bigl(\mathscr{B}(x_{0},4\sqrt{t})\cap H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)\geq ct

for every
x_{0}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
.
Consequently, since
x_{0}+W_{t}
has density of order
\geq 1/t
on
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},4\sqrt{t})
, there exists an absolute constant
c^{\prime}>0
such that

(I.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(x_{0}+W_{t}\in H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)\geq\mathbb{P}\bigl(x_{0}+W_{t}\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},4\sqrt{t})\cap H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)\geq c^{\prime}.

Recall the assumption that
p
is at distance
c_{2}\ell
from all barriers and note that
x_{0}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
implies that
\|x_{0}-p\|\leq\ell+2\sqrt{2}
.
It hence follows that
x_{0}
is at distance
\geq(c_{2}-1)\ell-2\sqrt{t}\geq(c_{2}-3)\sqrt{t}
from all barriers due to the triangle inequality.
Then, by (
3.28
) from
Corollary

3.12
, if
c_{1}
is sufficiently small and
c_{2}
is sufficiently large,

(I.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{t}=x_{0}+W_{t})\geq 1-c^{\prime}/2.

In other words, we have
X_{t}=x_{0}+W_{t}
with high probability.
The combination of (
I.3
) and (
I.4
) yields (
I.1
) with
q\vcentcolon=c^{\prime}/2
.

We next prove (
I.2
).
Let us consider the case where
\|\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu-\vec{n}_{i}(y)\|\leq c_{4}\sqrt{t}/\ell
; the case with
\|\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu+\vec{n}_{i}(y)\|\leq c_{4}\sqrt{t}/\ell
proceeds similarly.
Denote
\delta\vcentcolon=C\ell/\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}
for some constant
C\geq 3
which will soon be fixed.

The assumed inequalities on
\ell
and
t
imply, in particular, that
t\leq c_{1}^{4}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
.
Hence, by a second application of the assumed bounds on
\ell
, we have
\delta\leq c_{1}^{2}C
and
r_{\delta}=C\ell\geq C\sqrt{t}
.
Now, first fixing
C
at a sufficiently large value and subsequently taking
c_{1}
sufficiently small, it can be ensured using
Corollary

3.11
that for every
x_{0}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
,

(I.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(X_{t}\not\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\ \text{ or }\ s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{t})\neq s_{i}(0)\mid X_{0}=x_{0}\bigr)\leq q/2.

Recall (
5.1
) and note that the distance from points in
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
to
p
is at most
\ell+2\sqrt{t}\leq 3\ell
.
In particular, recalling that
r_{\delta}=C\ell\geq 3\ell
since
C\geq 3
, we may assume that
\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\subseteq\mathscr{B}(p,2r_{\delta})
for every
x_{0}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
.
Hence, for every
x_{0}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
,

(I.6)

\displaystyle x_{0}\in{}

\displaystyle{}\{z_{+}\in\mathscr{B}(p,2r_{\delta}):\langle z_{+}-p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\rangle\geq\sqrt{t}\},

(I.7)

\displaystyle H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\cap\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})\subseteq{}

\displaystyle{}\{z_{-}\in\mathscr{B}(p,2r_{\delta}):\langle z_{-}-p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\rangle\leq-\sqrt{t}\}.

Hence, also using (
I.5
), the desired result in (
I.2
) follows if we show that every
z_{+}
as in (
I.6
) is strictly on the positive side of
B_{i}
and that every
z_{-}
as in (
I.7
) is strictly on the negative side.
Let us prove this for
z_{+}
.
The proof for
z_{-}
proceeds similarly.

Recall that
\delta\leq c_{1}^{2}C
.
In particular, taking
c_{1}
sufficiently small, we may assume that
\delta<1/2
so that
Lemma

3.5
is applicable.
Then,
item

(3)
in that lemma implies that it suffices to show that
z_{+}
satisfies
\langle z_{+},\hat{n}\rangle\geq\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}
.
By the bilinearity of the inner product,

(I.8)

\displaystyle\langle z_{+},\hat{n}\rangle=\langle y,\hat{n}\rangle+\langle z_{+}-p,\hat{n}\rangle+\langle p-y,\hat{n}\rangle.

Recall that
\|\hat{n}-\vec{v}\|\leq c_{4}\sqrt{t}/\ell
and
r_{\delta}=C\ell
.
Consequently, using (
I.6
), the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and taking
c_{4}
sufficiently small,

(I.9)

\displaystyle\langle z_{+}-p,\hat{n}\rangle=\langle z_{+}-p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\rangle+\langle z_{+}-p,\hat{n}-\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\rangle\geq(1-2c_{4}C)\sqrt{t}\geq\sqrt{t}/2.

Further recall that
\|p-y\|\leq c_{3}\sqrt{t}
.
Hence, taking
c_{3}
sufficiently small and using that
\hat{n}
is a unit vector,

(I.10)

\displaystyle\langle p-y,\hat{n}\rangle\geq-c_{3}\sqrt{t}\geq-\sqrt{t}/4.

Now combining (
I.8
)–(
I.10
) and using that
\langle y,\hat{n}\rangle\geq\mathfrak{c}-4\delta r_{\delta}
by
item

(1)
in
Lemma

3.5
,

(I.11)

\displaystyle\langle z_{+},\hat{n}\rangle\geq\langle y,\hat{n}\rangle+\sqrt{t}/4\geq\mathfrak{c}-4\delta r_{\delta}+\sqrt{t}/4.

Here, note that
\delta r_{\delta}=C^{2}\ell^{2}/\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}\leq c_{1}^{2}C^{2}\sqrt{t}
by definition of
\delta
and the assumed bound on
\ell
.
Hence, taking
c_{1}
sufficiently small ensures that the right-hand side of (
I.11
) is
\geq\mathfrak{c}+4\delta r_{\delta}
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

5.4
.

We start with (
5.7
).
Denote the
n
th index with
X_{it}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
by

(I.12)

\displaystyle I_{n}\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{i\geq 0:\#\{0\leq j\leq i:X_{jt}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}\geq n\}.

Then, a random variable which indicates whether the
n
th visit to
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
was followed by a transition to
H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
may be defined by

Y_{n}\vcentcolon=\mathbbm{1}\{X_{(I_{n}+1)t}\in H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}.

Recall (
5.5
) and (
5.6
) and observe that
M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)=\sum_{i=1}^{N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)}Y_{i}
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)/N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)<c_{3}\text{ and }N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq n_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)}Y_{i}<c_{3}N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\text{ and }N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq n_{0}\Bigr)

(I.13)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}<c_{3}n\text{ for some }n\geq n_{0}\Bigr).

Here, applying the strong Markovianity of
Proposition

3.3
with the stopping time
I_{n}t
and using
Lemma

I.1
, there exists an absolute constant
q>0
such that

(I.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Y_{n}=1\mid Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-1})\geq\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\mid X_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime}):x_{0}^{\prime}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}\geq q

provided that
c_{1}
is taken sufficiently small and
c_{2}
sufficiently large.
Now, by
Lemma

5.3
with
\eta=q/4
, it holds with
c_{3}\vcentcolon=(3/4)q
that

(I.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)/N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)<c_{3}\text{ and }N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq n_{0}\bigr)\leq 32q^{-2}\exp(-q^{2}n_{0}/8).

This proves (
5.7
).

As for (
5.8
), let
I_{n}
be as in (
I.12
) and define

Z_{n}\vcentcolon=\mathbbm{1}\{X_{(I_{n}+1)t}\not\in H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}.

Then, similarly to (
I.13
), we have that

(I.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)/N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq{}

\displaystyle{}c_{3}\text{ and }N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq n_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl((N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)-M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu))/N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\leq 1-c_{3}\text{ and }N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq n_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)}Z_{i}\leq(1-c_{3})N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\text{ and }N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq n_{0}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\leq(1-c_{3})n\text{ for some }n\geq n_{0}\Bigr).

Further, similarly to (
I.14
), now using (
I.2
) from
Lemma

I.1
,

(I.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Z_{n}=1\mid Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n-1})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\sup\{\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in H_{-}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\mid X_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime}):x_{0}^{\prime}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}

\displaystyle\geq 1-q/2.

Recall that
c_{3}=(3/4)q
and fix some
\eta<q/4
.
Then, we have that
1-q/2-\eta>1-c_{3}
.
Hence, the combination of
Lemma

5.3
with (
I.16
) and (
I.17
) yields (
5.8
).
∎

Appendix J
Proof of
Lemma

5.5

We rely on the tools from
Section

3.2
with

(J.1)

\displaystyle\delta\vcentcolon=\ell/(2\min\{1/\kappa,1/\lambda_{\max},\rho\}).

Then, with
r_{\delta}
as in (
3.3
), it follows from (
5.9
) that

(J.2)

\displaystyle r_{\delta}=\ell/2,\qquad\qquad\delta\leq c_{2}/2,\qquad\qquad\delta r_{\delta}\leq(c_{1}^{2}/4)\sqrt{t}.

As a preliminary reduction, we replace
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
by another set which is more convenient for our purposes; see
Figure

10
for a visualization.

Lemma J.1
.

For every
\zeta<1/2
there exist
c_{1},\ldots,c_{4}>0
such that the following holds for every
t,\ell>0
,
x_{0}\in B_{i}
,
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and unit vector
\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying (
5.9
) for these constants.

Let
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
be as in (
5.1
) and
\hat{n}
as in
Lemma

3.5
.
Then, one has
\widehat{R}_{+}\subseteq R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
for

(J.3)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{+}\vcentcolon=\Bigl\{q\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta}):(1+\zeta)\sqrt{t}\leq\langle q-x_{0},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\leq(2-\zeta)\sqrt{t}\Bigr\}.

Figure 10
.

Visualization of the sets
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
and
\widehat{R}_{+}
considered in
Lemma

3.5
.

Proof.

Pick some
q\in\widehat{R}_{+}
and let us show that
q\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
.
By bilinearity,

(J.4)

\displaystyle\langle q-p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle q-x_{0},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle+\langle q-x_{0},\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu-s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle+\langle x_{0}-p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\rangle.

Regarding the first term, the assumption that
q\in\widehat{R}_{+}
yields that

(J.5)

\displaystyle(1+\zeta)\sqrt{t}\leq\langle q-x_{0},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\leq(2-\zeta)\sqrt{t}.

We next estimate the remaining two terms in (
J.4
).

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the fact that
q\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
,

(J.6)

\displaystyle\lvert\langle q-x_{0},\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu-s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\rvert\leq r_{\delta}(\|\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu-s_{i}(0)\vec{n}_{i}(x_{0})\|+\|s_{i}(0)\hat{n}-s_{i}(0)\vec{n}_{i}(x_{0})\|).

Taking
c_{2}
sufficiently small in (
J.2
), it can be ensured that
\delta<1/2
.
Then,
item

(2)
in
Lemma

3.5
is applicable and yields
\|s_{i}(0)\hat{n}-s_{i}(0)\vec{n}_{i}(x_{0})\|\leq 2\delta
.
Further, recall from (
5.9
) that
\|\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu-s_{i}(0)\vec{n}_{i}(x_{0})\|\leq c_{4}\sqrt{t}/\ell
.
Hence, using (
J.2
) and taking
c_{1},c_{4}
sufficiently small depending on
\zeta
, we may bound the right-hand side of (
J.6
) as

(J.7)

\displaystyle\lvert\langle q-x_{0},\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu-s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\rvert\leq r_{\delta}(c_{4}\sqrt{t}/\ell+2\delta)\leq(c_{4}/2+c_{1}^{2}/2)\sqrt{t}\leq(\zeta/2)\sqrt{t}.

Regarding the final term in (
J.4
), recall that
\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu
is a unit vector and that
\|x_{0}-p\|\leq c_{3}\sqrt{t}
by (
5.9
).
Hence, for
c_{3}
sufficiently small depending on
\zeta
,

(J.8)

\displaystyle\lvert\langle x_{0}-p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\rangle\rvert\leq\|x_{0}-p\|\leq c_{3}\sqrt{t}\leq(\zeta/2)\sqrt{t}.

Combine (
J.7
) and (
J.8
) to see that the total contribution of the final two terms in (
J.4
) is at most
\zeta\sqrt{t}
.
Hence, by (
J.5
), we have
\langle q-p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\rangle\in[\sqrt{t},2\sqrt{t}]
.

Recalling the definition of
R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
from (
5.1
), it remains to show that
\lvert\langle q-p,\vec{v}^{\perp}\rangle\rvert\leq\ell
.
Recall that
\vec{v}^{\perp}
is a unit vector and that
q\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta})
.
Hence, using (
5.9
) and (
J.2
) and choosing
c_{3}
sufficiently small,

(J.9)

\displaystyle\lvert\langle q-p,\vec{v}^{\perp}\rangle\rvert\leq\|q-x_{0}\|+\|x_{0}-p\|\leq r_{\delta}+c_{3}\sqrt{t}\leq(1/2+c_{3})\ell\leq\ell.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Let
Y_{t}^{+}
and
y_{0}^{+}
be as in
Section

3.2.2
.
Then, using the preliminary reduction of
Lemma

J.1
together with
Corollary

3.12
reduces us to studying the number of times that
Y_{t}^{+}
visits a horizontal strip:

Lemma J.2
.

For every
\xi<1/2
and
\eta\in(0,1)
there exist
c_{1},\ldots,c_{5}>0
such that the following holds for every
\ell,t,x_{0},p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu
which satisfy (
5.9
) with respect to
c_{1},\ldots,c_{4}
.
For every
1\leq J\leq c_{5}\ell^{2}/t
, every
n_{0}\geq 0
, and every
0\leq\gamma<1
,

(J.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\#\{0\leq{}

\displaystyle{}j\leq J:X_{(\gamma+j)t}\in R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}\geq n_{0}\mid X_{0}=x_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\bigl(\#\{0\leq j\leq J:\langle Y_{(\gamma+j)t}^{+}-y_{0}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\in[(1+\xi)\sqrt{t},(2-\xi)\sqrt{t}]\}\geq n_{0}\bigr)-\eta.

Proof.

Let
\zeta\vcentcolon=\xi/2
.
Then, taking
c_{1},\ldots,c_{4}
such that
Lemma

J.1
is applicable and defining
\widehat{R}_{+}
as in (
J.3
),

(J.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\#\{j\leq J:X_{(\gamma+j)t}\in{}

\displaystyle{}R_{+}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\}\geq n_{0}\bigr)\geq\mathbb{P}\bigl(\#\{j\leq J:X_{(\gamma+j)t}\in\widehat{R}_{+}\}\geq n_{0}\bigr).

Recall the stopping time
\tau
from (
3.7
).
Then, by
Corollary

3.11
, also using that
\gamma+J\leq 2J
by the assumption that
J\geq 1
and
\gamma<1
, there is an absolute constant
C>0
such that

(J.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{(\gamma+j)t}\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta}),\,\forall j\leq J)\geq 1-\mathbb{P}(\tau\leq(\gamma+J)t)\geq 1-\delta-C(Jt/r_{\delta}^{2}).

Recall that
r_{\delta}=\ell/2
and
J\leq c_{5}\ell^{2}/t
.
Further, we have
\delta\leq c_{2}/2
by (
J.2
).
Hence, taking
c_{2},c_{5}
to be sufficiently small,

(J.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{(\gamma+j)t}\in\mathscr{B}(x_{0},r_{\delta}),\,\forall j\leq J)\geq 1-\eta/2.

Similarly, using
Corollary

3.12
and taking
c_{2},c_{5}
to be sufficiently small, there exists an absolute constant
C^{\prime}>0
such that

(J.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|X_{(\gamma+j)t}-Y_{(\gamma+j)t}^{+}\|\leq C^{\prime}\delta r_{\delta},\,\forall j\leq J)\geq 1-\eta/2.

By (
J.2
), we can ensure that
C^{\prime}\delta r_{\delta}\leq\zeta\sqrt{t}/2
by taking
c_{1}
sufficiently small.
Further, recalling (
3.5
), we may assume that
\|x_{0}-y_{0}^{+}\|\leq 8\delta r_{\delta}\leq\zeta\sqrt{t}/2
.
Hence, by (
J.14
) and the triangle inequality,

(J.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\lvert\langle X_{(\gamma+j)t}-x_{0},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle-\langle Y_{(\gamma+j)t}^{+}-y_{0}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle\rvert\leq\zeta\sqrt{t},\ \forall j\leq J)\geq 1-\eta/2.

Considering that we defined
\zeta=\xi/2
and recalling the definition of
\widehat{R}_{+}
from (
J.3
), combining (
J.13
) and (
J.15
) in (
J.11
) now yields the desired result (
J.10
).
∎

Proof of
Lemma

5.5
.

The assumption that
x_{0}\in B_{i}
implies that
y_{0}^{+}
lies on the reflection barrier
A^{+}
for
Y^{+}
; recall (
3.4
) and (
3.5
).
Consequently, the process
\langle Y_{r}^{+}-y_{0}^{+},s_{i}(0)\hat{n}\rangle
is a one-dimensional reflected Brownian motion starting from zero, meaning that it has the same distribution as
\lvert\mathcal{B}_{r}\rvert
for
\mathcal{B}_{r}
a one-dimensional Brownian motion.
Hence, (
5.10
) follows from
Lemma

J.2
and the scaling property.
∎

Appendix K
Proof of
Proposition

5.1

Recall that an outline was given
Section

5.3
.
Details for the proofs of the performance guarantee in
Theorem

2.5
are given in
Section

K.1
.
Subsequently, we prove
Corollary

2.6
in
Section

K.2
.

K.1.
Proof of
Theorem

2.5

Following the pattern from
Appendices

F
and
G
, we start by stating some preparatory lemmas.

By combining
Corollary

5.4
with a union bound, the following result shows that the expected behavior of the number of transitions holds for all
p_{j,k}
and
\vec{v}_{n}
in
Algorithm

3
:

Lemma K.1
.

There exist
c_{1},\ldots,c_{6}>0
such that the following holds for every
\sqrt{t}\leq\ell\leq c_{1}(t\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\})^{1/4}
,
\epsilon\leq\sqrt{t}/2
,
\mathfrak{n}_{0}\geq 1
, and initial condition
x_{0}\in D
.

For every
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and unit vector
\vec{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, define a
\{0,1\}
-valued random variable
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{good}}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
as follows.
If
\|p-y\|\geq c_{2}\ell
for every
y\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
, then with
M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
and
N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)
as in
Corollary

5.4
we define

(K.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{good}}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\vcentcolon=\mathbbm{1}\bigr\{M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)/N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\geq c_{3}\text{ or }N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)<\mathfrak{n}_{0}\bigl\}.

On the other hand, if
\|p-y\|\leq c_{4}\sqrt{t}
and
\min_{\pm\in\{+,-\}}\|\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu\pm\vec{n}_{i}(y)\|\leq c_{5}\sqrt{t}/\ell
for some
y\in B_{i}
,

(K.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{good}}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\vcentcolon=\mathbbm{1}\bigr\{M(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)/N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)<c_{3}\text{ or }N(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)<\mathfrak{n}_{0}\bigl\}.

And finally, define
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{good}}(p,\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu)\vcentcolon=1
if
p
and
\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu
do not satisfy either of the preceding conditions.

Consider some arbitrary
\eta\in(0,1)
and assume that

\mathfrak{n}_{0}\geq c_{6}\ln(\ell T/\eta\epsilon t)
.
Then, it holds with
p_{j,k}
and
\vec{v}\mkern 2.0mu_{n}
as in
Algorithm

3
that

(K.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{good}}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})=1\text{ for all }j,k\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }0\leq n\leq\lfloor 2\pi\ell/\epsilon\rfloor\bigr)\geq 1-\eta.

It could be difficult to recover hits of the barrier which only occurred at the very end of the observation period as we may not get to observe much associated data.

Lemma

K.2
shows that this difficulty is irrelevant as it does not occur with high probability:

Lemma K.2
.

For every
\eta\in(0,1)
there exists
c>0
such that the following holds for every
T>0
and initial condition
x_{0}\in D
.
Consider some
\Delta\leq c\min\{T,1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
.
Then,

(K.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{s}\not\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i},\ \forall s\in[T-\Delta,T])\geq 1-\eta.

The proofs of
Lemmas

K.1
and
K.2
are given in
Sections

K.1.1
and
K.1.2
, respectively.
It now remains to combine the preceding ingredients with those in
Section

5
.
This amounts to a straightforward but notationally cumbersome computation:

Proof of
Theorem

2.5
.

To avoid ambiguity with other parts, let us denote
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}
for the constants in
Theorem

2.5
, and denote
\mathcal{C}_{4},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{7}
for the constants in
Proposition

5.1
.

The assumption preceding (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) is that

(K.5)

\displaystyle t\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}T,\ \text{ and }\ \ln(T/t)^{4}t\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\min\{T,1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}.

Further,
Proposition

5.1
posits that the parameters in
Algorithm

3
satisfy

(K.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}=\mathcal{C}_{4},\ \ \epsilon=\mathcal{C}_{5}\sqrt{t},\ \ \ell=\mathcal{C}_{6}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t},\ \text{ and }\ \mathfrak{n}_{0}=\mathcal{C}_{7}\ln(T/t).

It now has to be shown that these constants can be chosen such that the output of
Algorithm

3
satisfies the performance guarantees in (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) with respect to
\mathcal{C}_{3}
.

Let us start by determining appropriate values for
\mathcal{C}_{4},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{7}
.
Let
c_{1},\ldots,c_{6}
be as in
Lemma

K.1
, let
c_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,c_{5}^{\prime}
be the constants arising in
Lemma

5.5
with some fixed
\zeta<1/2
and with
\eta
replaced by
\eta/4
, and let
\tilde{c}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{c}_{3}
be the constants arising in
Lemma

5.6
with the same
\zeta
.
Then, appropriate values for
\mathfrak{s}
and
\epsilon
may be found by taking

(K.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{4}\vcentcolon=c_{3}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{C}_{5}\vcentcolon=\min\{c_{4},c_{5},c_{3}^{\prime},c_{4}^{\prime},1/2\}.

The choice of
\mathcal{C}_{6}
and
\mathcal{C}_{7}
is more tedious.
More specifically, having an application of
Lemmas

5.5
,
5.6
and
K.1
in mind, the following inequalities will be required:

(K.8)

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{c}_{3}\eta}{4}\sqrt{\Bigl\lfloor\frac{c_{5}^{\prime}\ell^{2}}{t}\Bigr\rfloor}\geq\mathfrak{n}_{0}\geq c_{6}\ln\Bigl(\frac{4}{\eta}\frac{T}{t}\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\Bigr).

Equivalently, by (
K.6
)
\mathcal{C}_{6}
and
\mathcal{C}_{7}
will have to satisfy the following constraints:

(K.9)

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{c}_{3}\eta}{4}\sqrt{\bigl\lfloor c_{5}^{\prime}\mathcal{C}_{6}^{2}\ln(T/t)^{2}\bigr\rfloor}\geq\mathcal{C}_{7}\ln(T/t)\geq c_{6}\ln\Bigl(\frac{4}{\eta}\frac{T}{t}\frac{\mathcal{C}_{6}}{\mathcal{C}_{5}}\ln(T/t)\Bigr).

An inconvenience is that
\mathcal{C}_{6}
occurs on both the left and right of (
K.9
).
To deal with this, one can exploit that the dependence of the left on
\mathcal{C}_{6}
is essentially linear while the dependence on the right is logarithmic, suggesting that the inequalities can be satisfied if
\mathcal{C}_{6}
is taken large enough.
More precisely, taking
\mathcal{C}_{1}
sufficiently small in (
K.5
), we may assume that
4\ln(T/t)/(\eta\mathcal{C}_{5})\leq T/t
.
Then,

(K.10)

\displaystyle c_{6}\ln\Bigl(\frac{4}{\eta}\frac{T}{t}\frac{\mathcal{C}_{6}}{\mathcal{C}_{5}}\ln(T/t)\Bigr)\leq c_{6}\ln(\mathcal{C}_{6})+2c_{6}\ln(T/t).

Further, taking
\mathcal{C}_{6}\geq 1
and taking
\mathcal{C}_{1}
sufficiently small, we may assume that
\lfloor c_{5}^{\prime}\mathcal{C}_{6}^{2}\ln(T/t)^{2}\rfloor\geq c_{5}^{\prime}\mathcal{C}_{6}^{2}\ln(T/t)^{2}/2
.
Then, with
\mathscr{C}\vcentcolon=\tilde{c}_{3}\eta\sqrt{c_{5}^{\prime}}/(4\sqrt{2})
,

(K.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{c}_{3}\eta}{4}\sqrt{\bigl\lfloor c_{5}^{\prime}\mathcal{C}_{6}^{2}\ln(T/t)^{2}\bigr\rfloor}\geq\mathscr{C}\mathcal{C}_{6}\ln(T/t).

Further, taking
\mathcal{C}_{1}
sufficiently small and taking
\mathcal{C}_{6}
sufficiently large, it can be ensured that

(K.12)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}\mathcal{C}_{6}\ln(T/t)\geq c_{6}\ln(\mathcal{C}_{6})+2c_{6}\ln(T/t).

Now, fix
\mathcal{C}_{6}
and take
\mathcal{C}_{7}
such that
\mathcal{C}_{7}\ln(T/t)=\mathscr{C}\mathcal{C}_{6}\ln(T/t)
.
Then, combining (
K.10
)–(
K.12
) yields (
K.9
).
Equivalently, (
K.8
) holds.

From here on,
\mathcal{C}_{4},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{7}
will remain fixed, and the goal is to show that (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) are satisfied if
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are taken sufficiently small, and
\mathcal{C}_{3}
sufficiently large.

We start with the nonoccurrence of false positives from (
2.11
).
A direct calculation using the previously fixed values of
\mathcal{C}_{5}
,
\mathcal{C}_{6}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{7}
shows that the assumptions in the first paragraph of
Lemma

K.1
are satisfied if
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are taken sufficiently small.
9
9

9

For instance, the assumption
\ell\leq c_{1}(t\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\})^{1/4}
can be equivalently be phrased as
\mathcal{C}_{6}^{4}\ln(T/t)^{4}t\leq c_{1}^{4}\min\{1/\kappa^{2},1/\lambda_{\max}^{2},\rho^{2}\}
and hence holds if
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is taken sufficiently small in (
K.5
).

Further, recall from (
K.8
) that
\mathfrak{n}_{0}\geq c_{6}\ln(4T\ell/\eta\epsilon t)
.
Hence, by
Lemma

K.1
,

(K.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{good}}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})=1\text{ for all }j,k\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }0\leq n\leq\lfloor 2\pi\ell/\epsilon\rfloor\bigr)\geq 1-\eta/4.

In particular, the probability in (
K.13
) is
\geq 1-\eta
.
Recalling that
\mathfrak{s}=\mathcal{C}_{4}=c_{3}
and recalling (
K.1
), it holds on the event in the left-hand side of (
K.13
) that for every
j,k,n
such that
\|p_{j,k}-y\|\geq c_{2}\ell
for all
y\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
,

(K.14)

\displaystyle M(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})/N(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})\geq\mathfrak{s}\text{ or }N(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})<\mathfrak{n}_{0}.

Recall the definition of
M(p_{j,k},\vec{v})
and
N(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})
from
Corollary

5.4
.
It follows that
Algorithm

3
will not add any point
p_{j,k}
which has distance
\geq c_{2}\ell
from the barriers to
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
.
Equivalently, every point in
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
has distance
<c_{2}\ell
from some barrier.
This proves (
2.11
) if we take
\mathcal{C}_{3}
sufficiently large to satisfy
\mathcal{C}_{3}\geq c_{2}\mathcal{C}_{6}
.

Let us proceed to the proof of (
2.12
) regarding the recovery of typical points.
Consider a stopping time
\tau
with
X_{\tau}\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}B_{i}
almost surely.
We start with some preliminary reductions.
First, write
\mathscr{E}_{\text{good}}
for the event in (
K.13
) where
\mathcal{E}_{\text{good}}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})=1
for every
j,k,n
, and recall that
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}_{\text{good}})\geq 1-\eta/4
.
Further, write

\Delta\vcentcolon=(\lfloor c_{5}^{\prime}\ell^{2}/t\rfloor+2)t

and note that
Lemma

K.2
ensures that
\tau\not\in[T-\Delta,T]
with probability
\geq 1-\eta/4
if
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are taken sufficiently small.
Hence,

(K.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{}

\displaystyle{}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\|X_{\tau}-p\|:p\in\hat{\mathcal{X}}\}>\mathcal{C}_{3}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t}\text{ and }\tau\leq T\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\|X_{\tau}-p\|:p\in\hat{\mathcal{X}}\}>\mathcal{C}_{3}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t},\ \tau\leq T-\Delta,\ \mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{\text{good}}}\bigr)+\eta/2.

Here, by the law of total probability,

(K.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\|X_{\tau}-p\|:p\in\hat{\mathcal{X}}\}>\mathcal{C}_{3}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t},\ \tau\leq T-\Delta,\ \mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{\text{good}}}\bigr)\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m\\
y\in B_{i}\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{S}_{i}=-1,+1\\
\mathcal{T}\in[0,T-\Delta]\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\|y-p\|:p\in\hat{\mathcal{X}}\}>\mathcal{C}_{3}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t},\,\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{\text{good}}}\mid X_{\tau}=y,s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau})=\mathcal{S}_{i},\tau=\mathcal{T}\bigr).

From here on, let us fix some
y\in B_{i}
,
\mathcal{S}_{i}\in\{-1,+1\}
, and
\mathcal{T}\in[0,T-\Delta]
and work on the event described in the conditioning of (
K.16
).

The choice of
\mathcal{C}_{5}
in (
K.7
) ensures that
\epsilon
is sufficiently small to guarantee that there exist
j,k,n
with
\|y-p_{j,k}\|\leq\mathcal{C}_{5}\sqrt{t}\leq\min\{c_{4},c_{3}^{\prime}\}\sqrt{t}
and
\|\vec{v}_{n}-\mathcal{S}_{i}\vec{n}_{i}(y)\|\leq\min\{c_{5},c_{4}^{\prime}\}\sqrt{t}/\ell
.
Then, recalling (
K.2
),
Algorithm

3
will add
p_{j,k}
to
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
if
\mathcal{E}_{\text{good}}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})=1
and
N(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})\geq\mathfrak{n}_{0}
.
Hence, since
\mathscr{E}_{\text{good}}
implies that
\mathcal{E}_{\text{good}}(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})=1
,

(K.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}\{\|y-p\|:p\in\hat{\mathcal{X}}\}>\mathcal{C}_{3}{}

\displaystyle{}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t},\,\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{\text{good}}}\mid X_{\tau}=y,s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau})=\mathcal{S}_{i},\tau=\mathcal{T}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(N(p_{j,k},\vec{v}_{n})<\mathfrak{n}_{0}\mid X_{\tau}=y,s_{i}(L^{\mkern-2.0mu{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\tau})=\mathcal{S}_{i},\tau=\mathcal{T})

provided that
\mathcal{C}_{3}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t}\geq\|y-p_{j,k}\|
which can be ensured by taking
\mathcal{C}_{3}
sufficiently large so that
\mathcal{C}_{3}\ln(T/t)\sqrt{t}\geq\mathcal{C}_{5}\sqrt{t}
.

Let
\gamma\in[0,1)
be the least value such that
\mathcal{T}/t+\gamma
